housecoat and slippers. Never do to admit to those two where I'll find it-or how. I'll just find it. There are some things that a woman might as well keep to herself. 

Hope I'll remember my own failings the next time I start to sound off about, "Why don't you put things back when you get through with them!" Reminds me of the man who joined a church because he heard them singing, "We have left undone those things which we ought to have done," and he figured that's where he belonged.

Just as I reached the bottom step with what I trust was an air of sweet innocence-and the oil can-the front door bell rang. There stood Laurie Pratt, and I never was more surprised in my life. I have kept sort of in touch with her since the summer, five years ago, when she rented the Carson house. But the keeping in touch was more her doing than mine for the simple reason that she never seemed to stay put long enough for the mail to catch up with her. A card from a hotel in Italy; then one from somewhere in France; a note from Bermuda. I guess those penned-up years of her childhood and the long strain of companionship with her father, before he died, must have made her want to get away and stay away until the restlessness wore off.

I might have known she would have changed, but, as a matter of fact, she looked ten years younger and smart as a design from Vogue. Jim and I always used to try and figure what kind of a person she might have been if she had been born the oldest of a poor family instead of the little sister of a wealthy one. I remember the amazement we had when we had supper with her one night. She looked, and acted, like such a tea-and-toast

sort of person and she can cook like an angel. She told us that night of how she had gone to cooking class the first winter after her father died. "It was the first time

I ever had a chance to meet real people-the first time I had ever been really on my own." Then she turned to me and added, "Let Peg grow up like other girls-don't try to make her something special and shut off from life." Well, Laurie has come a long way from that night, but I wish her eyes looked happier. Only a houseful of children and a tweedy man to fuss over could do that for her I guess. You can't buy those at a hairdresser's or in the Little Grey Salon-exclusive models for the woman of taste and discrimination. 

Laurie couldn't possibly stay for breakfast. She had things to see to-a real estate man to consult. She wouldn't take off her hat because she really wasn't staying. But, after a cup of Jim's really perfect coffee and two of the waffles I mixed up in a hurry, she thought some of the maple cream might taste good on just another quarter.

There's magic in food when it's cooked with affection and shared in friendliness. It unlocks human hearts and brings faith and courage. Laurie seemed so much easier and more natural in her manner than when she first arrived. It takes time for her to relax. "I wanted to tell you and Jim first of all-I'm moving back to Oakdale next week-I hope you're as glad as I am. This was the place I felt most at home, and you were the friends I remembered best. I'm going to have a house and a garden and a real kitchen-I'm so sick of things served under silver covers-they all look different and they all taste the same." 10

"Going to invite us over to dinner?" asked Jim. "I'm still holding your Dutch apple cake up to Doody here as the noblest example of what can be done with a stove." "Going to make some more of those doughnuts with the quince jelly in their middles?" asked Artie. "Boy, were those something!" 

She couldn't have looked more pleased. My menfolks are all right. Laurie seems to think so too, because she leaned across the table and took Art's hand in one of hers, Jim's in the other. "You're all invited-to dinner, to doughnuts, to anything you like-any time. It's wonderful to know you remember."

Five minutes later she was gone, and I sat there thinking of what it must be like to try to make a home alone with no one who needs you or looks to you for comfort and companionship. I've had a family for so long that 

I'd feel like a disembodied spirit without them. I wish 

I could help Laurie, but all I can give her is a share in our everyday life and happiness-if I have a chance. I'm glad she's coming back to Oakdale.

Jim and Artie insisted that they were both so stuffed to the ears that I needn't bother fixing another meal until tomorrow morning's breakfast. Which, I have learned from long experience, meant about five o'clock for dinner-at which point they would be starved and start saying, “Anything good in the ice box?" Besides, I like fixing Sunday dinner with the extras, and I'd sort of planned to make the southern spoon bread with hominy grits and creamed gravy with the chicken and little flecks of brown through the gravy. Peach meringue with frozen peaches and gobs of whipped cream.

II

That is one noble combination even to me who cares nothing whatever about desserts and has to watch my girlish figure-what there is left of it. Anyway, I'll make a nice plump old lady and they're always good natured and loved. No, "Yon Cassius hath a lean and hungry look" for me.

Well, here I was with almost all of a nice quiet Sunday afternoon before me and the seventh of December reminding me of "Only fifteen more shopping days until Christmas"-which information I had brought to my attention on practically every advertising page in the morning paper. Not being mathematical I could never have figured it out even with Jim's slide rule which he has trained like a trick poodle. The perfect time to make the Christmas jam! Each year I think I'll do something different just to prove to the neighbors that I have more than one thought in my head. But each year they all say, "Going to make your Christmas jam soon?" and seem sort of upset at the idea of a change from the pattern. 

Maybe they're right. It should be a season of nice old customs, and I'd miss Lottie Gibson's candy and Beth's hickory nut cookies that take their usual place on the side table in the dining room where everyone can nibble and pick and choose and forget the calories for once. 

I was dashing around the kitchen like a banshee on the loose. Dried apricots-how the price has gone up and the size of the package down since the years and years ago when I first started the Christmas jam making.

Poured over them the juice from the canned pineapple just before I went up to shower and get on a house dress-with Jim, who was stuffed to the ears, taking a 12

chunk of pineapple whenever he thought I wasn't looking. "My favorite fresh green vegetable," he remarked. "What are you making so much jam for?" He always asks that and then complains that there isn't half enough for the family by the time I start passing it around among the neighbors. He and Artie would prefer an individual soup bowl of it-and never mind the toast. 

When I poured the juice from the maraschino cherries into the pot-why, there was Artie ready to assist. Up to that moment he had been, or so I thought, up on the third floor taking his radio apart for the fifty-ninth time. Jim had called him to help with something in the basement. I had called him to ask about the car keys. "Never heard a thing-had my door shut and the blamed radio was full of static. You and Dad just don't realize how hard it is to hear when you're on the top deck like that." "Funny how you hear when I open a bottle of cherries," I remarked and ran my hand through his hair that always stands on end anyway-as though it were surprised and eager and excited. "Merely a coincidence!" said Artie grandly. "Just happened to be coming down for a tool I needed. Mind if I take a cherry? Who you making all that jam forus or the neighbors?" He wandered over to the table by the side of the sink where I keep the stack of old newspapers and began to read choice bits out loud. He and Jim love the funnies and always laugh twice as hard at one that is three weeks old than at the latest edition. 

I went on cutting up the apricots and pineapple because they are ready to cook sooner if I cut them before 13

That is one noble combination even to me who cares nothing whatever about desserts and has to watch my girlish figure-what there is left of it. Anyway, I'll make a nice plump old lady and they're always good natured and loved. No, "Yon Cassius hath a lean and hungry look" for me.

Well, here I was with almost all of a nice quiet Sunday afternoon before me and the seventh of December reminding me of "Only fifteen more shopping days until Christmas"--which information I had brought to my attention on practically every advertising page in the morning paper. But each year they all say, "Going to make your Christmas jam soon?" and seem sort of upset at the idea of a change from the pattern.

He always asks that and then complains that there isn't half enough for the family by the time I start passing it around among the neighbors. He and Artie would prefer an individual soup bowl of it-and never mind the toast.

You and Dad just don't realize how hard it is to hear when you're on the top deck like that." He wandered over to the table by the side of the sink where I keep the stack of old newspapers and began to read choice bits out loud. He and Jim love the funnies and always laugh twice as hard at one that is three weeks old than at the latest edition. 

I went on cutting up the apricots and pineapple because they are ready to cook sooner if I cut them before 13

soaking-took me three years to learn that simple fact of life. Jim was still nibbling at pineapple and slipping

Artie a cherry every so often, so I had to work fast if I was going to produce anything but stewed apricots. 

Some women seem to have a faculty for keeping their menfolks out of the kitchen and getting their work done in the most approved and capable manner. Truth of the matter is, I guess, that I don't really want to keep mine out-I like them underfoot and bothering me.

I took out the glasses that I had bought weeks ago at the five and ten-I always like to start Christmas right after Thanksgiving because it makes it last longer. It's fun to think up new wrappings for the jam so that it looks different and gay. This year I'm going Mexicanbright stripes on the glasses, amber and red of the jam, all the gaudy red and blue and yellow and green of the paper napkins-and tied with ombre shaded wool. God bless Mr. Woolworth! "Say, Art," said Jim. "Suppose you could operate on our radio one of these days when you have the time? 

It has a rattle in its middle." "Sure," said Artie. "Want to turn it on now and see how she sounds? Probably just a loose connection somewhere."

Jim took my hand and put his other arm around Artie's shoulder. We walked together into the living room. It came over me again, as it does so many times, how lovely that room is with all its shabbiness. It has lived with us as we have lived with it and all of us have been through so many things together. The books Jim and I have read to each other, the books the youngsters have enjoyed and outgrown and moved on to books 14

with wider horizons. I always remember the weather strip man who came to give us one of the unreliable things called an "estimate" two years ago. I don't know why they go through the motions, because the estimate and the final bill never seem to have any connection whatever. Anyway, he looked over all the windows and the doors and then stood in front of the long line of open bookshelves on the sides of the fireplace. He just stood. Finally he blurted out, "Your husband a lawyer?" "No," I said. "He's an engineer. Why?" "Nothing-just wondered."

The next week he was back to do the job and brought along one Bert-"my assistant." Never yet saw a man come to do a job-any kind of a job-on an ordinary house without lugging in reinforcements and moral support in the shape of "an assistant." Anyway, this one wasn't named Bill, which was a novelty. The boss introduced Bert to each window in turn as though it presented a real personality and problem. He showed him the three doors and explained the peculiarities of each. They all look alike to me, but, as Artie says, "Mom, you're just not mechanical." The boss took Bert by the arm and guided him over to the wall with the fireplace. "See all them books. And her husband ain't a lawyer —he's an engineer. I don't get it-do you?” "Nope," said Bert, "but I got a sister would sure love to get in here--I bet she sure would love to get in here. 

Regular bookworm she is and she reads deep stuff too -Ella Wheeler Wilcox and all the high brow poems she can lay her hands on."

I laughed again with my own private brand of inner 15

chuckle just remembering Bert and the boss. Jim and

Artie were fiddling with the radio-always fascinates me to hear what a funny hash you get when you turn from station to station with nothing particular on your mind. "Small stations seem clear," suggested Artie. "Try one of the main ones and let's see if we get the rattle-might be synchronizing with the motor on the oil burner and be on just a certain wave length." I couldn't follow this technical stuff but I am bright in my own way. 

I looked at my watch-just three-five. "Try one of the main stations. They always come through strong so you'd notice any little interference." 

Jim dialed and twiddled. A voice was coming all right, but sort of an electricity and tension seemed to be in it. 

Made me feel just the way I do before a storm really breaks-thank goodness, now that the youngsters have grown up I don't have to pretend I love storms and say idiotically "See the pretty lightning! Hear the great big boom in the sky!" Who says children can see right through you and can't be deceived. Bet it was a bachelor who wrote books on child training. 

I leaned forward to listen. Jim and Artie sat like two people who had been put under a strange spell of immobility. The voice went on- "The startling news of the afternoon will come as a shock to the American public. Pearl Harbor was attacked at 1:05 our time in a surprise assault by Japanese bombers in great force and without warning. The extent of the damage done has not been announced but fear is expressed that the casualties and loss in planes and vessels will be great. The attack was made at almost the same time that the Japanese Peace Envoys were in 16

conference with Secretary Hull in Washington. In the face of such planned treachery it is difficult to see any other course open to the American people except a declaration of war by Congress. The President is expected to call the Congress into special session tomorrow."

There we sat, the three of us-one little island of an American family in the whole sea of American families who were listening in and questioning, as we were questioning, what uncharted voyages lay before us. Our lives would all be different from now on until victory, by suffering and losses and personal courage and a tremendous force of arms and equipment, finally tipped the balance. We must all help in every small way, in every large way; set habits must go, usual things be changed to meet the storm. I thought about Jim and Artie; I thought about Peggy and her Navy Lieutenant and what it would mean to him and, through him, to her. 

I thought mostly about myself and prayed that I'd measure up. I thought of our friends and their sons and daughters who had grown up around us in these busy, sometimes troubling, often happy, years. Jim came over and sat down beside me. He and Artie are so much alike -when they feel deepest it is hardest to put feelings into words. He started to stroke my hand as he has done with the comfort and assurance it has always brought me when we've faced things together. "Well, this is it," he finally said. "Here we go. It's better than fighting fog and knowing you're going to reach the edge of the falling off place sooner or later.

Now we've got a real job-something to get our backbones stiffened up about." 17

Artie had said nothing. He sat there staring into space as he so often does when he's living in the fascinating world of his mind. He got up slowly and walked over to us. "Will you and Dad sign a permission slip for me?" "A what?" I asked stupidly. "I'm only eighteen and you have to get parent's permission or they won't take you." I still couldn't understand-things had been moving too fast for my mind to catch up with my emotions. Artie seemed so much older, so serious, so filled with a sort of remote strangeness that I felt like an outsider. "Air Corps?" asked Jim briefly-and then I knew.

This was man talk-between two men. "If they'll have me," said Artie. 

December 8.-Monday is a day that didn't get its name Blue Monday by accident-it has earned it through years of being the worst day of the week. Nobody likes it-everybody puts up with it. But this Monday of mine was more plaid than blue-a broken plaid at that with no possible set pattern. First of all Jim woke at six-thirty and decided it would be nice to take the seven-twentyeight instead of the eight-six. His bright thoughts always come in the dewy dawn when my mind is still in a complete and blanketing fog. The early breakfast did give me a chance to get things ready for Claudine to amble in and tackle the washing around eight-thirty. But at eight-forty-five the phone rang. George's brown velvet voice said, as usual, "Good mornin', Miss Lady-this is me.” “Yes,” I said hopefully-fearing the worst and being right. "Claudine she regrets to inform you she is indisposed and to tell you to please jes' let the washin' 18

sit. She'll come to your house in a day or so." As 

George's fortunes go up Claudine's health seems to go down. But, as George explained when he borrowed the honeymoon money from Jim five years ago, "She ain't so strong, my little girl-she kind of needs looking after." I'd about as soon shelter a Mack truck, but that's her story and she's stuck to it. Anyway, George is blissfully happy. In fact, he is convinced that she is the most beautiful and frail of flowers. Before he was off the phone he just wanted to ask of me one little flavor. 

Would I be happenin' to be goin' shopping? And would

I select for him a pink boudoir cap for Claudine's Christmas gif'? And would I get her a nice one that would look pretty-with plenty of lace and ribbons. And pay for it anything what's right-Claudine she don't like cheap stuff and money is comin' in free since I got me my De-fense job. Jim always said that anyone who could make our old lawn mower cut grass was a mechanical genius and George has proved it. At least he's on his way with a regular pay check as watchman in a defense plant. I'm honestly awfully glad his "now you eat and now you don't" existence is over-at least for the duration. But I do wish Claudine's health would hold up under prosperity. Dug into the washing myself -it couldn't just "sit." Found myself quite happy when the suds in the machine started piling up and decided

I might as well do the whole thing instead of the dibs and dabs I'd planned.

The phone rang. Ruth Woods on the wire! She was so sweet and inquired about the state of our family and what was I doing and how is dear little Peggy-how Peg 19

would love that-I knew something was coming. I was right. "I'm simply frantic, my dear-this war!" She spoke as though I'd never heard of it. "Three committee meetings today and I'm organizing a new Civilian Defense 

Unit-one must help in every possible way." One must and my share, it seems, was to take Chunky-just for today. The latest Woods queen of the kitchen hadn't arrived and Ruth simply had to go to these meetings and she couldn't leave Chunky by himself. I thought of four dozen reasons why I should say "No" and, of course, I said "Yes." Here I go for the duration with

Chunky more or less a permanent boarder. But I like 

Chunky and I do not like his mother, so perhaps my kind act will make up for my evil thoughts. She's one of those awful women who knows the answer to everything-if they'd make her a Brigadier General the war would be over in no time I'm sure-and she'd agree with me. What a sweet nature I have not!

Back to the washing with half my mind on what I could fix for Chunky's lunch and the other half on how 

I was going to explain to Jim why I said "Yes." Jim has a heart as big as a Jones' Beach parking field but he still lets his brain function. 

The front door banged with the crash that only

Chunky could produce. He always opens a door as though it were determined to resist him and he were equally determined to overcome it. For a five-year-old he has plenty of what it takes, as Jim says. "Down here, Chunky," I called. "I brought my dog-and he's new and kind of old and mother said I better bring him and that you 20

wouldn't mind because you like dogs." Heavenly grapefruit! What next!

The new dog that is kind of old turns out to be a thoroughbred Springer Spaniel that reminds me of a combination of an English butler and Saint Cecilia. He has the dignity and aloofness of the butler and the faraway other world expression of Saint Cecilia-seated one day at the organ. "He talks to his fleas," commented Chunky, "and he's very smart. Wag your tail, Hoskins-we call him Hoskins because he's of English and he has a white vest. 

What happened to your red dog that was named Japanese?" "Chinese-Wing Foo. Why, he was-he was-he died,

Chunky." I tried to soften it off a bit, remembering that ghastly morning in front of the house. I needn't of bothered. "I remember now! Did all the truck run over himor just a piece? Do you know that song, 'London britches falling down'? We sing it at nursery schoolonly we're on holiday. My mother always says on holiday is the way the English boys say it and she was very busy bundling for Britain-so she liked to say things that way. The other childrens say vacation. Would you like me to sing that song?" The washing machine chugged on and the dryer whirled like a dancing dervish but Chunky won out. "London britches falling down, falling down, falling -down. London britches falling-down-my-fair-la- -dy. And then the childrens grab you and you go to prison. It's lots of fun. Did you ever go to prison?" "Not lately." 21

"My mother knows people in prison-bad men and ladies. She says they're victims of-anyway, my mother fixes them up all right-she's on that committee. She's on every committee and she does all the work." "I'm sure she does, Chunky-how about some lunch?”

For that embarrassing moment-always bring out food. 

Hoskins joined us and made a light meal of everything 

I could lay my hands on. "He only eats once a day," advised his proud owner. "Mostly at night, but I guess he just wants to be polite.

Want to hear him talk to his fleas? He can talk and scratch, too, and he gets awful mad at them." Hoskins performed nobly-with mutterings and growls and a staccato yelp at intervals. I couldn't help laughing until

I was limp. Kept wishing Jim could see him. Perhaps he will-my hunches are pretty reliable-and I have a hunch that this won't be the last time he'll be under our roof. 

In the middle of lunch the phone rang. The front door bell rang. The dog let out deep guttural barks and then began to howl like the Hound of the Baskervilles. 

There was a roar overhead and the throbbing of the motors of heavy planes. Artie was on the phone, "Hold everything-go down cellar-enemy planes are one hour off the coast-Murray Field is taking to the air-they're evacuating the wives and children."

The door bell still rang-the dog still barked. Chunky had climbed into my lap and thrown his arms around my neck until I could scarcely breathe. "Come on home, Artie,” I managed to get out. If this was the real thing I wanted my family near me. I wanted Jim and Peggy. I wanted-of course, I wanted safety and protection, and for a second I felt completely 22

panicky. Chunky tightened his hold around my neck and plastered himself flat against me. The fire sirens started to blow from the main fire house and down in the hollow and across the bay in the other villages. "Artie!" But he had hung up. Suddenly I came to my senses. Chunky needed me, the dog needed me, my job was on the home front and this was no time to be thinking of how desperately much I'd rather be somewhere else. I loosened the tight and frantic little arms around my neck and eased his tense body until he was standing on the floor and clinging to my hand. "Let's go see who's at the door, Chunky. It might be something exciting."

It was. The excitement of seeing a very ordinary man in a very ordinary job suddenly grow big before your eyes. He was selling a new kind of kitchen gadget that cut vegetables so they looked like roses or latticed windows. "Come in," I said. "It might be better inside-if the news is true." "Gee, lady-you look kind of upset. Me-I don't worry. Either it's my time or it isn't and this war ain't going to be over tomorrow-or the day after. Take it as it comes and do my job the best I can-that's the way I figure. Now this here tool is all a housewife needs to have her home table the equal in style and class to the best the hotel world can offer-reverse this end and you make balls out of potatoes, carrots, beets. Reverse the other end and you have French curls that can adorn a can-apy tray."

Chunky forgot his fears and moved closer. 23

"My dog has curls-but he's of English-so would his be English curls?" 

The old man put back his head and roared. "Smart kid you got there, lady-take after you or your husband -if you'll pardon the question?" Before I could answer he was off again. "Your looks, his brains-well, the ladies like to be pretty and I guess that's what the world needs -something easy on the eyes to look at. Now, this center rod unscrews and acts like a de-threader, so to speak, on celery. Leaves it smooth as glass and no strings."

I still kept listening to the sirens and wishing he would go-yet hoping he wouldn't. It's going to take time to make the good old pioneer woman stock come out of me. When I'm scared stiff or worried I want a man around-preferably Jim, but almost any man seems more to lean on than almost any woman except Bethbut she's a wonder. Never lets you down when you need her most. 

False alarm on the air raid, but the real thing will come sooner or later. If the rest of the world can take it so can we. Common people have uncommon courage when real danger and mutual need brings it out of them. 

December 11.-Cleaned the pantry this morning-both because it needed it, heaven knows, and because Artie went in this morning for the first step toward enlistment. We aren't giving our son, because he isn't ours to give. But we aren't holding him back. Jim and I have always felt the youngsters were ours to help and to love but they belong to themselves and it's our job to stand back and give them a hand when they need it. 

But it's so much easier to have a theory than to live up to it. Discovered a cup of dried split peas in the far 24

corner of one shelf and put them to soak for soup. A ham bone in the refrigerator greeted them like a long lost friend, and a blade of celery, a stray bay leaf, an onion joined the party. What do people do without onions! We like them in everything except ice cream 

I think. Must get some frankfurters to slice and brown in butter and simmer in the thick soup just before it's done. How Jim and Artie will love that. Hope it rains, because then it will be perfect. Crispy, hot corn bread, grapefruit salad, and chocolate pie for dessert. It's fun and satisfaction to watch my menfolks eat and enjoy their vittles. Grand old time expression that word vittles. 

Reminds me of Della Lutes. That woman writes about food so that it would make you hungry even after a 

Thanksgiving dinner.

December 12.-Beth and I left all the things we should do to go and do the thing we wanted to do-Christmas shopping at the Outlet Shop in Hampton. Nine-tenths of the stuff is junk-pure and simple-but the thrill is in finding the tenth that is really good and amazingly' cheap. Found a perfect stocking present for Artie. A 

book, Ventriloquism-The Art of It. I remembered the time he sent ten whole cents by mail to get a volume- "A complete treatise on the art of ventriloquism—ten easy lessons that can be mastered in leisure hours." He read lesson one and gave up in disgust. "All it is about is the art of it and I wanted to use it at school tomorrow -we have a substitute." 

Bought Peg a clear glass bowl for ivy-cost fifteen cents and across the street in Ye Gifte Shoppe they are a dollar. More money for Defense stamps! A really nice leather wallet for Jim-complete with seals of gold and 25

cellophane wrapping and the brand name of a company that advertises in the high-priced magazines. What human being doesn't love a bargain? Odds and ends of lovely gift paper for three cents a package and big gold stars for seals at two cents a dozen. This Christmas has to be wonderful and beautiful and happy. 

Went over to Murray and Phipps to get a box of face powder. Came away with a new thought and a rather pleased feeling-even though the young lady at the beauty counter probably thought I was crazy. She tried to sell me everything in the place including a special tissue cream at two-fifty a jar and some eye shadow that would have brought whoops of mirth from Artie and Jim-strangely enough they seem to sort of like me as 

I am and never mind the glamor girl stuff. I told the young lady that all I wanted was a box of my usual powder.

She gave me a tender smile and pulled out the bedside manner, "But, Madam-one's coloring changes as one”—she hesitated-"shall we say, matures?" We shall and that is putting it mildly. "I must tell you, Madam, that you have distinct character lines-quite distinct. 

You won't always have this face." At which I smiled quite happily. There are times when I get very tired of this face and the thought of the possibility of a change of scenery was appealing. Anyway, I came away with the face powder and told Beth that it was nice to know that my character lines are quite distinct. Have often wondered just what credit you get for gaining strength of character if it isn't visible to the naked eye. 

December 15.-Wire from Peg this morning that has me absolutely baffled. Jim said sensibly we'll probably 26

get the whole thing straight when Peg arrives. We probably shall but I want to know now! Am I about to become a mother-in-law, am I already a mother-in-law? 

Who or what is Bill Curtis and what has become of the Navy? Peg has no mathematical sense-just like her dear mother-and she rambles on in a telegram with no thought of ten words for something or other. I've read the thing twenty times until I know it by heart. "Bill 

Curtis driving me down. Will stay overnight at his mother's in Syracuse. Prodigal daughter returns. Get out the bank. Hope you have room for Joan. Love and ten thousand kisses. Peg." Life is anything but dull when you're a parent at any stage of your career. 

December 16.-Jim has signed up as Air Raid Warden and goes to the first meeting tonight. This still doesn't seem real and yet I know it is. Think I'd better get busy and do some signing up myself. Just at present my main job seems to be looking after the family-and Chunky and Hoskins. They're here for the day because Ruth has to go to Washington to see a man. Anyway, I like

Chunky, and Hoskins rather grows on me. Now I know why they build houses with center halls like this one. 

The general idea seems to be that the family will use the center hall in going to the kitchen and thus save the dining room from becoming a passageway. Maybe some families do. But ours takes the dining room in nothing flat whenever the phone rings or they smell something good cooking in the kitchen or they want a drink of water at the sink. But Hoskins knows what halls are for-he lies across ours, on the bias, between the bench --which is a catch-all for everything on earth-and the wall. Just fills the space nicely so that you fall over 27

him or step on him or kick him every time you decide you'll go the hall way this time. Then you have to take time out to pet him and soothe his wounded feelings. Never saw such melting and accusing eyes. Makes you feel you are the lowest and meanest of living creatures. If he's going to be here for the duration-and it certainly looks like it—I'll have to find out what he eats and where he sleeps when he's at home. I asked Chunky but all I got was, "He likes mostly beefsteak and my bed." 

December 17.-Peg arrived this afternoon with Joan who is what Art terms "Smooth" and calls me "Little mother." It seems Bill Curtis was simply a form of transportation. He was on his way down for a dizzy round of good times with his best girl. Has no interest whatever in our Peggy-which seems strange and unbelievable but true-and they stayed at his mother's to break the trip. Joan just came along because she decided to come along at the last minute. The mention of the band was only because Peg was so delighted to be coming home and knew we would feel the same. We do but it's hard at my advancing age to adjust myself to the casualness of the younger generation. They're grand but it takes a lot of breath to keep up with them. Joan remarked to Peg this evening that she simply adored 

Peg's mother because she was so quaint! Perhaps I should have bought that tissue cream and eye shadow.

I feel like a memorial statue and should have some appropriate quotation engraved around my base. Something like the verse we used to chant in school-"Be good sweet maid and let who will be clever. Do noble deeds not dream them all day long." Even then it 28

seemed to me that being clever was much more interesting. Noble deeds sound so noble-and so smug. 

December 19.-Letter from Sally Greene that she and

Jud and Cindy would love to come down for the holidays if we can still have them. The house will be full and running over-just the perfect way for Christmas. 

Jud and Sally are almost too nice to be relatives. Fourth cousins once removed I guess. Never have figured it out but Sally's mother was my father's half-brother's wife. Jim says I get this completely mixed up and that it's really very simple. So if he says fourth cousins that's all right with me. And I'm tickled to death to know they're coming. What fun to have Santa Claus back again and a stocking for a three-year-old! Little Cindy has more personality and charm than any child I ever saw-except our Peggy-but maybe I'm prejudicedmaybe I am. 

December 21.-I decided, once and for all, that this year I am going to be systematic about getting ready for Christmas. Usually the night before I'm racing around like a seven-day bicycle racer and making queer noises because I can't find something that I've hidden only too well or have forgotten something that is absolutely necessary. Last year it was our usual stollen for 

Christmas morning breakfast and Jim tracked down every bakery within ten miles before he found one. He says that my childlike faith that stores are going to be open and have in stock exactly what I want at any hour of the twenty-four is touching. I always figure that you might as well have the pleasure of thinking that everything is going to be fine and dandy-mostly it isn't, but sometimes it is and trouble catches up with you soon 29

enough without looking for it. Always think of Jim's favorite quotation-"A pessimist is a man who has to live with an optimist." But he always quotes it with a smile in his eyes and loves me anyway. 

Bought the paper plates for Christmas breakfast and had to get two patterns because Reid's couldn't get enough new stock and this is all they had left-something about a paper shortage. Anyway the ones I bought are gay with red poinsettas and holly and a fat chuckling Santa Claus spang in the middle of some of them. Found green paper cups for the fruit juice and napkins that are grand and gaudy. Think the same old menu is best for the buffet breakfast. Gallons of fruit juice colored with cranberry, stollen, Christmas jam, hot bacon rolls, and two gallons of coffee. Everybody likes to eat off and on between opening presents and nobody could possibly sit down to a regular table. 

December 22.-Sally and Jud and Cindy coming tomorrow! Beds made all fresh and welcoming. I love to make beds for people I love. So much more to it than just putting on sheets and pillow cases and blankets and spreads. It's creating comfort and peace and happinessand this year it seems more important and worthwhile than ever. So much hatred and bitterness and destruction in the world, but inside these four walls there can still be gentleness and kindness and thought for each other.

Had the queerest experience this morning while I was baking the cake for Christmas Eve supper. The kitchen smelled simply heavenly with the spice in the air and the fragrance of bubbling apple sauce for the 30

cake. There was a knock at the back door and when I called, "Come in!" the door opened slowly and there stood an old gypsy woman as wrinkled and brown as a persimmon. She looked at me and I looked at her without a word from either of us. Haven't seen one of them since way last summer when the Carnival was on. They usually vanish south with the first birds when a cool breeze blows up from the ocean. She came in and sat on the high stool by the sink. "Much good luck, lady, some bad but much good-I tell your fortune?" "No," I said firmly, thinking of Jim and how he makes fun of me for my idiotic attachment to any and all kinds of dealers in the future. "No, I'm very busynot today." "Much good luck-the future holds surprises-only twenty-five cent, lady." 

That finished me-I do like a bargain. Much good luck-surprises-it seemed a shame not to take it. Besides, she was old and the wind was blowing colder and the coffee pot was still half full-and it was Christmas almost. She took my hand in her dry, wrinkled palm. "A man child you will have in this house, under this roof, before the flowers are on the trees again. A wedding there will be-sudden and with tears-but with happiness. And à stranger thing will happen to one you love-a blossoming and a singing in the heart. Not tomorrow, not for many coming and going of the moon, but it will come when snow flies and the birds are gone.'

We sat and drank coffee together and ate toasted 

31

honey bun. She ate silently then stood to go. "The recipe for the cake, lady? It smells like Christmas, and my man he would like one for pleasant dreams and luck for the new year. My man he is in hospital and I stay by him when my people go. You give me the recipe, lady?" I gave her the recipe, I gave her a dollar, and

I wrapped a package of mincemeat cookies for her with a ribbon and a ball from the Christmas-tree trimmings tied on the top. Maybe her story is made up in her own head, maybe I'm silly and trusting to the point of feeblemindedness. But when the tears come in my eyes I have to follow my heart wherever it leads and it usually leads me straight. 

December 23.-Peg and Joan went out this morning and came back with a red cellophane stocking for each one and a couple extra just in case. Tied a silver bell on top and Joan lettered the tags. She seems to be getting a great thrill out of helping. Her surface is so sort of airy and unreal that I wonder. While they worked 

I heard her say to Peg, "Never had a Christmas like this with all the fixings. 

Dad and Mother are sort of friendly enemies, if you know what I mean, and December twenty-fifth is just another day. When, and if, I ever go off the deep end my kids are going to-" Then she started humming the latest number one on the Hit Parade. Somebody else to add to my worry list as Jim calls it. 

Sally and Jud arrived-bags, baggage-and Cindy. She is such a tiny thing and climbs right into your heart and makes herself at home. When I get to be a grandmother I'm going to write a short book of instruction on "How to Spoil a Child-in Five Easy Lessons." I 32

know I'll be an authority on it because I take to it so naturally. Grandchildren must be wonderful. You get all the pleasure and none of the real responsibility. If they turn out a credit to the family you can beam and boast. If they don't you can always blame it on the way their parents brought them up and imply, by delicate inference, you would have done so much better a job. 

Cindy has Jim and Art wrapped right around her baby fingers and tied in a bow. She refers to the house as "her" house and beams at Jim. She turns her hundred-watt smile on Artie and says, "Her's a very nice boy-and I love her." She connects me with food and funny songs and Peg and Joan with the charms and delights of perfume and powder and says we all smell nice. In fact she has the social gift of charm and making each one feel important and special. Top that with a golden fluff of curls, deep brown eyes with lashes that flutter and curl, hands so graceful that you could watch them fascinated for minutes on end and I really can't see any room for improvement. 

December 24.-So glad I planned everything aheadnothing like system. The total result was that I was spinning around twice as fast as usual when the great moment arrived. Jud and Art decided they would buy the tree. It is simply enormous. So we needed more trimming and Peg and Joan dashed down to Reid's to gather up a few extra things. The tree is so big that we had to move the dining-room furniture every which way to make room. Good thing we did because it looks twice as festive as in the usual locations. The baked ham was a vision of loveliness with deep amber streaks of sugar and spice trailing down its sides and spiced apri- 33

cots-made them myself from canned ones I got at a bargain-marching around the big blue platter and set off by molds of celery and cranberry salad and parsley. 

Peg says she believes I wouldn't enjoy my own funeral unless there was parsley somewhere in the floral display. The big glass casserole-how many happy times that has served-seemed extra bubbling with the escalloped potatoes with bits of onion through them and a crusty top that oozed goodness and fragrance. I am such an infant about Christmas and never get over the excitement and thrill of the usual things we always have and the unusual things added in at the last minute. Like the beautiful cheese cake that Laurie brought for her share of the supper. Words simply couldn't do justice to the creamy filling with the tang of freshly grated lemon rind and the zip of fresh lemon juice-no substitute flavoring for Laurie when she can get the real thing. Set me wondering if you couldn't tell character by cooking. Some people are all for the quick and easy does it and others take the hard way because they aren't satisfied with anything but perfection-fascinating thought. The buttery crust was exactly brown enough to blend with the filling and the whole thing went with our big bowl of Southern Christmas ambrosia-so glad 

I grated the fresh cocoanut instead of using the canned.

All the vitamins may be in the cans but the soul has fled. 

Bruce Stevens dropped by to bring us a bottle of the Spanish sherry that Jim purrs over. He couldn't stay because he had a deskful of work to clean up and he might get some emergency calls-his housekeeper has gone home for Christmas. "And you're parking here," said Jim. "You can 34

switch your calls to this number-nobody with any sense would get sick on Christmas Eve. Just forget the overworked doctor stuff and enjoy yourself." 

He couldn't stay-but he did. The house full and every room alive with someone laughing and wrapping last-minute presents and whispering about surprises for somebody else. Radio going, youngsters dancing. Cindy sleeping through it all without a peep-when she sleeps she certainly concentrates on it.

We all trimmed the tree and argued, as usual, about which ornament should go where and whether the silver rain ought to be thrown at the branches or carefully hung on piece by piece. The vote was pretty evenly divided so we each took our own method and the final result was heavenly. We stood back to admire it and, all at once, I missed Laurie. Where had she gone? 

How long had she been gone? I'm a horrible hostess because I always have such a good time at my own parties that I forget to see that everyone else does-mostly they do, I guess, because they seem to want to come again. I scouted around the lower floor and no sign of 

Laurie, but her things were still on our accommodating hall bench. I went upstairs and Bruce followed me. Not a sign or sound of her up there either. All the doors were open except Cindy's and Bruce stopped in front of it. "Mind if I take a peek at the youngster? Purely nonprofessional interest." He opened the door and the Mystery of the Vanished Guest was solved. Laurie was sitting on the floor beside the bed with one hand on 

Cindy's goldy topknot. "I wanted to see her tonight," she whispered. "She 35

couldn't be as lovely as that picture you sent me—but she is-she's-she's-" There was a catch in her voice and she didn't finish. Out in the hall Bruce looked down at Laurie. She is such a little thing, and he always looks like a kid who is stretching out of his clothes. "Fond of youngsters, aren't you?" he asked. "In a way," she said briefly. Why does Laurie freeze up like that with most people-especially men? I know why, but it's pretty hard to go back twenty years or so and fight it out with her father. From what little she has told me of him he must have been about as warm and cozy as Grant's Tomb on a rainy day. I could have shaken her just the same, because Bruce is absolutely daffy over children and as sensitive as an adolescent. 

You could have measured the tension if you had the right instrument—at any rate, you could feel it and I was miserable. I'm always miserable when I'm so fond of people and they rub each other the wrong way. 

When we went downstairs there was too much noise, too much confusion, too much to eat, too much trimming on the tree-exactly the way I love it. We hung the stockings-eight of them-in front of the fireplace. 

Played Stille Nacht on the Schumann-Heink record that we've played every Christmas Eve for years and years. Never have I had the warm depth and richness of a voice give me such pleasure-what tenderness and understanding comes through it. I thought of how she had endured the last war with her sons in opposing armies-only a great woman would have distilled from its bitterness the sweetness and strength which she has.

Only the lights from the tree and the candles on the mantel were glowing softly and we all sat there in si- 36

lence while the last notes faded away. Jim reached for my hand and held it close. There were tears in Joan's eyes-I hope they were from happiness for she has missed so much. No matter what comes to us all, I thought, no matter where we may be-and that line of poetry ran through my head, "The safe kept memory of a lovely thing." 37

January 

MAKES NO DIFFERENCE TO ME, MADAM-CHUNKY SPENDS THE DAY, AND WE TIE UP THE FIRE DE- PARTMENT-ATTIC JUNK-LIFE IS A CHEMICAL MISTAKE-ART'S TEETH GO TO WASHINGTON—A CANDLE-LIT MEAL-LONG DISTANCE CALLING. 

January 2.-Today has pretty well convinced me that

Jim and Artie are right in telling me that I'm the Little Home Woman type and nothing can be done about it -I certainly function best in my own territory. I'm also convinced that I'm not equipped, by nature or inclination, to be a strictly law-abiding citizen. Every time I try it I get myself in a perfect mess. So many laws, to me, are something that Bob Smith, Representative from Nebraska, fixes up with Tom Jones, Representative from New Hampshire. "You vote for my bill and I'll vote for yours"-no more sense or reason to them than that. I tell Jim I'm perfectly willing to obey sensible laws-but so few of them are. Anyway, I'm too literal minded, I guess, to be trusted with a mere statement of the law-it needs to be interpreted. 

With gas rationing looming and tire rationing practically day after tomorrow, Sally and I decided we'd take Cindy in to see the sights of the big city while the car was still functioning. Made a huge casserole of Syrian lamb with tomatoes and onions and canned green beans, cooked a whole pound of brown rice so I'd have some left over for caramel rice pudding, fixed up a noble coffee Bavarian with whipped evaporated milk- 38

I'm certainly learning to teach a budget how to take a joke. Anyway, dinner was sitting in the refrigerator so we could make an early start and a late finish. Just as we were ready to leave the door bell rang-sorry Artie fixed it. Ruth Woods-complete in uniform-stood on the step with Chunky by her side. Her face fell fifteen feet and bounced right back when she saw I had my hat and coat on and Sally and Cindy bringing up the rear. "What am I going to do?" she fairly bleated. "What about?" I asked, in what Artie calls 

Duchess manner. my best "About Chunky! The maid left this morning-simply walked out, my dear! Wouldn't you think-" When

Ruth starts thinking there is nowhere it leads but farther from the subject at hand. Chunky stood there with the forlornest orphan expression on his solid little face. 

I can stand children when they cry, because half the time it doesn't mean a thing, but when they try to hang on and show so much more character than you think their size will hold it simply finishes me. I looked at 

Sally and caught a reassuring smile and a slight nod of her head. "Leave Chunky here-we can take him in town and we'll all have a grand time-he and Cindy." "Chunky's my best friend," Cindy added, "and he can have some of my lunch and we're going to ride on a bust and eat ice cream and see dolls. Please let

Chunky come!" This last urging was hardly necessary. 

Ruth leapt like a mountain goat and beamed on one and all.

Well, we started off and I found the Parkway right 39

where I'd left it the last time-which always surprises me. I took Sally and the youngsters down to Wanamaker's, parked the car in a masterly manner, and started off on my own to do a couple of things I had on my mind. We were meeting for lunch and I was going to put my twenty dollars of Christmas money right where it would do the most good-in a Defense Bond. I've bought them out home for months now, but 

I wanted this to be a special one on a special day. I went into one of these things they call a branch postoffice which is so huge you never can find where to mail a letter. Sailed up to a window with all sorts of signs and insignia on it and said, "I'd like to buy an eighteen-seventy-five Defense Bond, please." The face behind the bars simply looked at me wearily. "This is the Defense 

Stamp window, Madam. Go to window five at the end of the room." I went to window five and said my little speech again. The face behind these bars was the most completely bored one I have ever seen. "Fill out the application slip, Madam-go to desk B." 

Application blanks simply floor me-I always put the right things in the wrong places. Out in Oakdale I just tell Mary or Mr. Andrews what I want and they do the writing-they've probably found it saves time in the end. But I was not among friends here, so I filled out the pink thing with all the intelligence I could command. Took it back to window five and the face looked it over with no trace of human emotion. "Makes no difference to me, Madam, but you can't do it this way.' "What have I done wrong?" I asked meekly. "Makes no difference to me, Madam, but you have 

40

to put in the word 'or' when you wish joint ownership. 

Your husband's name and then 'or' and then your name." "Just write it in-and here's the money." I gave him best smile which Jim says always throws people off their guard. But not this lad. my "Makes no difference to me, Madam, but what is your name?" I told him and he sighed with the weariness of an old, old man. "Nobody'd ever guess it—and what is your address? Makes no difference to me but you got to have an address." Apparently nothing made any difference to him-and everything did. I was getting more addled by the minute and decided my next contribution to National Defense would be in the form of an anonymous gift to Washington-just saw in the paper that money gifts to the Government had jumped from thirty a month to seven hundred a week-perhaps this explains it more than plain patriotism. "My home address or my husband's business address?" I asked with all the assurance which I didn't have. I was going to go down with the flag flying if possible. But the young man knew the answer to that one, too. He sighed from the roots of his being and blew his nose with a mighty blast. "Makes no difference to me, Madam-just put down an address-you got to have an address."

The money changed hands and I stood-and then stood some more. Somewhere in the dim back corner 

I heard the sound of a typewriter. A paper was thrust at me under the bars. I took it gratefully and thought happily of the air and freedom. But the representative or servant of the people was not through. open 4I

"Madam," he said, "did you ever buy one of these bonds before?" "Lots of times-but I've bought them out home and they're used to me, I guess, and don't expect me to be too bright." "Makes no difference to me, Madam, but if I was you I'd continue to buy them out there." 

I definitely decided that I would never breathe all this to Jim and Artie but just say casually, "I bought a bond in town today." But it was too good to keep and they would feel so disappointed if I didn't bring them up to date on the Perils of Pauline as Jim calls my adventures. But the bond buying was as nothing-a mere gentle opening chorus-to what happened on the way home. We had a grand lunch sitting in the restaurant up high and looking out the windows on the city spread below us. Cindy had all the waitresses at her beck and call with her remark to each one as she passed, "You're a pretty lady." What natural born woman wouldn't fall for that? Chunky sat there in absolute silence and ate everything in sight with an endless stream of rolls coming up and butter vanishing like so much snow in the sunshine. I can't think of anything that satisfies me much more than to watch a healthy child eat. I like to feed them and I love to cook for them. I remind myself of the grandmother who prayed that when she died she'd be allowed to mend the little angels' britches. 

Only I'd pray I could make warm gingerbread, with raisins in it, and pour endless glasses of milk all through eternity-that would be heaven for me and I'll bet the little boy and girl angels would enjoy it, too!

We rode on the top of the bus and we window 42

shopped and we did the five and ten from top to bottom and front to back-it's better than a Fireman's Carnival and a three-ring circus rolled into one. Town looked about as usual except for the many men and boys in uniform and the windows showing officers' uniforms and boxes and baskets to be sent to camp. When Artie goes I must gather up all the packable recipes I can find and send him lots of home-cooked things. How much love and affection and real tenderness can be said with cookies and cake and nut bread and how easing it is to the heart to lighten the spirit through work with your hands. I think it was Oscar Wilde that said somewhere, "Nothing cures the physical like the spiritualnothing cures the spiritual like the physical." 

We left town about four and I had a wonderful time twisting through the traffic. It's fun to see if you can be as smart as a taxi driver-and a little smarter, because gallantry to the frailer sex is not their long suit. 

They'll try anything they think they can get away with the minute their eyes light on a woman at the wheel.

Just the same, it's quite a help that they are convinced that all women drivers are dumb and if you do anything wrong they accept it as the inevitable. 

Bowled down Queens Boulevard with the elevated roaring on my left and the cars and roller skating boys playing tag on my right. All of a sudden I heard a fire siren and the shadow of the law fell over me. I remembered the huge sign on one building, "At the first sound of the siren protect your firemen, obey the law, pull to the curb and stop." I did just that. The siren grew louder, men began running and shouting and waving their arms. I sat there with such a comfortable and sat- 43

isfied feeling that, come what might, I was a law-abiding citizen doing my duty for God and my country. A perfectly horrible blast on a horn nearly blew me out of the seat. Bells were ringing, more men were shouting. I sat on. Suddenly Sally grabbed my arm, "Look! 

Look where we are!" I looked to my right and two enormous ruby eyes were glaring at me through the dark. The front lights on the largest fire engine I ever hope to meet except in a nightmare. I was parked straight spang across the firehouse door and the engine was snorting within two feet of our fenders!

When I told Jim and Artie they simply looked at me with their mouths open-speechless. "It's a gift," said Jim finally. "Nobody but you would ever think of tying up the fire department of a city the size of New York-and doing it singlehanded-nobody but you."

Artie laughed until he was flopped on the davenport, perfectly limp. "Boy-turn you loose in the air force and you'd have the war over by Saturday." "Me and Major Eliot," I answered. As a newspaper commentator he's a one-man General Staff and Admiral of the Navy. Major Eliot Bids, Major Eliot Warns, Major Eliot Urges, Major Eliot Demands. Once in a week or so he softens up a little and Major Eliot Counsels.

When we reached home in nothing flat, after my effort to obey the law and protect the firemen, we found Joan and Peggy with the table set, the house lighted, the casserole simmering fragrantly in the oven, and a huge bowl of what Jim and Artie call "rabbit food" mixed and ready to serve. Can't see why all 44

mothers are so surprised when their children do finally grow up and begin to take hold. It's what we train them for and expect them to do eventually, but you can't quite believe it when the time arrives. Home and peace and the family all around seemed marvelous. My brain cells had quite enough of a workout for one day. And it was good to see Hoskins come bounding up the steps to greet us all like long lost friends. Peg said he had been sitting on the clammy front step when she and

Joan got down about eleven and simply refused to budge. His long, silky ears flapped with joy until he reminded me of Dumbo. He licked our hands and rubbed against us and gave funny little sounds in his throat. "He was waiting for me!" said Chunky. "He was waiting for me and he found this house." No doting father could have been more proud of his first born. A dog and a boy speak the same language and there is something between them that can't be put into words. 

What difference does it make if there is mud on the kitchen floor and hairs on the overstuffed furniturethey just don't count. Houses last so long and children grow up so quickly. 

January 4.-Sally and I spent most of the day going through the trunks and accumulation of junk in the attic to see what was worth giving for salvage, what could be made over into something useful, and what could be given to the district nurse. One good thing that is going to come out of this mess of war is a lot more cellars and attics with breathing room instead of enough stuff for three rummage sales. It's simply unbelievable the amount of junk you can accumulate in the course 45

of twenty years of married life and children and more than ample storage space. Artie was off in town for another examination of some kind-the Air Force certainly leaves nothing to chance or guesswork. Art doesn't say much and I don't ask him-at least, that's one bit of sense I've learned in living with two adolescents. They're about as chatty as a talkative clam when you try to get any information but they'll tell you almost anything if you give them time. He showed me a news item at breakfast this morning that both he and 

Jim grinned over. It said, "Vegetarian groups are pressuring the army to set up special units for soldiers who have conscientious scruples about eating meat." "That's one outfit you won't find me in," Art laughed. "If they ever put me in the Watercress Division you'll be looking for me A.W.O.L." 

January 5.-Joan and Peg have to start back for college on the seventh so they went off today for a final fling at the big city. They are both so full of life and enthusiasm that it's fun having them around-only I almost wish they would take things a little more seriously. Just the same Peg has moods here lately that worry me-she gets so down and is silent for hours. 

Every time the phone rings she jumps a foot and then sort of slumps again when the call isn't for her. She hasn't said a word about her nice Navy Lieutenant. He writes nearly every day, but the letters might as well be in Greek for all I know about them. One of the hardest things a mother has to stand is to learn to be on the outside helpless when she longs to be on the insidehelping.

Chunky was left on the doorstep again this morning 46

after a harried call from his mother who is at another crisis in her military life. Hoskins came along and licked my hands in a joyous but dignified greeting. Sort of reminded me of English novels where the old family butler greets the young master after a long absencethe manner restrained and correct but welcoming. 

Sally didn't feel very well and went upstairs to lie down while Cindy and Chunky took their so called naps. I could hear Cindy telephoning to one of her numerous unseen friends. She carries on a two-way conversation that is worth anybody's time to listen to— "Hello-whatcha doin'? That's good! You washing the dishes? That's good! Your baby O.K.? That's good! 

You coming over to play with me pitty soom? That's good! O.K. Good-by."

Chunky was playing Heigh-ho Silver sort of under his breath and then turning into a high powered bomber with sound effects. How Sally slept is more than I can figure but she said she did. She's had dizzy spells lately and has lost her appetite. I wish she could stay longer but they have to leave on the tenth-what an empty echo there will be in this big house when everybody's gone. Jim and I will simply rattle around and the house will be so orderly that I'll wish for somebody to mess it up. Laurie Pratt said once that if she ever has a house it is going to be full of children at the disorderly age where nothing is ever in place or finished. Guess she had enough years of a well-to-do, properly staffed menage that was about as cheerful as a nicely conducted funeral. I get little pieces of her life every time I see her until it begins to fit together and make a picture. 

She's had everything-and nothing. No wonder she 47

twists her handkerchief when she sits down for five split seconds. I do wish she'd go to see Bruce Stevens and get him to diagnose and prescribe treatment. He has a genius for seeing inside of people's minds and hearts when all they are complaining of physically is persistent headaches. Laurie doesn't complain of anything-just says she's tired all the time and wants to cry if anyone looks at her. What she really needs is a pair of lively twins that would keep her up nights-never knew anyone with such a love for children. She looks at Cindy with her heart in her eyes and tells stories to her and Chunky by the hour. They'll camp on her doorstep when she gets moved. 

January 6.-Sally and I had dinner alone tonight and at the big dining room table we looked like a scene in the baronial castle Orson Welles created in Citizen Kane. 

I still think that was one of the most marvelous pictures I've ever looked at-and one of the most cruel, like seeing the living spirit of a man on a dissecting table. Reminded me someway of The Portrait of Do rian Gray and I got to thinking how differently a human life would develop if there were more understanding of children. Unkindness breeds unkindness.

Jim phoned that he wouldn't be home until late. The factory is working on three eight-hour shifts and the office end of it is running on one twenty-four-hour shift as far as I can make out. Jim is so tired when he gets home that he just sits and I have to poke food down him by fair means or otherwise. Good thick soup seems to be my best bet. He likes Scotch barley broth with the chunks of tender meat sort of snuggling down in the soup part that is silky smooth with the oatmeal 48

thickening and has color and flavor accents of diced carrots and celery and onion and white turnip. Lamb shanks are fine for it and still reasonably cheap compared to other meat with half the nourishment. I put it in one of the red Chinese bowls and set that on a green plate and carry it in to him by the fire-the Christmas tree branches will help to make a fragrant blaze for quite awhile. Henry, the trash man, dumps his collection of them in our driveway as soon as he picks up a few on his rounds. Henry does admire my cooking. 

And I like Henry.

Art was out with some of the boys who are enlisting with him. They seem so young and yet so old. Peg and 

Joan phoned from town that they would be delayed and not to wait dinner. So Sally and I sat down to scrambled eggs with what appears to be the last can of anchovies in the village. So many things are disappearing from the grocery shelves that there are empty spaces in new spots each week. Keeps you thinking fast and changing your mind painlessly to figure out meals as you go along. Art and Jim say the meals we have are better than ever which must mean that I am using my imagination more. It's an inspiration to cook for this family because they always act starved to death and seconds are the usual order of the day. I don't know what I'd do with a family of fussy eaters. I remember the night that Art made a light going-to-bed snack out of a quarter of a cake and remarked, "Thought I might as well finish it-there was only one piece." 

About nine, when Sally and I had settled down with our knitting and the radio, the girls came in. I didn't recognize their steps at first because they were walk- 49

ing so slowly. Instead of the usual, "What's to eat?” they just came in and sat down. "Anything the matter?" I asked as casually as I could.

They both looked at me and then at each other. I thought of nine million things-and none of them the right one. "Spill it, Peg," said Joan. "Well-we both gave a blood transfusion-and we both passed out-the heroes return!" 

I hope the old ladies, who have been clicking their store teeth over the softness of this generation, will relax a little and admit that we have a pretty grand lot of youngsters growing up. They're honest and spunky and have plenty of courage. They may pass out-but they're up and at 'em again and don't lean on anybody. 

January 7.-Wartime certainly does bring out the worst and the best in people. I opened the door this morning to the latest thing in rackets. A young man, who looked like the illustration for a collar ad, was standing there and said, “Ah-good morning, Madam!” with just the correct modulation of voice to be used on the ageing to throw them off their guard-but for once I was a trifle cautious. "Good morning." 

He stepped inside the entry, but I stood my ground and wondered whether it was a magazine subscription that would be the very last one he needed to win the District Scholarship that would finish his last year in college. I know that one by heart having built up practically an entire college class because of my inability to say "No" at the right moment. But this was different.

He was doing his bit, or so he said, in the great battle 50

for Democracy by offering, at absolute cost, a specialized service to prepare all types of heating equipment against air raids. "And furthermore, this non-profit making organization is including ten pounds of chemically treated sand for the extinguishing of incendiary bombs-a recent discovery, Madam, and a most valuable one. All we are asking is for a slight donation of only five dollars to help us carry our overhead—there is no profit involved.

The sand will be delivered immediately-I may even have one bag of it in my car-and the furnace service will be attended to as soon as we catch up with our long waiting list, Madam." 

Must remember to tell Jim I really didn't fall for this. 

That's one, if only one, gold star on my good conduct chart. I suppose I should dutifully have reported him to the police, but my civic conscience doesn't function to the point of being personally responsible for getting my fellow man in jail. This one will probably land there before his waiting list gets too long, but I don't want to wake up in the middle of the night with his too handsome face looking at me through bars and begin to wonder if he had a mother who understood him. 

Why are women such saps? Especially me.

Sat down with the paper a few minutes and saw an item that made me sort of smile to myself-things do even up once in awhile even for the people who never seem to have a chance. The farmers of California are all stirred up because of a labor shortage and are complaining bitterly because so many of the Okies are going into Defense work. Reminds me of the suburban realtors. Before the depression they were urging the 51

joys of home ownership-whether you had any money at all. Huge houses were sold with no cash involvedand three mortgages. Then came the slump and the real estate section of the papers was full of the wails of the realtors that people should have had better sense than to have bought on a shoestring and it was all the purchaser's fault. Guess it's just human nature to blame things on the other fellow-makes your own conscience much more comfortable.

The new baby of the new family down the road began to tune up and sounded like a stray and forlorn kitten. It has been so long since our young were at that stage that I couldn't quite identify the sound at first. 

It went on and on and I certainly felt sympathy for the mother if she was half as upset and worried as I used to be when Peg gave voice. While she yelled I was sure she was in agonizing pain-when she stopped I was sure she had died of it and had to take just one peek to reassure myself. The one peek was all that was needed to start the chorus all over again. By the time 

Art came along I knew a whole lot more and took him with less strain on my nerves and emotions. It would be a grand idea if every mother could have her second baby first.

Jim and I have an addition to our vocabulary. I heard 

Peg and Joan talking upstairs, while they were packing, and every once in awhile Joan would come out with, "Gad! How foul!" She also announced to us last night that life is just a chemical mistake and religion an opiate for the undeveloped mind. "Ever hear of Einstein?" asked Jim. 52

Joan sounded suspicious, as well she might when Jim throws out seemingly casual remarks. "Certainly." "Ever see an amoeba?" "In the lab," Joan said cautiously. "Why? What's that got to do with it?"

Jim took his pipe out of his mouth-I wouldn't own a husband without a pipe-it gives a man such a contented look. "Well-Einstein is supposed to be one of the smartest scientists that ever lived. An amoeba is the simplest known form of reproductive life. But can Einstein make an amoeba?"

Peg looked at Joan as though to explain this very beloved but antique specimen of parenthood. But Joan needed no explaining. Her mind was already off in farther fields and wider horizons. But she started the conversation up again along another tack. "Did you ever stop to think that one does not need to be happy in order to live?"

Jim turned toward me and there was a twinkle in his eyes. "It's your lower jaw," he said. How many times we have smiled across a room at each other and quoted that well seasoned joke about the father who was lecturing Junior on some profound truth. When he finished with his impressive remarks he waited for a response. But Junior only looked at him fascinated, "Gee, Daddy, it's your lower jaw that moves when you talk-isn't it?” In twenty years of married life we have quite a collection of you-and-me phrases and jokes that form sort of a sign language between us. Other people may think we're a little queer when we laugh over seemingly nothing, but it's fun to have one person 53

in the world who gets your meaning and mood without an elaborate explanation. I wouldn't trade our secret language for a pass key to the Golden Gate.

January 11.-The house seems so strange and silent as though it were waiting for someone or something. 

Every room in order, everything in place, and I should be thankful, I suppose, for the rest and a breathing spell --but someway I'm not. Signed up today for Red Cross 

Surgical Dressings on Thursdays and Mondays, for the

First Aid Training Course on Wednesday and Friday nights, and promised Ruth Woods she could count on me if the proposed day nursery goes through. My heart wouldn't be in it, for I feel so strongly that love and care of children needs the personal touch. I can't imagine Chunky and Cindy in an impersonal, scientific group no matter how well it was run. A cupboardful of pans on the kitchen floor with an unscientific mother peeling potatoes at the sink is a lot more educational and warm than five feet of the latest equipment on nursery school shelves. When I talk like this Jim smiles across at me and remarks, "For a strictly modern woman you don't keep very up to date in your mental processes-but you suit me fine." And Jim suits me fine-which is quite satisfactory to the management. 

January 14.-Jim and Art and I went to the movies tonight to see Dumbo. What a thrill of joy Walt Disney must get in creating those lovable, human little animals. How he can pull your heart strings and make you laugh and carry away with you tender memories.

It's wonderful to see such superb talent put to such use. Same way I felt about The Yearling by Marjorie

Kinnan Rawlings. Beautiful writing and a subject so 54

well worth writing about. I'll always remember Jody saying to the wild little yearling, "How come you grow up so unlawful." And the father explaining to Jody the reason for his mother's difficult temperament, "Trouble never leaves people the same, Jody. Sometimes it makes them kinder and sometimes the other way-but it never leaves them the same." I'm not sure that I'm remembering it exactly but the philosophy will stay with me a long time. I've always had the theory that unkind people are unhappy people deep down inside themselves perhaps. When you're hurt you want to hurt someone else-or at least that is, unfortunately, the average human reaction. The longer I live the more I feel gentleness and patience and everyday kindness are the most important things in the world. Now more than ever when cruelty and hatred and destruction are ruling so much of the earth. 

January 19.-Lottie Gibson stopped in for five split seconds this morning, and I finally persuaded her that a cup of coffee and my own special cinnamon buns with the raspberry jam in their middles were just what she needed to keep up her morale for National Defense. 

That phrase seems to be the theme song for nine-tenths of the advertising these days, whether it comes on the printed page or by way of radio. The five and ten is pushing Courage lipstick-too bad Molly Pitcher didn't have the advantage of it. Lottie is one woman whom 

I'm glad and proud to see in uniform and I hope they make her a Brigadier General before she's through. The way she has organized this county is a marvel, and she's working twice as hard as though she were pulling down a fat pay check every Saturday come noon. Paul was 55

called down to Washington about two weeks ago and, I guess, is there for the duration. I only hope his brilliant legal mind will help untangle some of the weird confusion there seems to be. The more I read the strictly confidential last-minute reports from Washington, that are spread all over the papers, the less I understand. Maybe Jim and Art have the right diagnosis when they call me "Winnie.” But a lot brighter minds than mine seem to be mixed up too. 

January 20.-Art is down in the dumps-seven miles deep. His final report came from the Air Force. Everything is fine-but. The board has refused his application. The reason being that his teeth do not have proper occlusion! He's strong as a horse and smart as a whip-according to their own tests and records—but his teeth don't meet! "You'd think," said Art disgustedly, "they expected me to chew the enemy. All I want is a chance to shoot a few of them. I'm going down and tell that board just what I think of the whole bunch of addle-headed nuts." "That would solve the whole thing," said Jim. "Take easy and use your head-there must be some kind of appeal to a higher board." it "Sure, I can send a plaster cast of my teeth and jaw to the board in Washington and get their decision. The 

Major on this board told me so-but what's that going to get me? If they don't like my teeth up here are they going to like a reasonable facsimile thereof any better down there?”

Jim finally persuaded him to try it anyway-nothing to lose and perhaps something to gain. We know this disappointment is hard for Art, but we can't help laugh- 56

ing to ourselves over the mental picture we have of a solemn military board sitting around an impressive mahogany table and working a plaster cast of Art's jaw complete with molars, bicuspids, and all the rest. I don't know anything about his occlusion but it certainly never interfered with the way he could clean off the bones on fried chicken. 

January 26.-When Claudine came to assist me with my household infractions, as she calls doing the washing, we heard a horn like no horn this side of paradise.

It had everything-inflections, modulation, a real tune. 

There sat George and Claudine in the most amazing car that Mussolini ever missed buying. It was long and high and red and black-and brass trimmings where they would show the most. George was practically bursting the buttons on his watchman's uniform which he wears off duty and on duty and probably sleeps in. He helped 

Claudine out as though she were the Duchess of Windsor trying to get away from it all. "Jes' purchased a small model so I could bring my little girl to her De-fense position. It's kind of a thirdhand second-hand limousine and she make only six mile to the gallon-but me and Claudine we likes the best." "Got a garage big enough?" asked Jim. 

George threw back his head and laughed with that wholehearted mirth that belongs to him and his race. "We got no garage-this baby she sleep in the yard back of our residence. But Claudine she making a slip cover. 

Claudine she very smart." 

January 28.-Lottie told me the other day that their young Paul was trying to get into some radio angle of the Navy and today the word came for him to report 57

for physical examination. Someway, the only thing that makes me feel old is to see these youngsters grow up before my eyes. It doesn't seem any time since the year when he was taking elementary physiology in school and woke up screaming because he was afraid of the skeleton inside him. Now he is afraid of nothing or nobody and wants to get onto a Navy patrol or convoy service and be in the thick of it.
