He has tinkered with radio for the last five years and loves every tube in one of the animals. Art asked him to come to dinner tonight, and, by special request, I was to make the Saturday lunch dish. First time he had it here was one cold 

Saturday when Art arrived with three of his buddies and I had to scramble around to get enough for them to eat. Growing boys are bottomless wells when it comes to food, and it's such a satisfaction to try to fill them up. I suddenly thought of the dish that my nice 

Pennsylvania Dutch neighbor in Michigan used to fix. 

I can't pronounce or spell what she called it but, my, the flavor and satisfying quality were good! A can of whole grain corn, a pound of cooked string beans, some quartered raw potatoes and about two slices of bacon apiece. The corn and beans are all mixed up and seasoned with salt and a goodly dash of freshly ground pepper. Then the potatoes go in and the slices of bacon on top. My old Dutch oven does it to perfection because the lid fits so tight and the simmering bubbles away in a cozy fashion. The boys loved it and nearly ate me out of house and home. Made hot corn bread to go with it tonight, a cabbage salad with sour cream and flecks of celery seed, and hot gingerbread oozing with spice and molasses and topped with a big blob of 58

vanilla ice cream. The three menfolks simply purred with contentment and I beamed with delight. A nice low cost dinner that tasted like a million. And it snowed and the candles sent a lovely glow over this homely fare.

Everything seemed so peaceful and as it should be with kindliness and affection and good companionship. I wished the world could stand still right there. 

January 30.-Such a day! Went to the Red Cross First 

Aid this morning and heard more dumb questions than could be answered in a month of Sundays. Probably asked my share but, someway, your own remarks always seem highly intelligent. While some of the class were practicing bandaging on some of the rest I kept thinking of that silly verse that is going around. Can't remember all of it but the general refrain is, "Please, Lady, leave me lay! Just please go call a doctor and keep the crowd away. But please don't touch me, Lady. 

Please, Lady, leave me lay." I'm glad the course doesn't include mind reading or I would have been on the outside looking in.

I no sooner reached home than the phone rang and Ruth Woods wanted to know if she could bring Chunky over-just for the afternoon. "I've been trying to reach you all morning-where in the world were you?" "Red Cross First Aid." She sounded surprised to death and said she didn't know I was doing anything for the 

War Effort. I don't know what she calls looking after

Chunky so that she can be released for active duty. Not that I mind, as far as he is concerned, because he is a dandy and I have conniption fits every time I hear of a new "jewel" who is now taking care of Chunky. They 59

are all "jewels" the first three days and then they suddenly turn into "THE most impossible creature, my dear,

I have ever had in the house!" If Chunky wasn't the best-natured little mortal in the world he would have dropped some large, heavy object on one of their heads long ago. Many of the really smart domestic help have gone into defense factories and are gathering their thirty-five and forty dollars a week. And some of those left are too dumb to come in out of the rain-much less bring a helpless child in with them. Hoskins was also with us and this time Ruth brought his special feeding dish. “I thought he'd feel more at home," said Ruth. “And here are a couple of cans of his favorite foodjust in case I should be delayed." No question of Hoskins feeling at home-with dish and food or without. 

He has already picked out his special corner of the living room rug to relax on. Reminds me of the time that 

Laurie arrived with Wing Foo so the children could enjoy his merry comradeship for a couple of weeks. He stayed with us four and a half years and would have been here yet if he hadn't tried to eat one too many 

Macy trucks. How I groaned when he arrived-and how I cried when he was gone. Dogs and children climb right inside my heart and never climb out. 

Chunky went to sleep in Peg's bed with Hoskins curled up at the foot. The "jewel" didn't arrive and 

Ruth was unavoidably detained- "You've no idea, my dear, the pressure we are under!" I wouldn't know, but

Chunky is at least among friends. He put his arms around me when I tucked him in, and his soft little cheek was pressed against mine. "Do all Mummies smell nice?" he asked. Who says children don't appreciate 60

the fragrance of bath powder and the little fussing you do. All Mummies should smell nice.

The house settled down to a serene quiet with Art and Jim in bed and asleep early for once. I'm a night owl but they are worse. For some reason I simply couldn't go to sleep and kept thinking of Peg. Sometimes my hunches are wonderful, and then again they aren't worth a cent. I lay there listening to the trains going over the trestle. One out and the next one coming back about ten minutes later after they passed on the double track at Norton. Jim is so smart he can tell by the sound which way they are going, but it's all the same to me. I counted three sets of them, which would make it about one o'clock. Suddenly I thought I heard the phone ring-with three closed doors in between it isn't always easy to be sure. But I kept on hearing it and slipped out of bed and into my robe and slippers. "Long Distance calling," said the operator in that voice with a smile which has about as much personality as a receptionist. "One minute-ple-uz." The minutes kept adding up. Every so often I would hear other operators chatting-"Contacting via Pottstown-are you ready, twenty-two-go ahead Alden City." Finally, when

I was about frantic, I heard Peg's voice. She was crying -and I couldn't put my arms around her. I hate all mechanical things and telephones most of all when anything really important is happening. "Mummy!" She hasn't called me that for years and my heart turned a complete flip-flop. "Mummy-are you there?" "Right here, darling," and I tried desperately to sound warm and close with fifty miles between us. 61

"Phil has his orders-most of the officers and men from his station are going." "Where?" I asked stupidly. I could hear her draw in her breath trying to hold on. "I don't know-the Navy doesn't tell them. But he has only four days' leave and we're going to get married." "Where, Peg?" "Down home-I want you and Dad with us. Say yes, Mummy-please say yes." Her voice broke and then went on "I love him so, Mummy-you and Dad can't know how much I love him." We only know twenty years' worth, twenty thousand times as much. 

"Come home, Peggy," I said. "We'll be here and ready." Only four days and we've had so much. Her nice young Navy man whom we thought was just an interlude and one of many who have beaued her around. No wonder she was moody and quiet at Christmas. How little we know of the people closest to us. I thought of that shriveled up little gypsy woman— “A wedding there will be-sudden and with tears-but with happiness." I wonder if she really knew? There are a lot of strange and unexplainable things in the world. 62

February 

A NEIGHBORHOOD WEDDING-FLOWERS FROM THE PALACE OF SWEETS-LAURIE DOES A ONE WOMAN BLITZ-WE GO HOUSE HUNTING IN CONNECTICUT AND MEET ALLEN-MAKE A FRIEND OF YOUR JUNK MAN-WE HAVE A SENTIMENTAL VALUE. 

February 1.—I was still in a daze this morning and wondering if I had dreamed it all-things happened so fast. 

Peg arrived at noon yesterday, Lieutenant Philip Andrews arrived at one, his brother officer at one-thirty, and at three o'clock the service was over and Phil and 

Peg waving good-by as they vanished down the hill in their car. How we could have managed without the help of all our friends and neighbors I'll never know. I called Beth right after I had talked with Peg and she must have spread the news because, the first thing in the morning, there was Lottie with the back of her car full of evergreen branches and sprays of barberry with the red berries hanging like strings of scarlet beadsshe can even make a barberry hedge rise to an occasion and look three times as beautiful as any you ever saw before. She trimmed the whole living room with green sprays along the mantel and behind the wall lights and huge blue-green pottery jars full of evergreen and Chinese lantern on either side of the fireplace. The jars she had lugged from her storeroom where they spend the winter in between garden seasons. She spread the dining-room table with her best lace cloth and fixed the centerpiece with white pottery figures and tall white 63

candles. More greenery along the windows so that our shelves, with the jeweled colors of the collection of glass we have, made a lovely picture. 

Beth phoned me that she was halfway through the making of her famous cocoanut-lemon jelly cake and would bring it over in plenty of time. That cake is something for angels to dream about! Clear golden yellow lemon jelly filling between six layers of snowy white cake and then the whole thing covered with slathers of fluffy boiled icing and showered with freshly grated cocoanut. Art says he isn't going to get married until he finds a girl who can make one just like it-at that rate he's going to be single a long, long time. When 

Beth hung up the phone there was a ring from Lauriethe grapevine certainly works in this town! "How many are coming?" she asked. "I've invited about twenty and there may be thirtywhy?" "Just figuring on the sandwiches-I'll have them ready by one o'clock and I'm making some of the rolled pecan and date ones that Peg likes." 

I suppose I should have said "You really mustn't" or something equally polite and insincere. What I really said was, "That's wonderful! And you're a darling." 

What a relief to have the sandwiches off my mind. 

Laurie is a peach about figuring what you need most and then pitching into the job. Marcia Burton made the punch and Ina Carson the salted almonds-hers are simply superlative-shiny and brown as though they had been shellacked and then dusted with the salt crystalsand the flavor makes you eat just one more when they they are already running out of your ears. The phone 64

rang again and George's brown velvet voice said, "Mrs. --this is me." How many years I have heard that voice when he arrived north in the spring bringing his old man from the warmer climate of the south where he toted him so tenderly and nursed him so devotedly until he died. George's love and loyalty to that sick white man, deserted by his own people, was as near real Christianity as I ever expect to see. "Yes, George," I said and waited, wondering if he had called to tell me that Claudine was indisposed or he was in one of his old-time desperate financial situations. "Me and Claudine we heerd Miss Peggy was going for to get herself married to a soldier man and we like to assist. Claudine have a nice clean uniform and me I got a butler suit offen a frien'. We both knows how to do things nice and right and we be right over 'bout 'leven o'clock." They were there on the minute, too, and worked like troopers. All of a sudden I thought of the ice cream. I had completely forgotten to order it and remembered that, ever since she was a little girl, Peg had talked about the ice cream flowers she was going to have at her wedding. I ran out to the kitchen and started for the phone. "The ice cream!" But Claudine smiled at me calmly. "Jes' res' yo'self, Miss Lady-the ice cream is settin' at the back stoop. The foreign man he done bring it, and he say they is all kinds of flowers like Miss Peggy always want."

I went out to the entry and there sat a huge wooden freezer packed to the top with cracked ice. A card was sticking up in the mound and on it was written-"With 65

good wishes and the compliments of your friends Tony and Company at the Palace of Sweets." I could have sat down and cried right then and there, but the mother of the bride just can't do little stunts like that on the morning of the wedding. The Palace of Sweets has been the foster mother of Peg and Art and their crowd ever since they were in grade school. Right through high and senior high they have gathered there to celebrate victory and forget defeat in the joys of banana splits and double chocolate sodas. Tony and his brother are two of the best friends they have ever had. Bankers when they needed a loan to tide them over, audience when they were bursting with news or indignation, shelter from a sudden rain or snow storm, and personal service bureau when messages were to be left or books or packages to be picked up. 

Tony and his brother came to the wedding, after I practically lost my voice persuading them, and I never saw two prouder men in my life when Peg came down the stairs on Jim's arm and smiled across the crowd at them. Everybody was there except the station master, I think, and everybody looked so happy and so proud.

Peg was lovely in her gray-blue suit with her soft, fair hair fluffing from under her tricky little hat. And Phil looked like something out of an illustration for a love story of wartime romance. It was a beautiful weddingand I didn't cry-which I was determined not to do and was dead sure I would.

And now it was Sunday and everything seemed so still and empty as though nothing ever would or could happen again. Along in the afternoon Bruce Stevens came in, and he and Jim sat down with their pipes while 66

I cleared up the dinner things out in the kitchen. Bruce had a day off which, in his doctor language, means any free time beyond half an hour. "No babies on the way until next month, no old lady with a bad heart, no cars out to speak of because the roads are too slippery so there won't be any accident cases-boy what a life!”

Along about four Laurie came in to tell me about the new draperies she has ordered for her living room. 

She's all moved in but still has a lot of extras to finish up. A house is like a child-you keep mothering it and fussing over it until you get so attached that you can hardly remember the time when it wasn't a part of your life. Laurie looked lovely, with her cheeks pink from excitement and the cold air and her bronzy hair ruffled up by the wind. I can remember the time when Jim used to quote, "Even the hairs of her head are numbered." They did look so precise and mathematical and everything about her was done according to rule. Five years ago she looked ten years older than her age, and now she looks ten years younger. The right clothes and the right hair-do certainly make a world of difference --all she needs now is the right man-but that's not so easy. She certainly doesn't do anything to help herself along that line.

She was jabbering along about the curtains and the window seat where the youngsters could come and curl up to listen to stories and eat cookies. But when we got inside the house and she saw Bruce she shut up as tight as a head of iceberg lettuce. "Good afternoon, Miss Pratt," said Bruce. "Good afternoon, Doctor Stevens." All nice and cozy 67

like a young ladies' finishing school on visiting day. I could have shaken the two of them and wish I had. "Come on in," said Jim, "and listen to what the Doctor is telling me about a new treatment they've found for kids with nervous troubles-he's hipped on kids just the way you are."

I sat there as though I were watching the characters in a play. Bruce so intent and serious that I don't believe he realized the rest of us were alive, Laurie taking in every word but saying nothing. "Someday," and he said it like a little boy telling about a dream he had which he knew in his heart could never come true, "someday I'll have my own sanitarium and can do these things the way I believe they should be done. I'll hire nurses who love children more than they do their damned system and efficiency and can mother them back to normalcy-that's what most of them need instead of something out of a bottle."

We talked about everything under the sun after that, and, finally, Laurie and I went out to the kitchen to fix coffee and get out a plate of cookies. Bruce wandered out and went straight for the spice cabinet as he always does. One jar after another he opened and sniffed while he draped himself across a corner of the table. He always reminds me of an English sheep dog somewaysort of rangy and friendly and yet with a lot of sense and responsibility.

The coffee was perking away with that perkling sound that is so soothing and the fragrance of it filled the air. Laurie fixed the tray with the precise, well-ordered way she does everything. Suddenly she stopped and stood absolutely still. 68

"It's Chunky!" I hear him crying. Before the rest of us heard a thing she was through the dining room and we saw her run out the front door. We followed and saw a strange sight. Coming down the front steps, from the upper terrace, was Chunky, crying in choky kind of sobs and holding one arm with his other hand. 

Grabbing onto his shoulder was the stupidest and the angriest looking woman I have ever seen. She had a doughy kind of face unleavened by any trace of kindness or intelligence. She kept shouting, over and over, "You are bad! You are a bad boy!" "Let go of him," called Bruce. "The boy's hurt." 

Her only answer was another shake and shout. Laurie was up those steps in nothing flat. Her timid, apologetic manner was gone as completely as though it had never existed. She grabbed the woman's arm, bent it back until she let go her hold on Chunky and gave a yell of pain into the bargain. To finish the job she gave the woman a shove that sent her backward until she sat down on the icy step, all spread out like an underdone biscuit. Chunky stood there for a half-second until 

Laurie held out her arms. "Come to me, darling, I'll take care of you." He cuddled close to her and buried his head on her shoulder. 

Bruce pulled that lump of a woman to her feet and glared at her. “Who are you-and what happened?” "I am the maid by Mrs. Voods—and tomorrow I am already leaving. That boy! He is bad and he makes all the time trouble. Off the shed roof he falls and breaks himself. He say he is a Captain on a boat-and I say he is no good-just bad!" "Skip it! And get out of here." He took Chunky from 69

Laurie's arms and carried him into the house. "Now, let's have a look and see what you've done to yourself.

Get me that black bag from the hall bench, Miss Pratt, and put on your coat. We'll probably have to take him down to the office and my nurse is off for the afternoon. 

I'll x-ray this elbow. Think it's a dislocation but it might be a fracture-so swollen I can't tell by feel." 

Laurie was trembling so that she simply couldn't find the armhole when Jim held her coat for her. Bruce stood up and gave one look. "For the love of Mike stop that shaking and get going. And button up your coat-it's cold as the devil outside!" He is the best doctor we have ever had the good fortune to own-but he certainly has not the bedside manner. Queerly enough, Laurie straightened herself out, stopped trembling, and smiled --and her smile is a lovely thing. I've long believed that every normal woman has a secret craving for some man to boss her around and make her like it. There she stood, calm as you please, and happier looking than I have ever seen her. "Ready, Doctor," she said, as though she had been taking orders from him all her life and enjoying it. 

After they were gone I turned to Jim. I was so upset and sizzling mad over that dumb woman and that adorable Chunky. "Go on-get it off your mind." He always knows what I think and how I feel before I know myself.

Someway I had to figure this thing out. Ruth Woods was up to her eyebrows in war work and, I must honestly admit, doing a good and important job-a job that 

I would be no earthly good at. Here was I, born domestic, born maternal, and my own family outgrowing the 70

need for either home or mother. Someway the answer had to be found. I couldn't bear to think of Chunky, a war orphan if there ever was one, being brought up by a procession of maids. I kept thinking of that little boy in the story by Kenneth Grahame-the one who said, "And then I went back to the house where I never did anything right." What unhappy hours and years were in that simple statement. 

February 2.-The sun was shining through the windows over the sink while I was washing the breakfast dishes this morning, and I watched something so simple and so beautiful. The thoughts of rising living costs, the headlines of reverses and dissensions, the announcement that a fifty-nine-billion-dollar war budget is just a beginning left my mind for a few minutes and I felt the wonderment of a child looking at its first rainbow. I had taken down the amber-colored apricot brandy bottle-Jim and I had picked it up on one of our junk shop adventures-in order to wash it and put in fresh water for the sprays of ivy. I emptied it and poked it down in the dish pan to fill up with soapy water. Bubbles of suds began to form until, one after another, they formed a perfect, geometrical pattern which caught the sun and threw off rainbow lights. The whole top of the water was a mosaic of changing shades. I don't know why it made me so happy, except, perhaps, that it seemed rather like an assurance that there is a plan that, underneath the confusion and uncertainty, there are laws and system that will someday get a chance to function again. 

Long after the kitchen was clean and in order I still thought of it. Someway the war has changed values for most of us. The little things seem so much more im- 71

portant-the big things so much less so. During the past few years when Jim's income has been on the way up after the depression years, we started to plan again, and investments, new things for the house, a better car, advancement in business were what we worked toward and wanted most. Now what we hope for and work for is content in our own four walls, our love for each other and the children, a treasuring of every small thing that goes to make even a temporary happiness. We are all part of something so much bigger, so much more vital, than personal gain. The words of the Mayor in The 

Moon Is Down come to my mind so often: "There must be a spark in little men that can burst into flame. I am afraid, I am terribly afraid, but at the same time I feel a kind of exultation, as though I were bigger and better than I am." What a piece of writing Steinbeck did in that book. Phrases and scenes stay with you long after you've read it. No Greek drama ever packed into a scene such tension and foreboding as the paragraph where Molly heard the tapping on the door in the blackness of the night. She blew out the light of the lamp. "Her voice was strained and sweet. She called, 'I'm coming, Lieutenant, I'm coming!" "

February 4.-Came back from the First Aid Class to day to be met halfway up the road by Artie who was shouting and waving both arms. At first I couldn't make out a word he was saying and, of course, feared the worst. Why do we always think something awful has happened when it quite as often is something good? At last I understood him. "My teeth are O.K.-they're going to take me! Isn't that swell?" It was something awful-and something 72

good. He is so happy and excited and Jim proud and relieved things have worked out the way Art wanted them. I'm proud, too, and happy for him, but it's going to take a lot of pantry cleaning and digging into war work to keep my chin where it belongs as the mother of a flier. 

February 7.-Jim phoned me from the office this morning and asked if I wouldn't like to go house hunting over in Connecticut. I had nine million things to do but none of them I'd like to do half as much. We have house hunted in the highways and byways for years.

Saved thousands of dollars by not buying the places didn't buy. Jim says as a financier I'm a grand cook. 

This house of ours has been perfect but now it seems about as cozy as the Metropolitan on an off day. Six bedrooms and three baths available and one bedroom and one bath being used after Art goes. Peg will be home once in awhile but her last letter doesn't sound as though she were any too sure what she'll do next.

She talks of finishing this year at college and then entering nurse's training if Phil is sent out of the country. 

She still doesn't know where he is since he left at the end of the four days' leave. These youngsters have spunk and try to keep their heads and plan things reasonably far better than we did. 

I met Jim with the car at twelve and, by some miracle, had no adventure worth thinking about. I did try to go over the upper level of the bridge the way I always do when I drive in for shopping. Completely forgot that the traffic reverses after eleven. So there stood as large a cop as I ever expect to see. 73

"Where," he demanded, "do you think you're a-goin'?" 

I still didn't catch on. "Why, into New York-I always go this way." "Not with me on duty you don't. I niver yell at lady motorists-makes 'em nervous. But lady or no lady you obey the law. The upper level is closed to ingoin' traffic after eleven A.M. You just turn yourself around and go the lower level like it was intended." 

By that time I was so flustered that, having no geographical sense anyway, I started in the wrong direction. 

My friend shouted after me and walked up beside the car. "Now listen to me careful. Turn right. The big thing down there is the bridge and you drive over it. Keep your eyes on what you're doin' or I'll have to be fishin' you out of the river entoirely. Did you have a fight with your old man-or what? Now turn right and watch what you're doin' and keep your mind on it.”

When I met Jim and he took over the driving everything went just fine. We got on all the right roads and over the right bridges-in the right direction. He has an uncanny sense for anything in the wide open spaces, but turn him loose in a closet or a dresser drawer and everything hides from him and he has to send out an S.O.S. for me. I have to remember our varied talents or I'd certainly get an inferiority complex. We stopped at one of these log cabin roadside stands for lunch and had wonderful hamburgers. Perhaps it was the cold, crisp air and perhaps it was the company-Jim and I always have the best time ever when we manage to get off by our lones-but these hamburgers were certainly 74

something! They had finely chopped onion and parsley mixed with the seasonings in the meat and right in the middle of them a chunk of sharp cheese had been tucked so that it made a smooth, tangy pool when they were cooked. "My wife Mattie made those up," said the boss proudly. "She used to be secretary to a fat old guy name of Howard. Couldn't fry an egg right side up she couldn't when we got hitched. Now she can cook circles around me-and don't she know it! Anyway, she likes it better than punching a clock night and morning and saying, 'Yes, Mr. Howard,' all day long. Mattie's smart as they make 'em and good lookin' too." He turned around and bellowed into the rear room. His voice shook the beams in the ceiling. "Sugar-pie-come out here and meet some customers. They like your hamburgers." Just before she appeared he explained his subtle manner. “Gotta be soft spoken and gentle with 

Mattie-she's easy upset like all the ladies." 

Mattie and I swapped recipes. Jim and her Eddie talked war and politics and what on earth those dumb bunnies in Washington think they're doing and isn't 

MacArthur swell and where in heck is the navy. We parted the best of friends and I promised to let Mattie know if we found our little house. I wonder if we ever will!

We'd brought along a couple of pages of real estate ads so we could look up some agents. I love the real estate section of the paper and I love the realtors. No matter what is happening everywhere else here you'll find the greatest of cheer-the trend is upward! First port of call was Mae Elton Andrews-Estates and Ren- 75

tals. Miss Andrews had a breathless personality which oozed enthusiasm. We told her we wanted a small, simple place with a little land, an apple tree, a hill, a fireplace and within commuting distance. She pulled down her very tweedy tweed skirt, popped a hat on her head, tucked in a few stray curls of red hair, and called her dog. "Allen goes everywhere with me and he knows the houses as well as I do. We'll fit into the front seat all nice and cozy and Allen can sit in the back seat-it's his favorite place anyway." She beamed at me and collected Jim in the circuit of her smile. "You both like dogs? I always say that people who like the country like dogs. You'll love Allen." We did-by necessity. His rear portions occupied the rear seat, but his breath was down our necks, his long, friendly tongue took us in from hats to shoulders. He was the most enormous 

Great Dane I have ever met. Beside him Hoskins would look like a Pekinese and a puny one at that. Up hill and down dale we rode over really superior bumps. "Just wait," said Miss Andrews, "until you see what 

I have to show you! The place, my dear, is simply reeking with charm-definitely reeking!" "Seems a little off the beaten track," remarked Jim. 

But this didn't discourage her. "One wants vision to appreciate this place. Two artists owned it-they put their very souls into that garden. 

Of course, you can't tell about the garden at this time of year-but in the spring! A riot, a veritable riot of blossoms."

With one last unearthly bump we came to a dead stop and there stood the house. Allen leaped out and began 76

to run in circles like a calf with mosquito bites. Miss

Andrews tipped her hat over one eye, gave another tug to her skirt, and announced triumphantly, "Voilà! The place of your dreams! A happy house!"

We crawled in through a cellar window. She had forgotten the key. We shivered in the cold damp of a place which I am sure had not been opened to the sun and air for many, many moons. There were murals on the walls, landscapes on the ceilings, the kitchen was definitely Mexican, the living room French, the dining alcove modernistic with pictures of keys hanging on nothing, pictures of clocks sitting on nothing, a painted hook with a painted overcoat pendant. There were steps up and steps down from every room into every other room and over it all hung the cheery atmosphere of a Frankenstein movie. Miss Andrews led the way and kept repeating, "Reeking with charm, my dear! 

Isn't it different?" 

Jim broke in practically with, "Where's the heating system?" She was visibly wounded. "Oh, the heating system? Artists never think of such things-but it would be a simple matter to put one in you know. And you should see the garden in the spring!

All sorts of little bulby things peeking through the grass and the apple orchard is a dream!"

There was a loud crash from what the English refer to as "above stairs." Allen came leaping down and bumped into the newel post, which was painted a vivid magenta in contrast to the jade green rail, and took the top ball of it along with him. Chasing it over the bare floor was a wonderful new game and we jumped from side to side as he progressed. How we ever got out of 77

there unmaimed shall always remain a mystery. We got into the car and nobody said a word until we bumped down the hill again. Then Miss Andrews broke the silence. "You don't like it. I can always tell when my clients fail to respond-of course, I realize it isn't a house everyone would vibrate to, but I did hope you would feel its appeal. Now I have another little place-definitely yours." "Some other time," said Jim firmly.

We went to search out a gentleman realtor-it seemed restful to think of a mere man who wouldn't talk in exclamation points and cart along a dog named Allen. 

We found him. "George Bates," the sign said. "Houses, farms, sales and rentals." George was restful. So much so that Jim had to practically pry him out of the house and into the car. He was eating peanuts with a manner of thorough detachment. Jim showed him the ad, "House with a view. Modern conveniences. Brook, apple orchard, studio at rear. Must be seen to be appreciated." "Yep," said George, still eating peanuts. "That's the 

Morton place. Road not too good but the next meeting of the town board will fix that up. House needs a little working on here and there, but you folks would enjoy tinkering with your own property." We were practically moved in and settled as far as he was concerned. 

As an afterthought he added, "Nice place for chickens, too-in case you're thinking of chickens." We weren't but let that pass.

We started down the rutted dirt road and began to gather neighborhood history. "The dimensions of some 78

of these folks I couldn't explain. Fella down here a ways collects wooden boxes-all kinds and sorts-got two fields full of 'em. Nobody knows why and maybe he don't. Woman up the road a piece has got her barn and yard full of bottles. They ain't antiques and she ain't a junk dealer-just collects bottles. Pays the kids around here a cent a piece for 'em and just piles them up inside and out. Man in the next house to hers eats sardines and drinks beer-morning, noon, and night that's all the vittles he eats. Think he'd get kind of fed up with the same things all the time, now wouldn't you?

But live and let live is my motto-if it suits him it suits me. Nice neighborhood, though, and good views. The 

Morton place is in an ideal spot. Up and away, so to speak, and yet handy."

We finally reached it after winding through a woods road, over a stretch of gravel, and up another hill. "Here we are!" said George. And there we were. Nothing, since the stage setting for Tobacco Road, has ever equaled it. A house gray with fatigue and neglect leaning on one elbow in its last gasp. A yard full of gravel, with patches of half-melted snow exposing the bones of its bareness, one solitary sycamore tree standing gaunt and mottled against the winter sky. We didn't go in. 

We didn't get out of the car. "It won't do," said Jim. "Some other time."

Home never looked so good as when we pulled into the drive at six o'clock. Art had turned up the oil burner and every room was heavenly and warm. I always know that, when I am so completely comfortable I could purr, someone else in the family is going to say, "This place is like an oven! Do you know the thermometer 79

is eighty?" and I always say, "Yes, isn't it wonderful!"

Made a large pot of Jim's favorite potato soup with chunks of potato and transparent slivers of onion in its creamy depths and lots and lots of crisp bacon added at the last minute. It is as satisfying a dish as I know, and the apple Brown Betty, sweetened with maple syrup and topped with chopped walnuts made a finish that was grand. There are so many things like that which are easy to make early in the day and then finish up in the oven at night. With the can shortage coming on

I don't know how long I can buy the canned apples but 

I'll figure out some other way when those are gone.

Certainly there is no danger of my mind going to seed -too many new problems all the time. Sort of an adventure in seeing how clever and adaptable you can be.

Too bad it seems to take the upheaval of war to jolt most of us out of an easy-going habit of taking the easiest way. 

February 10.-Went to a salvage meeting this morning and had the grandest laugh I've had in a long time. 

All the best people were there rigged out in their best country tweeds with the proper shoes, gloves, lisle sports stockings that cost two-fifty a pair. Lottie was Chairman and ran it, the way she does everything, with complete competence. Two guest speakers were on from Washington to explain what is needed, how it is to be collected and how everyone can help. The woman was a whiz and I certainly admired her. Smart looking, quick thinking, and decisive. At one point she was explaining that many things were best collected by the regular junk dealer. 80

"Make a friend of your junk man, because he is most important in the war effort.”

From one of the ringside seats a lady arose who never picked up her Scotch homespun outfit on Fourteenth Street. She must be new out here for I never saw her before, but she is certainly somebody and doesn't care who knows it. With a most Duchessy manner and vurry, vurry English accent she announced: 

"Madam Chairman-I am Mrs. A. Norton Melrose. I 

wish to question the speaker." Lottie gave her one of those funny looks she reserves for Japanese beetles and such. But the lady was not sensitive. She went right on. "Don't you think-that in most communities of this type and income level-that one hardly knows one's junk dealer-ah-socially? It seems to me that, as a class" --and she pronounced it with an extremely broad A—– "they are of a rawther low order and apt to cheat oneif permitted."

For a second there was a total silence and then a general community laugh started the rounds until it spread all over the crowd. When it had died down the lady from Washington did as neat a job of what Jim would call "pinning her ears back" as I have ever witnessed. "Mrs. Melrose, if everyone in the United States were giving the Government the whole-hearted co-operation we are receiving from the junk dealers our job would be much simpler. They are working far harder than normal, much longer hours, and at extremely little financial benefit to themselves. The junk must be sorted and delivered to the proper sources before it is of any possible use. I repeat, 'Make a friend of your junk man."" 

I only hope if a bomb does land in Oakdale that Mrs. 81

A. Norton Melrose will be right there and have to crawl under old Henry's horse for shelter. I get so everlasting mad at people who put on such airs and then talk about Democracy as though they invented it and had taken out a patent. I only hope Bruce Stevens gets her for a patient. He has a genius for insulting people like that. 

And he does it in such a nice way they don't wake up to the fact until' hours later. Sort of like The New Yorker. 

February 13.-Even though it is Friday the thirteenth the great moment of my life arrived this morning. For nearly twenty years I have been waiting for it and almost gave up hope. All that time I have been sending for service men for the vacuum, washing machine, sewing machine, and the rest of what the advertisements called "Your silent and tireless servants." They were silent, all right, when something went wrong, and I would stand there with a baffled expression and poke this and pull that with the result that nothing happened. 

Then I would call the service man-as near to you as your phone. There have been a whole procession of them and they all had a helper named Bill and all started off with the same theme song.

First the boss would look over the particular mechanical demon I'd called him for-"I think it's a gasket," I would suggest timidly-that being the first technical term that popped into my head. But the boss failed to respond. He was deep in thought and troubled beyond words. Finally he would turn to Bill. "Bill, take a look at this thing and see if you agree with me." Bill always did. "Looks to me as though the motor is shot, the bearings seem to be burned out, and 1 1 1 1 82

the center shaft is corroded." Then he'd turn to me and go into his song and dance. "Frankly, Madam, I wouldn't spend any money on this piece of equipment -wouldn't last you another six weeks. Just throwing your money away. You'd do far better to turn it in on one of our new models. You'd be surprised the improvements they've made." Then he'd get down on his knees the better to view the beast. "Hm-this is the model turned out in the fall of twenty-five." Sometimes it was the spring of something else. Models seemed to have good years and bad like certain vintages of wine. "That year the design was faulty-not enough copper used in the mixture. We've had a lot of trouble with this model. Nearly all of our customers have taken our advice and turned them interms are very easy, Madam, you'd never miss the payments and you'd have a brand new, up-to-date piece of equipment that would ease your work a hundred per cent."

When I insisted on the antique, of three to five years of age, being repaired, the boss would look at me more in sorrow than in anger. "We'll put it in as good order as possible, but I'm telling you frankly you're making a big mistake." It was never just a mistake or a slight mistake-it was always a big one. 

But today, Friday, February the thirteenth, it was different. The boss arrived. There was no Bill or anything resembling him. "Your helper?" I asked brightly.

It hardly seemed legal without one. "Gone into a Defense factory. You can't get a kid for love or money. They can pull down their thirty-five 83

a week and what we can pay them is just peanuts. Now where's this washing machine?" 

We trailed down cellar and I said firmly, "You'll have to fix it someway. I can't buy a new one-our money's going into Defense bonds and taxes." 

He stopped dead in his tracks and looked at me as though the Home for the Feeble Minded were missing an inmate. "Certainly I can fix it and go on fixing it for the duration. You can't buy a new one because-whywe haven't any to sell you. Our showroom is cleaned out and our factory is running twenty-four hours a day on Government jobs. You ladies will have to learn to take care of things and not mess them up all the timethe good old days are over." "Hallelujah!” I said, and he never even heard me.

February 14.-Jim and I made the rounds of the realtors this afternoon and definitely put the house on the market. We explained, over and over, how roomy it is, how well it heats, what an excellent location it has, and that the only reason we were selling is that the family had outgrown it. Three of them promised to come over tomorrow and inspect. In the meantime we are listed. 

The real estate section of the paper is full of interviews with the leading realtors as to the great demand for the older, larger houses. "The trend is upward in larger residential property as no more can be built and priorities are also cutting into the small house construction except in Defense areas. The large house owner is at last coming into his own and stands an excellent chance of making a generous increase on his investment." 

On the strength of this we looked around at a few apartments-just in case. After the room we've had they 84

do seem awfully boxy and cramped. What they call a view usually consists of the visible branch of one tree or a vista down the drive to the Service Entrance. Some had doormen and elevator boys. 

"Count them in your budget," said Jim. He always thinks of things that never enter my head until it is too late to do anything about them. There would be Christmas with an artificial tree in the lobby and all the front door and the back door staff to be remembered. I thought of that picture in The New Yorker-how I do enjoy that magazine-the plump dowager trying to climb into an elevator which was three feet above the floor. She turns to her husband and says, "I told you we should have given him something for Christmas.'

Jim is right, we'd have to figure tips in our budget. I love to give people presents-something perfectly luxurious that they want terribly and don't need at all. But 

I hate giving tips! It seems so snobbish and impersonal and puts the tip receiver in the undignified position of being thrown crusts from the master's table. Silly to feel this way, I suppose, but human beings have a right to human dignity and should be paid a living wage for honest, hard work. Reminds me of the housekeeper,

Mrs. Haskell, in Windswept. Nobody was going to give her extra money for anything. She did her job and she was paid for her job and that was the end of that. Mary 

Ellen Chase drew a real character and a stimulating one in that woman. I'd like to have known her as a friend and labored with her on any job. She belonged in the windswept cleanness of Maine, but she would have fitted in anywhere that she was really needed-a grand woman! 85

We looked over four apartments and I didn't really like any of them. I kept thinking where would we put 

Art's friends when he has leave and brings them home for a raid on the refrigerator? Where would we tuck 

Joan when she comes back with Peg to jabber into the early hours of the morning and sort of blossom out into the girl she was intended to be if she'd been lucky enough to have a family with some sense? Where would Chunky play when he stays with us? And what on earth would we do with Hoskins? He would hate the three and a half rooms-with bath. No trees to poke under looking for our one brown rabbit that comes every spring in time to eat the blue hyacinth bulbs, no road to growl at when strange feet have the audacity to walk on it, no anything that a real dog could be bothered with. Chunky and Hoskins would loathe an apartment. Maybe we would too but the kitchens almost redeemed them. Everything so shiny new and convenient. I began putting the bright pottery on the shelves and designing tricky curtains of yellow calico with peasant colors in the tape borders. Perhaps the spicy smell of gingerbread would seep through the other rooms and make them seem like home-perhaps. 

February 15.-Up and at 'em bright and early this morning to get the house in order for the visiting realtors. I really am a fairly good housekeeper most of the time, but, when it comes to having perfect strangers poking into closets and opening cupboard and pantry doors, it's something else again. I kept thinking, in every room, what things we would want to take with us and what things we would give away or keep for that problematical future when Peg or Art might find use for 86

them in their own homes. We couldn't possibly put the contents of a ten-room house into a four-room apartment-even my mathematical sense can see that. "Jim!" I'd call from the muffling folds of the clothes in our closet. "Do you think we'd better take that extra cherry chest in the guest room?" "Can't hear you." Then I'd stick my head out of the door and repeat. Jim and the youngsters say my most maddening habit, and I have plenty of them, is the way 

I start talking when I'm running the washing machine in the basement or digging into a trunk in the attic. But my mind seems to function best when I'm surrounded with plenty of work for my hands. I always get annoyed when the family doesn't click and answer my question and they always get annoyed because I imagine that an answer is possible. This has been going on for years with no sign of improvement. Families are like that and perhaps it's what makes them individual. 

A well organized family, which would run smoothly and efficiently at all times, would be about as human as a file cabinet with a competent executive in charge. 

Not a chance with us.

Finally Jim gave up attempting to get the semaphore system of pieces of what I was saying and came upstairs. "Now, what was it you asked me?” "Shall we take the extra cherry chest in the guest room?" "Take it? Where?" For a bright man Jim can ask the dumbest questions. "To the apartment, of course! Will we have room 87

for it, or do you think we should put it in storage for 

Peg when she and Phil start housekeeping." "You and Napoleon," said Jim and laughed at me the way he does when I'm sort of young and highly amusing. "What's Napoleon got to do with it?” "His genius lay in the fact that he had so much futurity in his mind--he was seven jumps ahead of everyone else-read that in a book." Just the same I like to plan ahead so that I know what I'm going to do next. Only 

I didn't have the decision in my own hands. The realtors did that.

The first one came about ten and went through the house from top to bottom and bottom to top. Jim pointed out to him the clever way the plumbing is arranged for each bathroom so that all you have to do is shut off the one set of pipes. Then you open a panel in the hall and everything is right there. Not that I have ever seen it make any difference to the plumbers we've called in. They always discover that, "The trouble, Madam, is somewhere under the floor or back of that there tiled wall." But it is a good talking point. 

The realtor said, "I see," in as cold and impersonal a manner as is possible to get into the human voice. 

When we came to the kitchen I proudly showed what marvelous storage space we have, with plenty of room for a good supply of canned goods, and a shelf over the refrigerator for laundry supplies, and a cleaning closet for the brooms and mops and such. The realtor said, "I see." When he was all through with inspecting, and the Gestapo couldn't have done a more thorough job, he turned to Jim and suggested, in a manner that 88

definitely got the message across to me that if I were to go elsewhere it would be perfectly all right with him, "You and I had better sit down and talk this thing over." It sounded ominous-and was. "As I gather," Jim explained to me after the gentleman was gone, "we are living in a shack. It is hardly fit for human habitation but we're used to it. The house is outmoded, it is not air-conditioned, there are Venetian blinds in the living room only, the kitchen lacks the stream-lined cupboards and continuous working surfaces that are now demanded-" I broke in- "Didn't he say anything nice about our house at all?" Jim grinned and tucked me inside the curve of his arm. "Lady-our house has sentimental value. That he freely admitted and laid it on thick. It has sheltered us from wind and storm, it has seen the birth of our little ones now grown to manhood and womanhood, in our minds it carries the happy memories of holidays and family life. In short, it has sentimental value-but, on the market, it is worth less than nothing." "But what about the articles in the real estate section?" I managed to break in. "They say the old, big houses are in demand and people are rushing to buy them. What about that?" "That is known as 'the publicity game.' They're talking about some other houses-not ours. 

The next Inspector General was much the same-only more cheerful in his manner. When he finished his tour he turned to both of us-apparently he figured I was now a big girl and could stand things better. The other one tried to shield me. "You folks want me to be perfectly frank, don't 89

you?" We did. "Well, my honest advice is that you'd do a whole lot better to stay right where you are. Sales on the big places are very slow. People don't appreciate good construction-they want the latest thing in ventilating fans in the kitchen and air conditioning-not worth a damn, pardoning me, in my opinion-but that's what they want. Rentals are better, but if you start to rent you'll have to re-decorate from top to bottom and paint the outside trim on the house-cost you four or five hundred dollars. The way it is you folks are used to it, but new people coming in are awful choosy. Then you rent, in times like these, and you get a man in Service. He gets shifted halfway across the country and where are you? You can't hold him to a lease and you have to do some re-decorating before another renter comes in-no profit in it and a lot of headaches." He gave me a smile that was sort of a pat on the head to a child who has just been told that, "We can't have the picnic today, dear, because it's raining"-a soothing kind of smile. "I realize that this place has great sentimental value to you and your little family but as marketable property it's a flat tire-maybe another year things will be different. But for now I'd say stay where you are and sit tight. You've got a nice, comfortable place here and an apartment would seem awfully cramped and kind of impersonal. This place has definite sentimental value-to your family." I guess there won't be any need for me to worry about what to do with the cherry chest. It can stay right where it is-and so can we. But what in the world are we going to do with all this space? "Shut off some of the rooms," says Jim.

But I hate shut off rooms-they're so gloomy and de- Thandi bat 90

pressing. They remind me of the place they always discover the body in the mystery books that Jim and 

Art are so fond of.

The three of us went to the movies tonight to see 

Ginger Rogers in Tom, Dick, and Harry. Exactly the antidote I needed to pull me out of the gloops. Jim and 

Art didn't need to be pulled out for they weren't in them-at least not for the same reason I was. They're full of high spirits over the fact that, apparently for the duration, we're going to live in this house. "I didn't know you were so upset over the idea of an apartment," I said to Artie. "Well-it was up to you and Dad-I won't be around much anyway-but I kind of like to think it's here and my room is in the same mess as usual. Peg will sure be glad, too-she was awfully down.”

We have sentimental value-the house, Jim, and I. 

The wear and tear of years has softened all of us and made us closer and more part of each other. Cracks in the plaster, stains on the wallpaper, a kitchen that is not stream-lined with continuous working space, faults in Jim, faults in me, faults in the children, and we love each other for them. 

February 16.-On the way to surgical dressings class this morning I stopped in at Reid's and bought a set of bright pottery mixing bowls-one jade green, one golden yellow, one claret red. Also bought a set of six tea towels made out of flour sacking and dyed in vivid peasant colors and all six for seventy-nine cents. Our nice roomy kitchen is going on at the same old stand but I'm going to fix it up to be gayer. It will be a little at a time as I can manage the money. And Peg's old 91

yellow calico print housecoat is going to furnish the valances for the curtains. It is yards around and she never wears it any more. Be nice to have curtains from the first grown-up housecoat Peg ever owned. I can still see her swishing it around as she walked and then going into a whirl to see how far the skirt would swing-when she thought nobody was looking. Art called her the 

Duchess when she swished and the "Oomph" Girl when she whirled. The curtains will have some more of that sentimental value-and I'll love them. 

February 18.-Noticed in the paper this morning that

Albert Payson Terhune had died and wondered if all the dogs that he has made deathless in his stories would be waiting for him on the other side. I don't see any reason why the heart and love and loyalty of a fine dog shouldn't have a place in the next world if it is a world of the spirit and I don't see what else it can be. 

February 19.-Laurie Pratt-how that name Laurie seems to fit her and bring out qualities I never saw before-came in this afternoon and there were stars in her eyes. She had on a new hat that was the most becoming thing I ever saw. Where she got the ones she used to wear is more than I can figure, but Bruce was right when he said they made her look ninety. She sat down on the davenport and actually tucked one foot under her. Her bag dropped on the floor and she went right on talking as though it didn't matter in the least. 

It didn't really, of course, but she used to be such a precise little fuss budget that every one of her belongings had its neat and allotted place in the world-and the floor was not one of them. She leaned forward and 92

was positively eager. She has the bluest eyes when she's happy and excited. "I've signed up for the Nurse's Aid. Dr. Stevens said it would do me a lot of good to see other people's troubles for a change and that I had the makings of a crackerjack nurse. I don't know why he thinks so because the only time I ever helped him was when Chunky hurt his arm." "Bruce knows a whole lot of things that aren't in the books." But I looked at her closely. She is so exquisitely dainty about everything she does and wears that I simply couldn't imagine her in connection with bed pans and the bathing of every sort and kind of person. She's never bumped into anything very real in her life and nursing doesn't soften the seamy side one bit. "Do you really think you'll like it?" I might just as well have saved my doubts for her face lighted up like a Christmas tree. "Like it? I'll love it! Think of being important and needed for the first time in my life. Perhaps I'll get work in a children's ward and can do things for them and bring things to them. Perhaps some of them will even love me and want me to come back every day. I'm talking like a gushy schoolgirl, I know, but I never was anybody before and now's my chance. I feel so ill at ease with most grown people, but with children I can be myself." 

I remembered so well the time, when Peg was little, that Laurie had said to me, "Let her be like other girls -don't keep her away from the world and real living. 

I never had a chance to make real friends until last winter when I went to that cooking class and got to 93

know people for the first time. It's lonely being different, because you're shut in by money and other people's ideas." I wonder if the advice of the father in Rachel 

Field's And Now Tomorrow wasn't all wrong? He said to his daughter, "Always do what other people expect of you.

It saves trouble in the end. I never learned the trick." Anyway, it was something like that and it seems to me that more often than not that principle leads to trouble. You lose yourself and you wind up by not suiting the other people anyway. 

February 23.-Fixed up the kitchen this morning with the new curtains and hung up a red and a blue towel- "just for pretty" as Claudine says. Hung the cups on the hooks so that the red and yellow and turquoise and green were arranged to look their gayest. The kettle on the stove was purring so that I could start the cracked wheat for breakfast. I like to cook it long and slowly so that the flavor of the sun and wind and rain that grew it comes out in its full richness. Everything seemed so peaceful and lovely and the headlines in the paper such a horrible contrast. "Battle raging in the jungles of Bataan and lush fields of the Netherlands Indies." 

Men dying and suffering and struggling and I was hanging cups on hooks and thinking about breakfast cereal. 

And Artie going to heaven knows where or what. I have no right to be so happy over simple, homey things. 

Yet what is it all about if those small things don't endure and homes go on being homes and havens of peace and love and understanding? I've got to go on caring about doing my job and helping in whatever way I'm needed-saving in little ways that big ways may be ac- 94

complished, keeping what courage and cheerfulness I can manage. I only hope I'm equal to it. 

February 24.-If I had a dollar for every confidential, right-off-the-boat piece of information that comes my way these days I'd be a rich woman. The latest is that there is going to be a terrible shortage of toilet paper! 

Women were down at the market this morning buying every roll they could lay their hands on—some beautiful friendships went to pieces before my eyes. The veneer of ladyhood is very thin sometimes and cracks at the slightest strain. Lottie remarked about one neighbor, "I always thought she'd act like a fishwife when she let herself go-and she did." What I can't figure out is, if there is a shortage of anything really necessary, how anyone with an atom of regard for their neighbors could enjoy having any when the rest had none. But look at the black markets in Europe! At the same time you'll find utter selfishness and complete self-sacrifice in the same person if the need arises for help in an emergency that endangers life. What is that remark the funny man in the movies is always making-"Monkeys is the craziest peoples!" Something like that. 

Made some cracked wheat pudding this afternoon.

Jim and Art love it and I get sort of a quiet satisfaction out of seeing so much good nourishment go down them in a way they enjoy. Grand way to use up the leftover breakfast cereal, and with the milk and eggs and raisins and spices it really is good. I sneaked another egg into the hard sauce, too. Makes it look richer, uses less butter and sneaks another egg, or at least part of one, into my menfolks-both of them shy away from just eggs and both of them need them. Jim is working harder and 95

longer hours each week, I believe. No more long, quiet evenings for us with talking and reading our pet passages from our favorite books. He comes home late and leaves early and Sunday is just another day with more hours most of the time. I hope Art's orders come through soon because he is so restless and at loose ends, but I dread the time when he goes and I have endless hours to fill in and I don't hear the rattle of the lid on the cooky jar seven times a day. Art says I can't count because I always announce proudly that I've made five dozen cookies and when he and one of his buddies count there aren't near that many. I'll bet the cookies 

I've made in the last twenty years would make a path to the moon and back again. It's fun to bring up a family, but it's an awfully empty feeling when they grow up and go on into their own lives. Not that I'd want anything else for them but, just the same, I'll have to figure out a new life and activities. 

February 27.-Two seed catalogues came this morning and I was so filled up with their glories that it's a wonder I could keep my mind on the First Aid Class.

We practiced attitudes of coma and anguish so as to know how to recognize them and what treatment to give. One bright mind asked, "What are we supposed to do if the victim is not breathing?"

I'm going to have an herb garden this year or know the reason why. Nothing gives glamor to simple food like the right herb in the right quantity. Lamb stew with rosemary is marvelous! From some more of those absolutely reliable, confidential rumors I hear that meat next summer and fall is going to be scarce and high and not too good in quality. Well, anybody with the brain 96

of a mosquito can cook a top grade porterhouse so that it tastes like something, but it takes a real cook to make a pound of chuck fit for company and seconds called for. I'll get out that prize recipe of mine for Russian meat loaf with sour cream. I'd serve that to Churchill himself and be proud of it. The flaky crust, made with nice clear bacon fat, and the flavorsome meat filling of the beef, with chopped hard-boiled eggs in it and just a dash of sage and marjoram, is something. Ice cold sour cream passed along and then a jellied beet salad and toasted rolls. Spiced pears cooked slowly with honey make a perfect dessert and black coffee or Russian tea served in our amber glasses. Guess I just like to cook and compose meals. The more difficulties there are about doing it the more kick I get out of the results. 

Plenty of opportunity to prove this beautiful theory of mine. Sugar rationing next and that means lots of substitutions and searching for recipes. I'll go back to that ancient White House Cook Book and dig out all the cakes and cookies and puddings that call for honey and molasses. Might even experiment on my own with corn syrup and I never could take to the sticky, insipid stuff. 

February 28.-The last day of a short month and I feel as though I had lived through ten years since morning. The phone rang at six A.M. as Jim was in taking his shower. Art was dead to the world so I answered it. Western Union calling-I never have and probably never shall get used to telegrams, they scare me to death long before I know what they are about. It was for Art -he is to report and be ready to leave on Monday for his training camp-where, I don't know. I hung up the 97

receiver and just stood there trying to gather myself together. I knew it would come-I even hoped it would come soon for Art's sake-but, someway, I wasn't prepared for the actual happening. I wanted Jim. I didn't want Jim. I couldn't bear to have him look in my eyes and see fear and the misting over that I couldn't control.

I felt so ashamed and so helpless. I heard him come out of the bathroom and go in to our room to dress. Time to start the coffee, I thought, and someway the very necessity of doing something, even such a simple thing with my hands, steadied me. Stirred up some muffins and took out the Damson plum jam-Art loves that and always insists I never make enough. By the time I had the table set and the bacon sizzling my world had settled down to somewhere near normalcy again. Thank heaven for the things I have to do! 1 98

March 

SOLDIER'S FAREWELL-CLAUDINE HAS A MISERY IN HER FEET-SURGICAL DRESSINGS AND THE GALLENTO FAMILY-SHIRLEY TEMPLE IS NOT COARSE-DR. BRUCE STEVENS AND GOD-BEN JY MET A BEAR-TWO NEW DURATION BOARDERS. 

March 2.-I saw Art off on the train this morning and had the strangest experience. Someway, how I'll never know, I managed to smile and wave and keep the tears down deep inside my heart. As the last car finally went around the bend my eyes simply spilled over and I stood there in the crowd too blinded to see anything. 

Suddenly I felt a hand on my arm and heard the gentlest voice I have ever heard. I was finally able to look around and saw a little woman, old enough to be my mother, who reached barely to my shoulder. "I envy you so much," she said. "Envy me?" "For having someone who means a great deal to you --someone you can grieve for and worry about. Someone you love with every breath you take. I haven't."

Then she was gone and left me standing there repeating to myself what she had said and knowing how right she was. Jim and I have loved each other and our children for all these years, and now pain and anxiety are on the other side of the balance sheet of happiness. We are to be envied because we have lived and will go on living taking the bad with the good-having our share in the common lot. 99

Someway, I couldn't go home. Jim was seeing Art off in town and wouldn't be back until late dinner. The house was in order, the food ready to do the final cooking. The house didn't need me and I longed, someway, to be alone with myself. How little of that there is in the rush we all live in. Along the main street I kept meeting people I know, answered their friendly questions about Art and Peg and Jim. Answered their urgings that I join this and that-now when I'd have so much time on my hands.

A raw wind was blowing and I turned down a quiet side street to find myself before the open wrought-iron gates of the Friends Meeting House. A huge old oak stands in front of the tranquil gray building and, on one side, the burial ground with its uniformly low headstones is sheltered in peace and dignity. The door of the Meeting was open and I walked in. Sober gray benches, sober gray carpet, sober gray walls. Perhaps it was the accumulation of the spirit of generations of people who have met there, to come into contact with a strength that is limitless and inexhaustible, that gave me a new sense of strength and acceptance-I don't know. But there came into my mind those words from the New Testament, "Peace I leave with you, my peace 

I give unto you; not as the world giveth, give I unto you. Let not your heart be troubled, neither let it be afraid." 

March 4.-Claudine has a misery in her feet-why she has any feet is a mystery to me. One day she wears a size six French-heeled little number that tilts her forward like a large ship under full sail; the next day it may be a pair of size five sneakers. No matter how 100

much money she and George make she still has an unconquerable desire to inherit shoes-anybody's shoes. I know that, at one time, the misery in her feet got on my nerves to such an extent that I persuaded Bruce

Stevens to take a look at them and prescribe some treatment or some footwear that would stop her groaning. I persuaded Claudine to go and see him and, for all the good it did, I might better have saved my breath. "The doctor man he say I should get some nurse shoes -two pair-and wear them all day and everday. I paid me the six dollars for each pair and they don't do me no benefit." "But you aren't wearing them!" "No, Maʼam-I wears them shoes two days and they miseries my feet more worse than befoh. I keeps 'em under my bed now."

This morning she arrived in the family limousine and plunked herself down on the kitchen stool as soon as she arrived. "My feets," she groaned, "is near killin' me. I done lef' the place with that rich lady and I got to take me a little vacation to res' myself. Her husban' he make a whole lot of money awful quick and they have too frequent of the service and too few of the food-I rather work for not so rich people like you." I guess not so rich people just about classifies us. Five minutes later I heard her starting to fill the washing machine while she dressed for the day's labors. There is always half a line of pink silk underwear hanging down there when 

Claudine changes costume. The shelf over the tubs is filled with face cream jar, powder box, rouge, lipstick,

IOI

and a bottle of some mysterious concoction labeled "Youth Glow." 

Soon I heard her singing, in her low, rich voice, "Throw out the life line-throw out the life line. Someone is sink-ing, is sink-ing to-day." This alternates with "Jesus wants me for a sunbeam." Whichever one she chooses is kept in perfect time to the chug of the washing machine and the slap of the damp clothes into the basket. Claudine is quite a gal! 

March 10.-I certainly had one grand "clarin' up" spell today and found enough stuff to keep me busy for the next two months. A box of metal for salvage was pretty near a family history. Old metal airplanes of 

Art's from the period when he had dozens of them and made hangars out of the empty cartons from the grocery. They gave me sort of a queer feeling and I put them hastily down in the bottom of the box. An old brass doll bed of Peg's came to light in the back of one of the closets on the third floor, and I remembered the 

Christmas when it had arrived complete with handhemmed sheets and pillow cases, a pair of pink blankets, and a patchwork spread. Jim's mother went to all that work and I know there was a heart full of love in every stitch. No wonder we all adored her. When I get to be an old lady I'm going to try to be sweet if it kills me. Better start working on it right now and learn to control my snippy temper. At Surgical Dressings yesterday I certainly talked too much and said too much but I was "mad enough to spit" as Peg describes it. Mrs.

A. Norton Melrose was there and just as idiotic and annoying as she was at the Salvage meeting. Some of us got to talking about how wonderful it is the way every- 102

body is pitching in to help win the war and how too bad it is that a war has to come along to get better neighborliness and co-operation. Mrs. Melrose, with that lorgnette manner of speaking of hers, broke in- “If only the foreign born element would do their sharethey're so indifferent." "Meaning who?" said Lottie. She's a whiz at pinning people like that right down so that they are about as efficient as a turtle on its back. "Well-take the Gallentos for instance-and I don't say they're the only ones. He won't serve as an Air 

Raid Warden, she won't come to Red Cross or Defense meetings-first generation Italians and one never knows where their sympathies are." 

Before Lottie could open her mouth I came in with my two cents' worth- "Their sympathies are with two sons in the U. S. Army and one in the Navy. Mrs. Gallento turns in more knitted stuff to the Red Cross than anybody in town. And both she and her husband are out in the garden at six every morning and until dark at night getting ready for a huge vegetable planting. "And," I added for good measure, "they don't have thirty-five pounds of sugar in the house!" That last uncalled-for remark I had gathered over a Benefit Bridge table-word does get around. I'm sorry I blew off like that-but, come to think of it, I guess I'm not. Becoming a sweet old lady will not be an easy job for me I'll need lots of practice. 

March 12.-Spent the whole evening going through the first seed catalogue of the season. Jim didn't get home to dinner and I managed with a tray-glass of milk and a sandwich. No wonder lone single women 103

take to the tea-and-toast menu and then work down to crackers and tea because it's easier. Cooking for yourself is about as exciting as dressing up for a party when there isn't any.

A seed catalogue and a dictionary are my undoing. 

No matter what I start to look for I'm bound to detour along the way. I took up the book with the full intention of going straight to the Victory Vegetables for Victory section. The colored pictures of gladiola caught my eye and reminded me of how many years

I tried painfully to pronounce it with the accent on the I-only to have to learn all over again to go back to the nice, old-fashioned pronunciation with the accent on the O. So-called authorities are no more dependable or predictable than the rest of us.

The colors were wonderful-like samples cut from fine silk damask-deep ruby red, clear violet, frosty white, apricot pink, and one that took my breath away and was the startling orange of a tangerine skin with flecks of crimson put on as delicately as though an artist had etched it in. It's a strange, mixed-up world with sorrow and suffering and destruction but I'm still willing to believe when I look at such beauty. Even the names were fascinating-Maid of Orleans, Minuet, Picardy and, right among them, the Shirley Temple. The description said, "Equal to Picardy or Miss New Zealand without coarseness."

I managed to move myself out of the gladiola pages but the roses stopped me again-ours are still asleep under their quilts of Christmas tree branches, but I'm uncovering them slowly and holding my breath for fear it's too soon. Why don't they send thermostats with 104

new babies and roses? Saw one rose called Cynthia and thought of little Cindy. She's been in my mind a lot the past few days and I keep remembering her sweet, "Good night all bodies," as she climbed up the stairs to bed. Hope she isn't ready for college by the time I see her again. They grow up much too fast and you have to keep your eye on them for fear you'll miss something. Chunky asks about her every time he comes over. Hasn't been here much lately-Ruth must have another jewel. Glad his arm was a dislocation and not a break because it seems good as new now. Children heal so fast physically, but the things that hurt them emotionally last a long time. Not that I agree with the psychiatrists who lay all your adult maladjustments to the fact that you once had a baby sister and therefore hate all blond men. Their reasoning is beyond me, but

I guess they have to make a living and who wants to pay ten dollars to be told something simple like, "Get to work," or "Take a mild laxative"? 

That's the trouble with Bruce Stevens. He won't deal in hooey and he won't tell people what they want to hear. He simply tells them the truth and most of them don't like it. His strong suit is children and he has the patience of a saint as far as they are concerned. If I ever get that large sum of money the fortune tellers predict will surprise me, I'll back that dream sanitarium of his with the last cent of it. He makes me furious the way he is always talking himself down, after a hard case, with, “I didn't pull her through-it was God and good nursing." He's so modest it's painful and so capable it's thrilling. 105

March 15.-Sunday used to be a quiet, slow-moving day with nothing much happening except friends dropping in to have tea with us in front of the fire or out on the porch. But times are different-everything happens on Sunday. Jim was down at the plant and I was going to meet him to go out for dinner. "Take it easy, Mom," Art always says, "and you'll last longer." For so many years there wasn't any question of taking it easy-too much to do. But in the past few months the whole picture has changed and I feel sort of like that Wedgwood teapot we kept on the dining-room shelf. 

It was of no use any longer because a deep crack ran from the handle clear to the base. Once it had been part of our everyday living and doing its share. It still looked all right but the strain of boiling water or lifting would have finished it. I'm not quite on the shelf—but 

I'm on my way. At least, I thought I was. 

At four o'clock Ruth Woods came in and I honestly felt sorry for her. She looked smart as ever in her uniform but there was a kind of depressed look on her face and the way she carried herself. For once she forgot to be alert and decisive and gracious. She just sat down and started right in. "I'm in an awful mess-and if you want to say 'No' go right ahead. I haven't any business asking you anyway." "Asking what?" 

She hesitated for only a split second. "To take 

Chunky for the duration." "Take him? Where?" "To live with you and Jim. They want me in Washington and I honestly feel I'd be of use, but I'm pulled 106

this way and that way. My husband's down there now and will have to stay-it's no place for a child with houses so hard to get and help all in Government jobs.” "Are you trying to persuade yourself-or me?" 

There were honest tears in her eyes-not the ones that Jim says I sometimes turn on for my own not-sogood uses. "I know how you feel. I love Chunky-I really do-but as a mother I'm not a wild successeven when I put my whole time and mind on it. 

When I try, and I have tried, to go completely domestic I'm so restless I could scream. Women have different talents, the same as men have, but everyone expects any woman to be a born mother and homemaker. Nobody expects every man to be a born carpenter or electrician-it isn't fair." 

I started to laugh and she looked at me in astonishment-I couldn't blame her. But going through my mind were the lines from the play My Sister Eileen where the two girls are in that perfect dump of a basement apartment. Everything has gone wrong and they're tired to death and discouraged to the eyebrows. Sister 

Eileen says to Ruth, "It just isn't fair!" Ruth looks at her with a dry and worldly wisdom, "You'd be surprised, when you grow up, how many things aren't fair."

I reminded Ruth Woods of this and we laughed together. The tension was broken. I don't know what Jim will say but I want to take Chunky-and I'm fond of his mother for the first time in my life. Perhaps I'm growing up, too, and have a little more ability to see the other fellow's point of view. 

I kept thinking, "What will Jim say?" But now I 107

know what he'll say for this isn't the half of it. Ruth

Woods was no more than out of the door with my cheery, "I'm sure we can work it out some way," than the bell rang and there stood a scrawny little girl with a too-large coat and a Western Union cap parked over one eye. I don't know which makes a child look more forlorn-a coat that is too big or a coat that is too small. 

Most of the Western Union boys around here had coat sleeves that crept up their arms and left the exposed part looking so hungry that the mother in me came right to the surface and I was always asking them in to have something to eat. Then, for a long time, all telegrams were phoned and I didn't see them any more. But I worried about them just the same-being made that way. "Come on in and sit down-there may be an answer. 

And how about a few cookies and a cup of cocoa? It's pretty cold out today." She wiggled around inside her coat until it seemed as though she were going to get lost in the folds.
