"I don't care," and, by that universal language of all shy youngsters, I knew how much she did care. Hating to open telegrams anyway I put it off by making the cocoa and getting the cookies. Then I sat down, too.

By this time, of course, I had Art in the camp hospital at death's door. Peg flat on her back with an appendix, some arrangement made for Jim's meals while I dashed to their various bedsides. But it was from neither. It was from Sally Greene. She must have been listening in to Ruth Woods for she started off the same way. "If you want to say no, go right ahead and no hard feelings. Jud going into the service. Benjy met a bear in August. Cindy hopes he's a girl. Can we share ex- 108

penses and enter the tourist home? Tell Jim I won't whinny. Letter follows. Love. Sally." 

What in the world did it mean, "Benjy met a bear in August"? Then the light dawned! Sally dizzy and sick at Christmas. Her favorite limerick-"Benjy met a bear. The bear was bulgy. The bulge was Benjy." December to August-of course. The old gypsy- "A man child you will have in this house, under this roof, before the flowers are on the trees again." A little off in her dates, but she knew something. What an uncanny memory I have for foolishness like that and how the educational facts of life slip right through my mind without a stopover. 

Wonder what Jim will say? But I know only too well. He's half convinced now that I am more or less of an idiot and this will make him sure of it. For I want 

Chunky for the duration. I want Sally and Cindy and 

Benjy. I seem to feel all alive again and young and needed in the job for which nature designed me. 

March 16.-Special from Sally this morning-I haven't had the nerve to say anything to Jim yet and thought 

I'd wait until I had more details. I have them all right and I'm so mad! Most people are so everlastingly kind and decent that it's always a jolt when you run into one who isn't. Sally's landlord gave her and Jud a great song and dance about how it was their moral duty to stay in the house and carry out their lease. So Sally made all her plans to have the baby up there. Then the landlord arrived on Saturday with the word he had an excellent renter, at a higher rental, and they could either meet the figure or move out by April first. 

Grand news for a young Army Lieutenant with another 109

baby coming-and the bills attached thereto. Legally they can hold the landlord to the lease, "But," Sally wrote, "I'm just not in a fighting humor-too much of that in the world now. I don't want Benjy born with a chip on his shoulder.” 

March 19.-My first chance to talk to Jim because he has been getting home so late and so tired. So I've thought and stewed over the tactful approach-and I might as well have saved my energy. Will I ever be able to figure what that man is going to do next? I hope not for life would be very dull.

Jim sat down in his big easy chair that I bought at an auction and have had re-covered three times because there isn't another one in the world that would suit him as well. I perched myself on the arm of it and leaned up against him. "Well, what is it you want to do? You've been going around here like a broody hen ever since Sunday.' 

I don't know why it astonishes me so when Jim sees through me like that, but he always has and he always will. There are times when it comes over me that he's a whole lot smarter than I am-which he probably knew right along. So I told him about Ruth and then led up gradually to Sally. He just sat and puffed on his pipe in the calmest way. When I'd finished he said with that smile of his which makes me go pulpy, after twenty years. "I believe you're just nutty enough to want to take in the whole procession." I nodded. "Well-why don't you? I like Chunky and Cindy- 

I'll probably like Benjy in time. We've got enough

IIO

room here for all of them and you'd be in seventh heaven. Stepping over youngsters is your long suit."

I could have squeezed the breath right out of himand did. 

March 20.-The clouds this morning were flat on the bottom and fluffy on the top like the meringues on floating island. I'll have to make some of that when my new big family arrives next week. I never saw a child yet who didn't think it was a party when the big glass bowl is brought in with the daffodil yellow custard and the snowy peaks with ruby jelly in each depression. And the daffodils will be out then on the south side of the porch and the robins will be back in whole flocks and eat up the grass seed faster than I can plant it. They are such cheerful, cocky little things and look just like fat aldermen on parade. I hope Hoskins doesn't chase them but he probably will. Hoskins simply has to come with Chunky-they come in a set. The place will be such bedlam anyway that a dog more or less won't make any difference. But I'll certainly break it to him gently but firmly that his taste for beefsteak will have to be curbed-and no more canned dog food very soon. He has to know, sooner or later, there's a war on and cans are needed for something more important than catering to the likes of him. He'll probably turn those mournful St. Cecelia eyes on me as though it were all my fault.

March 21.-Counted over all the blankets this morning and got George to get down Peg's old crib from the attic where we put it after Christmas. What do people do without attics? George tells me he has another III

raise and he and Claudine are going down to Florida for a Victory vacation. "What kind of a vacation is that?"

He pulled a travel leaflet out of his pocket and handed it to me. “This here 'splains the business. Claudine she read it out to me-she's awful smart, Claudine is." In my best judgment Claudine has a heart of gold and a head of ivory, but I'm not in love with her-which 

George definitely is. "Take a Victory vacation in the land of sunshine.

Relax from the tension and demands of your workaday world and come back full of vim for Victory. You owe it to your country's Defense to keep yourself in the best of physical condition and your morale on high." As Artie would remark, "Oh, yeah!" "How long will you be gone?" I asked with visions of mountains of clothes to be washed and Claudine basking in the land of sunshine. She's not too good but at least she still washes and laundresses, and day workers are getting scarcer by the minute. "Maybe ten days-maybe two weeks-Claudine she ain't decided." That lady certainly wears the nether garments in the family and is smart enough to keep him from knowing it-perhaps she isn't so dumb. 

March 24.-Planes droning overhead tonight, as they have every night since war began, back and forth at regular intervals. I got up and looked out of the window and the sky was criss-crossed with beams of light. 

Every once in awhile the beams would catch the plane and I knew that on the records of the practice flight a score would be marked up. How long will it be practice for those boys and how soon reality? Down the 112

hill and across the bay a few bright windows in homes -the rest in darkness; a dog barked on our hillside and the sound echoed, everything was so quiet. It's hard to realize the war here in spite of the fact that Art is on his way into the middle of it, sons and husbands of other neighbors have gone, our whole lives are rearranged and different, our point of view changed, our plans for the future put aside for the duration. The steadily flying planes overhead still give me a sense of security and safety, the beams of light, marking a design across the dark sky, a sense of beauty and peace. 

Perhaps it's just as well. There is no use in straining every nerve and muscle and mental and spiritual reserve before the real need for it comes. The main thing is to be ready when it does. I began to think of various passages from Flight to Arras. Never has a book thrilled me as that one did. Breath-taking adventure, deep philosophy of living and a style of writing that is pure poetry in its simplicity and the things left unsaid. Do your job, follow your heart rather than your intellect. 

Never try to dodge life but to be a part of it. And nothing good ever comes of a sortie you have gotten out of. I don't know whether he and General MacArthur have ever met in reality, but the same qualities of integrity and personal responsibility are in them both. 

Which, I suppose, is the same as saying that all great people have much in common. I know I'm a hero worshipper but I like to read about and hear about someone so far beyond me in courage and accomplishment that I could never attain to his standards-it gives me a lot to aim for and be proud of. 113

March 26.-Planted the whole front border with hardy annuals this morning and, in between stooping and digging, I took time out to sniff the air. Was there ever a smell like spring? It makes me restless and it makes me alive. I want to turn the house inside out and wash and shine and scour everything in sight and dig out all the hidden corners with the dust of winter trying to hide away. 

California poppy seed was coming up in tiny, feathery clusters between the green blades of the iris. What a golden glory they make later in the season-so fragile and so sturdy and each petal looking as though it were covered with waterproof shellac and then waxed to give a final finish. I put in some calliopsis seed-Jim never can get coreopsis and calliopsis straight and yet he can reel off figures by the running yard and show me a pin point on a map and know all about the history and appearance of it. Between us we are a very intelligent person.

Chunky and Hoskins came along while I was working and sat down on the top step of the terrace to visit. "We came over because my mother is busy. Are you busy, too?" "Not very." "I didn't think you'd be-and I like cookies." We fixed that up by making a trip to the kitchen and out again. Chunky sat down with one arm around the brown and white curls of the dog's neck. He looked up into the blue sky and watched a cloud float across it. "You know," he finally said, "this is a day I've never seen." I think if the heavenly recorder put down that 114

comment it must have repaid God for a lot of hard work and discouragements. 

March 27.-Mrs. Ralph Waldo Emerson is coming into her own. She has been quoted over and over, in various uplifting essays and articles, for her thrifty rules of "Eat it up, wear it out, make it do," and nobody paid any attention. Now it's fashionable and patriotic to be thrifty and I saw this same quotation, in letters half an inch high, on a poster down in the village. 

March 28.-Nearly the end of the month and I don't know where it has gone. Sally wrote she wouldn't be able to get down until Monday as they are having trouble getting a truck. Some of their things we can store or use here and the rest will go into regular storage. 

She has everything labeled with colored tags and the packing will be arranged so the things that come here unload first and then the truck go on to the warehouse. 

The men will arrive here Tuesday morning and by noon we'll be all straight. Cindy is so excited that she wakes up in the middle of the night to pack and repack her suitcase. All her dolls are dressed and rehearsed daily as to their proper behavior on the big train that goes to "her" house. "And her is a berry nice daddy and her doesn't love bad dolls. And Chunky is a nice boy and he can spit."

I didn't know this was one of his many accomplishments-I'll have a lot to learn. Wonder how soon Ruth will really go to Washington? There seems to be an awful lot of red tape connected with it. She wants to go, they want her to come, but it would never do to go about it in some simple, direct way- "All through the proper channels." 115

Anyway, we're all ready to take over Chunky whenever the Government decides to take over Ruth. The pantry is stocked to the edges and back to the walls, the cooky jar is full. Marketing these days reminds me of that favorite song of my younger days, "Yes, we have no bananas." Every time I go I see something else that isn't there. But there certainly .is plenty of something else and hominy grits is still among us. How Jim and Art love a big dish of that with pork chops fried so slowly and so brown that they make perfect milk gravy. If I ever catch up with another can of pineapple 

I'll have that dinner the first time Art gets leave. 

Glazed pineapple slices are the final and perfect touch.

The juice is still on the market and I might try soaking stewed apple quarters in it overnight and then glazing them. There's a thought. 

March 30.-Wire from Sally, "No truck. I'm not 

John Burroughs." Just poking a little fun at one of my favorite quotations, "Serene I fold my hands and wait." Many a time that poem has calmed me down as 

I said it over to myself, but I can see Sally's point. Waiting for a moving van is more nerve racking than waiting for a dentist when you have a toothache.

March 31.-Letter from Art-brief and to the point. 

"The boys are a swell bunch. The officers are slick. 

Grub good. Hope you and Dad are well. Got quite a few things to learn yet. Love, Art. P.S. Those last cookies were slick. Tell Peg I can still read her letters --if she'd write any. My regards to the Navy but the 

Air Corps of the Army has it beat a mile." 

Neither Peg nor Art will ever be able to compile, between them, even a thin volume of their collected 116

letters, but when Peg does write she lets herself go a little more and does make you feel near her with all the small details of her life. Milton brought a letter from her this afternoon and waited until I opened the door so I knew he had something on his mind. "What do you hear from your boy?" I asked. He shifted the heavy bag on his shoulder as though, by the shifting, he were easing another burden. "Haven't heard a word for going on weeks-his mother writes every day and I write when my thoughts get together. But it seems like we kind of put the letters into nowhere. Only address we have is by Army unit number and then they route it on. His mother feels awful bad not knowing if where he is might be hot or cold-this war kind of spreads itself out, don't it?” 

When he had gone I sat down with Peg's letter and thought how I should treasure the nearness of my family even though we aren't together. At least I know where they are and whether it's hot or cold. I wonder how much longer I'll be able to do that? Peg started off with- "Can you imagine your darling daughter sitting down of an evening with a Graduate Dietician and boning up on calories and vitamins and figuring nutrition charts a mile long and two miles wide? I can now rattle off how many pounds of beef are necessary in serving meat loaf to fifty men and how many quarts of sliced apple are needed to make apple pie for ditto number.

In exchange for all this acquired knowledge I'm repairing and remaking some of dear teacher's clothes. She can sling a mean frying pan and compose nine dozen rolls that practically float off the plates, but in sewing 117

she does everything upside down. We get along fine because each one of us is so dumb in the other one's specialty. The reason for all this urge for the higher education on my part is that I have a notion they may park Phil on the west coast for awhile and, if so, I'm going to follow the fleet as long as I can be near him. 

I don't want to spend days and weeks sitting in some furnished room waiting for my hero to turn up in the evening-if he's lucky enough to have any evenings. I figured some of the big defense plants out there might find use for a gal who can cook or at least boss others who can cook. Anyway, this keeps me out of mischief and I get too tired to miss Phil quite so much. It has just dawned on my bird brain recently how ghastly it must be for you with Dad gone half the time. Your little Peggy is sure growing up." 118

April 

LAUGHTER AND TEAR BOTTLES-GRUBBING IN THE GARDEN-OLD CLOTHES FOR VICTORY-"HE'S WON- DERFUL!" SAID LAURIE-MOVING MEN ARE ALL ALIKE-OUR FIRST SPY SCARE-A LITTLE PRES- ENT FROM GEORGE WHO MUST KNOW SOMEBODY. 

April 1.-April Fool's day dawned bright and fair and seemed almost too calm to be interesting. I could think of all the years when Peg and Art were little and planned ways to fool Jim and me. Crumpled paper in the toes of our slippers, Jim's trouser legs sewed together, and the sleeves of my dressing gown pinned to the back so that I looked like some nervous octopus when I tried to get into it in a hurry by the light of early morning. I wish there were some way to bottle up all the laughs we had so that we could uncork them on occasion like rare old wine. Vintage of 1932 with bubbles of laughter, vintage of 1933 with chuckles of mirth as Jim tried to unfold the morning paper and found it fastened together with Scotch tape-how the youngsters did go into giggles over that one. And the time that Jim and I discovered the April Fool candy they had labored over so long-frosted chunks of soap and ornate blocks of wood dipped in chocolate-we had to do some superfine acting to be surprised and fooled when they passed it around. Why doesn't someone think up a way to save all these delightful memories? The old Romans had their tear bottles and sad young men dig them up from the ruins and carefully 119

enter them in books of records. But think of running across some delicate piece of glass and finding it full and running over with laughter and smiles. 

April 2.-Not a word out of Sally and I was dashing around like a banshee this morning getting ready to go to Surgical Dressings-I'm really getting good at some of those tricky things and, if I were on an assembly line, my daily output chart would show a nice rise. 

I decided, at the last minute of course, to wear brown shoes. Which meant I had to carry my brown bag and transfer the contents of one into the other. I often wonder what women did with their time before they became ensemble conscious. It's a great idea if you have nothing else to do but grandma's one best black dress and bonnet had its points. Now they even try to sell you perfume for tweeds, perfume for the afternoon mood, and perfume to enchant by the light of the moon. With the extra ten per cent tax on all such I guess most women will be thankful for one bottle of whatever 

Santa Claus brings them. And I really can't believe that the national war effort is going to depend upon whether we all smell and look beautiful or not-in spite of the efforts of the advertisers to make us believe so. Sometimes I get mad as hops over that kind of blurb and, then again, I laugh at it-which is probably better. 

April 4.-The most beautiful day we've had so far this spring. I went out to count the crocus in the rock garden, for it is a pastel tapestry of pale yellow and lavender and glistening white. Stopped to pull a weed or two and went on and on-weeding is like eating popcorn, you never know when to quit. Queer thing about most weeds is that you get sort of attached to 120

their charm and personalities until you begin to find hundreds of them and get furious because they are crowding out the really good things. There is one starry-eyed little variety that is covered with the tiniest of white flowers-each one is lacy and perfect and about as big as a fairy's boutonnière. The rock garden is full of it and it's tucked around the clusters of crocus and tangled up in the masses of rock pink. No way to get it out except by hand and with bended knees. It should give my spirit humility, but all I seem to acquire is stiff joints and an unholy glee at how much I have managed to pull up.

The wind was blowing my hair all around my face- Jim always says I have baby hair because it is so fine and completely unmanageable but he likes me the way 

I am, tousle and all. I'm so glad he met me and I met him and we became us. It doesn't seem as though it could have been any other way or any other combination of two people. Well, there I worked, grubbing away for dear life and having a wonderful time, when a voice startled me. I looked up and there was the boss of the road crew. I think he hibernates in the winter and comes out in the spring because he always looks sort of thin and unfed for the first few weeks and then fattens up nicely toward summer. "How are the delphiniums?" he shouted. He and I have had many a chat on the subject and he has his theories and I have mine. We argue on bone meal versus sheep manure and wood ashes as a cure or not cure for black mold. All I know is, that no matter what I do to mine, some of them give up the battle every winter and die off. They are the most lovely, and the most 121

temperamental, plant in the garden. If the season is too wet and the drainage not right, they get crown rot or something equally horrible. If the season is too dry they curl up and depart this life and, in between times, they acquire a nasty little insect of some sort that curls up in the new and tender leaves and the leaves curl right along with them. Just the same I mother them along and put out some new plants each year with an unquenchable hope. "Mine are fine," I shouted back. "How are yours?" "Never better-that bone meal is the stuff! And I put in some King Arthur and Guinevere, and Lancelotthey ought to go good together." I didn't argue it with him but, to my notion, there is the making of a nice family row if I remember my Idylls of the King. What was that farewell speech of Arthur's to Guinevere, "Thou hast not made my life so sweet that I, the King, should greatly care to live." When Tennyson put his mind to it he could write poetry. Some of it sings in your head for years after you've read it. Even a child, to whom the words mean little or nothing gets the music of it. I read The Lotus Eaters to Chunky one night when he was restless and he dozed off to the smooth, gentle rhythm of it- "Music that gentlier on the spirit lies than tired eyelids upon tired eyes." I'm glad I have some of the old timers in my memory files. 

April 4.-George came in today to help me give the house a real digging out and gather up some more odds and ends for the salvage collection. It is simply amazing the amount of things that have been collected in this one small town, and everybody is better off with the decks cleared and ready for action. Jim has our third 122

floor really looking like something. The whole west end is completely bare except for the buckets of sand, the shovel, the Army blanket ready to be wet and wrapped around you while you fight incendiary bombs. 

Every time he comes home from an Air Raid meeting he has another method urged by another expert. One says spray the bomb, the next one says use a steady stream and they all agree that if you happen to draw one with a joker in it which explodes you're just out of luck. An Air Warden, who has been through the thick of it in England, told them last week that, in case of a bomb falling in the street there were several things a pedestrian might do- 

1. Get inside a building if possible-but not down in a cellar. 

2. Lie down flat on your face in the gutter-and don't mind your dignity. 

3. Put your hands back of your head for whatever protection that might afford. 

4. Count seven very slowly. "Why seven?" someone asked. "Because seven is a lucky number-and you're going to need it." 

I only hope I have that much sense of humor left: if the curtain rises on this rehearsal and we're in for a real performance. The general public seems to be divided into two classes-those who say it is ridiculous to even imagine the Germans will try to bomb us and those who are convinced they will. I belong to the "It's coming, sure as fate" group. I can't for the life of me see why they wouldn't attempt to at least give us a nuisance raid to see how well we scare. But I don't 123

believe we'd scare worth a darn. Nothing makes the average, everyday American madder than to have some big bully try to push him around. 

George says, "I ain't no pacifist-I'se a man of peace." And so are most of us. I can't imagine anything I would enjoy less than trying to shoot anybody.

But I'm dead certain that if it were a question of protecting myself, or someone I love, I'd certainly do my best to shoot straight. I remember the scene in Gone with the Wind where Scarlett kills the drunken Yankee soldier. Even her hard-boiled nature didn't feel happy over it but what else could any woman with spunk and a sense of responsibility do? Just the same 

I keep hoping I won't have the decision to make.

Laurie came in this morning and she was simply glowingly happy. She is working days, nights, and

Sunday-whenever they'll let her-to finish her eighty hours of training so she can be capped as a full-fledged Nurse's Aid. She has had only theory so far, but next week she starts in the supervised hospital training part of it. She wants Jim and me to come to the capping ceremony and we'll surely be there. She put on her uniform for me, minus cap of course, and it is certainly becoming. The clear blue with the white blouse sets her off to perfection and brings out the blue in her eyes. 

They were shining and alive as she went over every single incident of the last few days-especially when she spoke of Bruce. "Dr. Stevens came in today when I was learning to give an alcohol rub to Belinda-that's our dummy patient that we practice on-and he said I had the touch 124

all right-but perhaps he was just trying to encourage me." "Not if I know Bruce-and I think I do-he's never been known for his tact and the correct thing to say. 

That's the trouble with him-too blunt and honest for his own good. It isn't appreciated by rich old ladies. 

If he says you're good you can take it one hundred per cent." "He's wonderful!" said Laurie, and all I could think of was myself at the dewy age of seventeen with my first crush on the captain of the football team. I do hope Laurie doesn't go off the deep end-Bruce simply isn't the marrying kind and you don't get over those things at her age as easily as you do at seventeen. Jim says I can figure out more things to worry about than any other three women he ever knew. But I am growing fonder and fonder of Laurie and I'd hate to see her hurt. I never knew Bruce to spend any time around 

Red Cross classes before. I sort of wonder. 

April 5.-Saw a cheering note in the Sunday paper this morning. "Still good style to wear old clothes. No need to rush out and buy new ones the Government assures us." As Claudine says, “I certainly does depreciate that." My old clothes are getting the mending and the pressing of their lives. No more safety pins instead of buttons, no more little rips that grow into big ones— I'm a reformed woman and enjoying the sense of thrift and accomplishment it gives me. I took an old Chinese silk nightgown this afternoon and replaced the worn binding with strips of the flowered material I had cut off Peg's best housecoat when I shortened it. Yards and yards of it and the gown is really prettier than it was 125

before and will wear a long time. It's amazing what satisfaction a job like that can give you.

No more swooshy housecoats like that for the duration. The new fashion regulations will soon be out and, from all I read, we'll have to cultivate the streamlined figure if we aren't going to look like a lot of sausages. 

Cutting down on sugar may be a blessing in disguise.

Chunky and Hoskins came to dinner-how that child can eat and how that dog can beg! I was glad I had a sizeable roast of lamb, even though it had seemed silly to buy it for just Jim and me. Chunky was delighted with the peaches filled with mint jelly that I had broiled and fixed around the platter. Children have far more appreciation of attractive looking food than people give them credit for and it is worth while fussing for them. 

Not that I believe in wearing yourself to a rag and a bone and a hank of hair to coax their appetites when fresh air and happiness will take care of that.

Jim pointed out a real estate ad today that gave us both a good laugh. "Are there ghosts in your home? 

Do empty rooms echo to your footfall and groan 'Too many rooms-too few people'? Adjust your life to the tempo of the times. Select a suite in Haven for the duration and banish all care. Maid service. Café, cocktail room, valet service. Home at its best-without the burdens of home." We'll take the burdens and keep the home-if it's all the same to the management. 

April 6.-Our rooms got rid of their ghosts with a bang today. Sally, Jud, and Cindy arrived bag and baggage. Jud is waiting for his final orders and came down for a few days to help them get settled. Sally looks wonderful even though her nice straight figure is get- 126

ting a little more Benjy. The moving van was due at eleven A.M.—and arrived at eleven P.M. "Tire trouble," the boss said. All Sally's careful labels were so much decoration as far as the men were concerned. The things for this house were clear at the back of the truck and the things for the warehouse were at the front. The result was complete bedlam by the light of the moon. "Dames don't know how to plan nothin'," said the boss disgustedly. Up and down, in and out they went with everything in the wrong rooms and then carried out again. I made a gallon or two of coffee and kept handing out doughnuts whenever their enthusiasm seemed to falter.

By about one o'clock they had everything put in the wrong places and Jim and Jud began to help them. I began wondering if I had enough eggs in the house because it looked like an all night job with a full crew on hand for breakfast. Sally and I sat down in the living room to get our second wind before taking up the struggle again. "It's wonderful," I said, "how well Cindy sleeps through this racket." "Yes, isn't it-marvelous!" I thought there was a funny note to her voice and a twinkle in her eye so I looked around the edge of my chair. There stood Cindy standing like a yellow-haired Christmas doll and just as quiet, but her face was crinkled up in the way she has when she's pleased with the world in general. We tucked her in the corner of the davenport with the rainbow afghan around her and everything seemed peaceful. "Too peaceful, if you ask me," said Sally. "I don't 127

hear a sound up there and four men should be banging something around." 

I called, "Jim!" No answer. 

Sally called "Jud!" No answer.

I tiptoed up the stairs. Not a sign of life on the second floor and one chest of drawers smack across the bend in the hall. I opened the door to the third floor and stood stock-still.

The boss was holding forth, "I sez to my old woman, I sez, sure I voted for him but that don't mean I got to swallow everything he does and think it's swell. And

I sez to Alvin here you been on this truck with me going on five years and you know I'm a square boss but you vote one way and I vote another-and what the hell!" I didn't quite follow his reasoning but maybe 

Jim did because his voice broke in. "His foreign policy may be O.K.-I wouldn't knowbut his domestic policy-now take that—” 

I'll never know what you take because Jud started out with, "What can MacArthur do if they don't get the stuff to him? I ask you that?" It's amazing how logical men's minds aren't. There they sat, at one in the morning, draped over trunks, sitting on buckets of sand, spread out on the Army blanket-cozy as you please and settling the problems of the world while the furniture just sat. From the amount of smoke pouring down the stairs you would have thought an incendiary bomb was on the job. But it was only two pipes, one cigarette, one cigar-Alvin, it seems, favors cigars. I tiptoed down again. It didn't seem quite the moment for the stern voice of duty to break up that Town Hall Meeting. 128

April 10.-Our first trial blackout tonight-and our first spy scare. I hope everybody in the neighborhood feels as humble and ashamed about our spy as I do. 

Jim was out with the rest of the Air Raid Wardens as soon as the sirens started blowing their series of blasts as the instructions described. Someway, on paper and in reality they are very different. Sort of an eerie sound that goes through you and makes you wonder if it is the real thing in spite of the fact the test was announced. Sally and I put out all the lights and stepped onto the side porch to look over the village and the hills. Not a glimmer anywhere but the moon bright and shining in a serene and impersonal way. I love moonlight but I can never get any real affection for the moon-too much like a beautiful woman with no warmth of heart.

The sound of a car or two on the main road and then as complete a silence as I can imagine. Figures of neighbors, with their Warden arm bands, went past every so often. The voices of people in their yards came rather muted as people's voices do in a large and almost empty church. Then we heard a Warden's whistle. No lights could be seen, everything was going as planned and rehearsed and scheduled. Suddenly we heard Jim calling to Jud. "Try ringing the bell again-I'll go in and phone." 

But he came back again and reported. "No answerfunny thing. There's a light in the window that blinks off and on, under the edge of the blinds. Who lives in that third house around the bend?"

I hadn't the vaguest idea but only knew some new family had moved in about two weeks ago. I had been 129

too busy trying to win the war for Democracy and tend to my own family's comfort to take the time and trouble to be a decent neighbor. Right at that moment 

I wasn't rating very high in my own estimation. Nobody else seemed to know who the new people were either, which didn't make it any better. Several of us walked down the hill, to see for ourselves this mysterious on-and-off light. It was just a dim glow, coming through a space of a few inches where the Venetian blind wasn't quite down-but it definitely did shine and then dim. We heard footsteps and looked around to see George and Claudine coming up the flagstone walk. "Hello," I called. "Evenin'!" they answered in chorus and nodded to each of the group in turn. George and Claudine are sort of neighborhood property and go from one to the other of us as they're needed. Claudine still does but, since 

George got his defense job, we've had to do our own lawn mowing and gardening and window washing—that kind of help seems to be out for the duration. You can't even get a boy any more. "Know these people?" asked Jim. "Sure does-she's a nice lady and her sister she's a nice lady too. Me and Claudine see 'em down to the movies tonight-they goes to the movies a whole lot, 'count of they's lonesome. Claudine she does for them on Tuesdays, and she say they don't cry when she's lookin' at 'em but they cries plenty when she don't know it." "Cry?" I said, feeling a queer sinking in the pit of my stomach. 

George stood silent a minute, as though wondering 130

whether he should go on. "The husband of the lady he's reported by the Navy man as missing in action and the husband of the sister he's flyin' one of these here airplanes in one of them funny-named places that's in the paper. That's why they bof' cries when nobody ain't lookin'."

There wasn't a sound out of any of us but I believe everyone was doing the soul searching that was going inside of me. We had forgotten all about the glow of light with its pulsating quality. We had forgotten our silly, loose talk of possible spy signals. But Claudine brought us back, "That pore lady she done forget to turn off her goldfish 'count of the blackout. They sets there in the window and swims forth and back all day and nights she turns on a l'il light and they shines nicer colors. She says they's company and her man bought them for a surprise once. She thinks maybe he come back someday when he ain't missing no more." 

April 11.-While Sally and Cindy were digging around out in the garden this morning I made an extra cheese cake, after Laurie's recipe, and, when it came from the oven all crusted over with golden crumbs, I carried it down the hill to the third house around the bend. Sally had cut some sprays of flowering quince that will force so quickly now in warm water and a sunny window. Mrs. Kirkwood and her sister Mrs. 

Langwell were putting fertilizer into a new flower bed along the south side of the porch. "Did you dig that huge bed yourselves?" I asked, just to get by the first awkward minute. 

She smiled up at us. "I started to-but George wouldn't let me. He told Claudine to tell me it wasn't 131

suiting for a lady and he'd be by some evening and give ´me a hand. He did, too, and Claudine brought us a lot of seeds saved from their garden. People are so kind, aren't they?" 

I didn't answer. I couldn't. 

April 14.-Sally and I have worked out a grand scheme to get the work done. I love to cook and hate to sew and she just reverses that choice. I was so delighted when I read, in Cross Creek, that sewing always gives Marjorie Kinnan Rawlings a stomach ache. She tells how she sat at the sewing machine getting stomach ulcers in an attempt to make clothes for a family of neighbor children. Hand work I enjoy and the finer and fussier it is the better-making doll-sized dresses and slips for Benjy is my idea of a good time. But turn me loose with a life-sized pattern and yards of goods and I end up with a sick headache and something that no human figure could possibly wear to a dog fight.

So Sally is deep in making over a lot of my old things and creating lovely new ones from material I have bought in weak moments and never done a thing with. 

And the cook books and kitchen stove are getting a real work out under my care. I read in some paper lately that Clarepotter, the designer, said of the new fashion regulations, "There are no restrictions on designing-and any real designer welcomes some limitations since they tend to stir her imagination and ingenuity." Sugar rationing, and all the rest that will probably have to follow, will give us a chance to prove what we can do. 

April 16.-We got out the Sears Roebuck catalogue tonight so that Sally could order some of the things 132

she needs for the baby. As usual we began browsing in every other section except the one we started after. 

We struck the pages where the double faced records are listed and some of the combinations set us all laughing as we read them aloud to Jud and Jim who were deep in the evening paper and their pipes. 

Seven Beers with the Wrong Man and I'm Not

Coming Home Tonight. 

I'm Lending You to Uncle Sammy and Are You Sure? 

Careless and In an Old Dutch Garden.

The man who put those combinations together either has a grand sense of humor or none at all. Reminds me of the double feature movies that Jim and I are always chuckling over.

We finally reached the pages we were looking for- Necessities for the Wee One, or something of the sort. 

It's amazing how many necessities a wee one requires to be decently presentable. "Double duty step-on pail," read Sally. "They have two grades-shall I order the $2.95 or the $3.95 one?"

Jud said nothing. He was on the second page of the funnies. Sally repeated. He finally came to-"The $2.95 or the $3.95 what?" "Double duty step-on pail-for the diapers." "Diapers?" as though he had never heard the word before. But the memory pains came over him all of a sudden. "Gosh, sweetie, do we have to start that all over again?" His face looked like a little boy who has just discovered there isn't any Santa Claus. 133

April 18.-Read in the paper this morning that there is a bumper crop of maple sap this year-and prices will be twenty-five per cent higher. Somebody is always taking the joy out of life. Candied sweet potatoes in maple syrup are Jim's favorite vegetable and Art can eat his weight in maple mousse-it's as smooth as a book agent's sales talk. I'm going to try fixing both of them with part corn syrup and see how they turn out. I still don't like corn syrup. But, "You must keep an open mind on the fascinating field of substitutes," our nutrition instructor lectured us. Parts of the course have struck me as plain silly or just using your common sense, although a lot of it is opening my eyes to things I never thought of before. And down at the market they have huge signs over the fruits and vegetables -Vitamins A, C, D-or whatever that particular thing happens to have. We won't absorb or use all of this knowledge but some of it is bound to sink in. 

April 21.-After today Bruce Stevens might as well enlist in the medical corps and get it over with because he has certainly finished himself as far as the country club crowd is concerned. If I wasn't so fond of that man and so proud of his real ability I wouldn't have minded, but to sit by helplessly and watch him lop off a whole chunk of his most profitable practice in one fell swoop! I'm sure Mrs. A. Norton Melrose will never call him again even for what she so grandly refers to as "the servants," and the crowd she has gathered around her will follow the leader. She has a huge house and scads of money, so, quite naturally, a lot of the Civilian Defense groups have been meeting up there. 

We were supposed to go yesterday and the thing was 134

called off because Mrs. Melrose had the grippe. This morning, bright and early, the maid phoned that "Madam is up and about and would wish you to come at two-thirty this afternoon."

We were all there, about thirty of us, and sitting around in her living room that reminds me of the lobby at the Waldorf-all except the bell hops. I used to go in there to meet Jim and, just for the fun of it, start to light a cigarette. Immediately those cute little Buttons converged on me and did everything but smoke it for me. It gave me such a nice helpless, looked-after feeling. Mrs. Melrose did look a little pale and washed out but she was going full tilt and is a born organizer. 

Just as she was appointing a few chairmen for special committees I drew the job on the canteen-the chimes from the front door sounded. We heard the maid go slithering down the hall in that refined and silent way maids have at eighty dollars a month-I'll never forget our Rosa, at thirty-five per, who used to bawl "Telephone!" if I was on the third floor and she in the basement.

The front door was opened and we heard a man's voice, "Dr. Stevens-I ran in to see how Mrs. Melrose is getting on-shall I go right up?" We heard the maid mumble something and our hostess turned a couple of shades paler but not for nothing was she Mrs. A. Norton Melrose with trimmings to match. "Come right in, Dr. Stevens-your little patient is here.” Her voice had a gay sort of "what are you going to do about it" quality but she didn't look too happy. 

She looked even less so when Bruce came striding through the wide arch from the hall. His face had lost 135

all its kindness, all its easy smiling quality. He didn't give so much as a glance at the rest of us but walked straight over to her. "I thought I told you to stay in bed until I said you could get up. If you want another man on the case that's your privilege, but-and get this straight-as long as I'm in charge you'll do as you're told." Mrs. Melrose started to open her mouth and closed it again. The rest of us didn't get that far. Without another word Bruce picked her up and marched to the stairs with the quaking maid trailing after them. The meeting seemed to be over. 

April 24.-Saw Bruce again tonight and, I must say, he seems surprisingly cheerful and nonchalant for a rising young physician who has just dumped a goodly chunk of his practice overboard. When Mrs. Melrose takes you up she takes you up, and when she drops you the splash can be heard for miles. Jim and I had gone in to the hospital to Laurie's capping ceremony and we were no more than in our seats when Bruce slid in beside us. I was honestly terribly surprised because he never takes time off for the social side of living-but there he was, big as life. Before I could give any more than a startled "Hello!" he began to explain-at least he said something. "Had to take a run in to see a patient and thought I might as well wait and drive you and Jim out. You didn't bring your car, did you?" "Not on your life!" said Jim. “My tires won't stand any joy riding and I don't expect to live long enough to get a new set."

There was a rustle and everybody began turning 136

around in their seats. The Nurse's Aids, in their blue apron uniforms and white blouses, came two by two down the center aisle. The lights were very dim and there was sort of a hush like in a church, just before the organ starts to play the music that makes little quivers go all over me. There was music here too—a harp, a piano, and two violins played by the regular hospital nurses in their crisp white uniforms. I tried to pick 

Laurie out as they filed slowly past and the tears came in my eyes and blurred them all together. There was something very solemn and impressive about watching this very average group of women of all ages coming to the end of their training period and ready to pitch in to help. Nobody had made them stick on the job until the finish, nobody had even made them start what is really hard work and much of it unpleasant physically and a strain emotionally. Laurie has told me enough as she went along to make me realize it is no afternoon tea-and your feet and back and heart ache many times. 

Bruce was sitting on the aisle and, as my eyes cleared a little, I looked up. Laurie was just opposite us and I tried to catch her eye but she didn't see me. She didn't see anything or anybody-except Bruce. His hand raised only the slightest bit, he looked back at her and smiled as though they were the only two people in the world. It was only a second but my breath caught as though I had witnessed the briefest glimpse of a miracle. But it was gone as quickly as it had come for Bruce remarked casually, "Her slip shows."

I could have shaken him to something resembling partly set gelatin with the greatest of pleasure. Men 137

have the greatest faculty for pricking the balloons of feminine day dreaming. Anyway, Laurie did look lovely when she was capped and the pin put on the front of her uniform. No sweet girl graduate of eighteen could possibly have been more radiant.

Jim took the four of us around to a Russian restaurant in the neighborhood and we ate heavenly blini with cool, thick, sour cream and drank red Caucasian wine. Quite a party. 

April 25.-Mrs. Melrose has pneumonia and Bruce is still on the case. The gossip goes that she won't let him out of her sight and even refuses consultation. It's a queer world and women are the queerest thing in it. 

April 27.-Jim spaded up the back garden today to get ready for vegetable planting. The flowers will simply have to stand on their own feet and get along the best way they can-you can't beat Hitler and the Japs with petunias. But there was a new sweet pea at the flower show this year that I've give my eye teeth to have-the Mrs. Douglas MacArthur-a lovely deep salmon that reminds me of the description in one of 

Beebe's books of The Vale of Butterflies. I'd like to have it for its beauty and for its connection with a woman who knows what a real marriage is. Someday the story of her flight to Australia, with her husband and her baby, will be told. Only Conrad could do it justice. 

April 28.-George came by tonight with the most wonderful present-a load of manure! Where he got it or how he got it we didn't ask. Like the bootleg daysyou have to know somebody who knows somebody. 

You can't buy manure in the open for love or money. 138

"Jes' a li'l token of friendship from me and Claudine. 

I ain't never forgot how good you all was when my ole man died and I didn't have the buryin' money."

Most people's gratitude is like annuals-in full bloom one season and vanished the next. But George's is a hardy perennial which increases and grows deeper roots as the years go on. What a really fine man he is. He'll mother this Victory garden of ours as though it were his own, and it's a blessing that he will for neither Jim nor I have the green fingers when it comes to vegetables. I know we're in for some mistakes and headaches but we can learn-and George will teach us. 139

May 

EIGHT OUNCES A WEEK PER PERSON-SOME RAN- DOM THOUGHTS ON THE LONG ISLAND RAILROAD THE BEST CIRCUS I EVER, EVER SAW-NEW YORK IN THE DIMOUT-STOP AND GO BRIDGE AT DINNER FOR FOUR-THE BOOKS DON'T KNOW EVERYTHING. 

May 4-We added two more children to our family today--and lost our two men for the time being. Ruth's final order came and Chunky moved over with all his belongings. "My mother's going down to tell the President what to do," he announced-and I'll bet she will if she ever gets the chance. 

Cindy threw her arms around him much to her pleasure and his evident five-year-old embarrassment. But he took it like a man even though he stood stiff as a dowager and was only on the receiving end. 

Jud is on his way to somewhere on the Pacific coast --it's anybody's guess for how long or where next. And

Jim is Washington bound to try to straighten out a priority ruling that has his company tied in knots and machines standing idle.

But we have Cindy and Chunky and Hoskins-and Whisbick. The latest addition to our reasonably busy family is of Cindy's doing. How long he'll be here I wouldn't know.

Cindy was helping to set the table for lunch-children always love to do those little deeds of kindness when they are too small and then, later on, they can scent 140

useful labor a mile off and detour rapidly out of sight. 

She put on an extra place and Sally asked, "Who is that one for?" "Whisbick!" "Who on earth is Whisbick?" 

Cindy looked at her with great patience. "She's a boy and when he grows up she'll be a Mommy and have little girl and boy Whisbicks. She has a mosquito bite on his hand and I have to wrap it up so he won't get sick." Sounds like Lewis Carroll- "She's all my fancy painted him." Who was it said that "Genius is prolonged childhood"? 

May 5.-Cut the first apple blossoms this morning and wondered if the gnarled old tree felt any of the thrill and joy that I did. I hope so, because it would make up for struggling through the cold, bleak winter when, many times, every twig on it is coated with ice and the wind shrieks through it like the Dance Macabre with the rattling of bones. I brought in the branches

I had cut and arranged our blue Rookwood jar full of them on the old drop leaf table in the hall. Cindy and 

Chunky came to admire and even Hoskins put on his St. Cecelia expression. Cindy's face was a study in the wonder and wonderment of a child, and Chunky put his hands behind his back, in that little old man way he has, and just looked. Finally he turned to me and announced solemnly, "There are twenty on that piece -twenty flowers on that one piece." "They're talking to me," said Cindy.

Chunky gave her a patient but slightly scornful smile. "Flowers don't talk!" 141

"My flowers do-and they sing to me when I keep still." "She's just a baby-and I'm five years old. She doesn't know." "And you're a dumb-bell," she came back sweetly. 

Where she added that tid-bit to her rapidly growing vocabulary I haven't discovered. Children never imitate the good qualities and polite manners of othersvirtue is not dramatic. 

May 6.-Went over tonight to register for sugar rationing. If Jim had been home I would have attended to it on the first day instead of the third. He's always one for getting things done promptly and in order. But 

I always figure I might be dead and then I wouldn't have to bother. I can reason out, quite logically, that he is undoubtedly right, because I never have died in time to avoid anything and there it is staring me in the face with a last minute rush and confusion-but I thrive on excitement. At least half the town must belong to my school of thought because they were all down there waiting in line. Sort of a nice, neighborly affair at that. 

Saw people I haven't seen for a month of Sundays.

Some of them were behind the tables doing the registering and the school certainly didn't look natural with no children around. Some people went up and filled out the applications with no fuss and bother, but a lot of them took half an hour to explain why they had twenty pounds of sugar on hand and why it shouldn't be counted because-well, just because, when you really got to the bottom of their excuses. It was as good as a county fair complete with side shows. All I missed was a fortune-telling tent and a hot dog stand. Finally 142

reached my turn at eleven-fifteen. Maybe Jim is right.

He so often is but I love him in spite of it. 

May 8.-Read a short article this morning on the training of dogs for the Army and one sentence stuck in my head-"The degree of perfection in appearance, required by dogs in the ring, will be sacrificed in favor of complete dependability and co-operation between the dog and the sentry. The dogs will be working with the sentry mostly at night and must be alert to give warning." There's a thought for the Fashion publicity girls to ponder. To listen to them you'd think battles were won or lost in the Salons and at the Openings. 

May 11.-Gas rationing starts next Friday and Claudine walked to work this morning! She's just getting in some practice, she says, because a swell car like theirs can't go so far on a diet and George wants to save up against their vacation. She started a new tune over the washing machine-"I'm a headin' for the Glory Shore" and alternated with "Whose Lil' Sweetheart Are You?"

Guess there's no question about that with Claudine. She and George could give some of these much married debs a pointer or two. 

May 12.-Some one of these days I'm going to take time out to write a short treatise on What I Do Not 

Like About the Long Island Railroad. Come to think of it I don't believe I could really do it justice in less than novel length-a new Gone with the Wind. Today's trip into town gave me material for at least one more chapter. The car I rode in must have turned up somewhere when they were digging for ancient ruins in a forgotten corner of the earth. Some of the seats wouldn't budge and stuck two thirds of the way back 143

so you had to sit as bolt upright as an old time minister at a funeral. Some of the seats had no backs, some of them tilted forward at a drunken angle as though they had given up all hope. The metal strips across the tops of the seats have ingenious little devices supposedly designed to hold tickets-at least that is the general impression-until you try to get a ticket into one. The opening is just big enough to encourage you to try and then it leers at you in a nasty way, the ticket catches by one frail corner and, at the first breath of air, falls to the floor. So you try again, thinking you simply haven't the knack of the critter, the same thing happens and you give up. I decided that the man who designed them has a mean streak in his nature and worked off some of his dislike of the human race in this subtle way. 

The woman next to me remarked, "Hot, isn't it? Mind if I open the window?" I didn't mind so she pressed the levers at the side and began to lift upward, in a ladylike fashion. Nothing happened. So I took one lever and she the other and we heaved together. Still nothing happened. We waylaid the conductor. "If you ladies don't mind stepping out of the way- 

I'll fix it." The man was either a born optimist or the long years, growing gray in the service, have broken his spirit. The window presented the blank, emotionless expression made famous by Marlene Dietrich and gave not so much as a quiver. "This one must be stuck!" said the conductor as though he had made a strange and unexplainable discovery. In fifteen years of travel on this so-called 144

public conveyance I have never seen one that wasn't -the two levers are put there solely for ornament. 

We finally arrived in town, in spite of the fact that at every stop the train gave groans and jolts and then crashed together, where the couplings are supposed to be, until your teeth rattled and your backbone seemed to leave its moorings. Then we got stuck in the tunnel and sat there. "Nothing serious, Madam," said the conductor soothingly. "We'll be moving along soon." Fifteen minutes later we did move along but nobody explained why we were stuck in the first place. Explaining anything to the passengers is not in the rule book. Many a night 

I've sat down at the station in the dead of winter slowly freezing to death as time rolled on. But asking why the train was delayed, how long it would be delayed, whether I had a husband or a casualty was not for me to know. "The six-eighteen is delayed, Madam," was all the satisfaction I got—and I knew that already. The chauffeurs always took it more philosophically. The Master wasn't their husband-only the boss, and one boss was much like another if the check came promptly on pay day.

Just as I was leaving the train my eye lit on one of the car cards-"Those who work or shop near the Long 

Island Railroad find it Faster, More Comfortable, More Convenient." "Than what?” I thought to myself and decided a coffee milk shake might sweeten my disposition. Jim is a saint to commute on this line every day and not go around biting babies in their cradles. 145

When I went in to buy the things that Sally wanted for Benjy I was waited on by a most starchy and efficient young lady who told me she was the nurse in charge of Infant's Wear. I read off the list to her and she smiled tenderly. "My dear, that won't be nearly enough of the really needed things-is this your first baby?" "My fifth," I said, rapidly figuring Art and Peg and Chunky and Cindy. At least experience should count and the absolute statement of fact is often so uninteresting.

She came unstarched and became quite human. Her sales talk vanished, "Oh, that's different. My sister has six and they're cute as bugs. I'm crazy about the baby -she's two and so smart you wouldn't believe it." We had quite a chat and compared notes on mine and hers and matched each other, story for story, on the brightness and cuteness of our own. "Hope you have an easy labor-you know, short and snappy," she said as we parted. I hope so too. Wish to heaven I could have this baby for Sally-it would be much easier to go through it myself than to stand around helplessly and be miserable with her. I've always contended, in spite of the funny stories about young fathers, that they have the worst of it. I've had babies and I've stood around waiting for other people to have babies. I've had major operations and have been just "one of the family of the patient." Give me the role of being the patient any time! Then you're either fully occupied or unconscious. If luck is only with us and Jim and Jud are on deck. Either one or both have that nice steady assurance that makes you feel they 146

could stop a bomb. But Jim is in and out of town so much now and nobody knows where Jud will be by then. Thinking of pioneer women, alone in the wilderness with only an Indian squaw to help things along, doesn't do me a bit of good. Babies are grand and I'm all for them, but the process could be improved. 

May 14.-Never saw such a spring for abundance and beauty! It almost seems as though God were trying to make up for the destruction and horror. Every flowering shrub is a mass of blossoms and every blossom larger and clearer in color. The white Le Moyne lilac, on the south side of the house, is tapered from top to bottom, with fragrant plumes that almost take your breath away.

Sally was sitting beside it on the porch this morning knitting a pale blue sweater for Benjy and I would have given anything to have a color photograph of her. She has always been lovely looking but, it seems to me, here lately she is more lovely than ever-a sort of serene and quiet strength in her face and happiness in her eyes.

I left her there on the porch and took a leisurely walk around our home place. Cascades of bloom everywhere. 

The bridal wreath in snowy foam drooping over the top garden and the drive, wisteria in lavender and deep orchid over the front door. How I love that front entrance with the many times it has opened to our friends, the friends of our children, the Christmas wreath that means to us welcome and tradition and closeness to each other. Yes, our house has sentimental value-and what could be more priceless? 

May 18.-Read in the paper this morning the firsthand accounts of two American correspondents who have been prisoners of the Germans and Italians and 147

are now on their way home. Both of them said that the experience had its funny moments and that their guards couldn't understand the childishness and craziness of Americans who could laugh and play makeshift baseball and fix candied orange peel over smuggled-in hot plates. These particular prisoners, being favored beyond most, were housed in hotels part of the time but under the constant eye of the guards-"First you have the fidgets, then you have fits of anger and, finally, you become just plain bored." The police protested they were not solemn enough and were a bad example for the natives who had been ordered to maintain a serious mien because of the war. The prisoners replied with the fact that they could laugh because they knew the 

United States would come back with a knockout punch.

Finally the police gave up and told the natives the 

Americans were plain crazy. I don't suppose any two nationalities really understand each other but, for that matter, our own Mayflower passengers would be just as upset by our laughter and wise cracking. But the spunk and endurance is here all the same. Some of the individual stories coming from Bataan and Corregidor make you awfully proud to be an American. I don't know why people talk so nobly about "our sacrifices" when they really mean giving up a little gas and sugar and silk stockings and getting along on less income and more outgo. I sometimes think a few carefully placed bombs might do us all a lot of good and put things in better proportion. I still have faith in our meeting a real test when it comes but we haven't met it yet. 

May 20.-Cindy and Ralph Waldo Emerson would have been boon companions I'm sure. His essay on Self- 148

Reliance expresses her point of view. This morning she had vanished from the earth in the space of ten minutes. Sally went in one direction and I in another with 

Chunky searching the home grounds and hide-aways. 

Half an hour later we were still searching and calling and getting more frantic by the minute. We thought of traffic and we thought of kidnapers-this house is so big that it looks a lot more important and prosperous than it is as though somebody in it might dig up a few thousand-which they couldn't. Cindy is such a friendly little mortal that she'd be likely to trot off with anybody. I called Beth and Marcia on the phone and was about to notify the police when Chunky came shouting into the house- "I found Cindy-and she hasn't any clothes on! She's visiting at Whisbick's grandmother's house." He led us to this mysterious residence-under a wild cherry tree in the little patch of woods across the road. There sat 

Cindy, as calm and smiling as you please, with her clothes in a little heap beside her and her favorite doll in her arms. "Whisbick's grandmother is asleep in her kitchen," she remarked. "Her kitchen is nicer than ours because the floor is cleaner. And she lets childrens eat all the cake they want." 

Sally and I started to laugh from sheer relief at finding Cindy safe and in one piece, but Chunky didn't approve of such light mindedness. "His grandmother is sleeping and you'll wake her up. But you should tell Cindy people don't go out in the street without any clothes on." Chunky is a great 149

one for the conventions. But Cindy is a rugged individualist. "This people does!" she announced and went on dressing her doll in a Dorothy Lamour sort of drapery made out of what was once one of our bridal guest towels complete with blue birds and twining pink roses.

I never could see any sense in the dabby little things and never used them. Think I'll take the rest and make them into tray cloths. I'll need some tray fixings when 

Sally gets home from the hospital. That's one time when you enjoy frills and being fussed over. 

May 21.-While I was doing the dishes this morning

I looked out of the window and saw a Baltimore oriole flash its orange and black through the opening between two of the upper branches of the apple tree. The blossoms have gone and the orioles have come. His demure little Mrs. soon came bustling along and, as I watched them, they put the finishing touches on their nest. There it hung, swaying in the warm spring air and reminding me of the crocheted straw bags that the women carry for shopping down in Caracas. I brought one home from that trip and it still holds the family darning. "'Holds' is right!" says Jim, because I'm never known to get at the darning until he and Art tell me they are down to the last pair in their dressers. I've heard of women who do the mending systematically and even boast how they carefully darn all thin places on socks before they break through—but I'm not like that. Anything that I enjoy never gets put off, but anything I dislike finds me quick as a wink with an alibi as to why some other time would be much better. I'm sure my 

Pennsylvania Dutch grandmother would take me for 150

a weak character but she gets her innings when I go on a regular binge of scrubbing out drawers and pantry shelves just every so often. The only difference is that she did it regularly and as a matter of duty. I do it irregularly and because it gives me such a virtuous feeling of accomplishment, so my way is more fun-for me.

The pair of orioles would have suited her exactly, because they are so businesslike and tending to their own affairs. The Mr. seems a little gayly dressed for a working man with fatherhood just around the corner, but his children don't have to have tonsils removed and glasses fitted and I.Q.'s taken, so perhaps it isn't such a responsibility. I must remember to show Cindy and 

Chunky the orioles' nest. 

May 23.-Jim gets back tonight and, someway, everything I do seems to have more meaning and reason. 

When he's gone I go through the motions of keeping house and getting meals but none of it is so very important. I think of that old Scotch song that Peg loved to have me sing her when she was little-"For there's little pleasure in the hoose when my good mon's awa." 

I made cookies with less sugar and more chopped raisins,

I made a graham cracker pie with a lime gelatine filling, I made glorified hamburgers with Spanish rice and bought our first bunch of asparagus this season. It has been so high, but this morning a new crop must have arrived, because it was full and fresh and green and the price way down. Jim likes it with browned butter and a little lemon juice and chopped chives.

Laurie stopped in on her way to the hospital and she looked like Santa Claus ahead of time. Her bright red jacket and white blouse were a perfect background- 151

she was chirky as a jaybird in the morning-how those 

Uncle Remus sayings do stick in your head-my head.

She was loaded down with boxes and bundles and dumped them on the hall bench before she followed me out to the kitchen. That high stool by the sink is a sociable spot on which to perch and visit, but Laurie, as she used to be, never went beyond the straightest chair in the living room. This time she perched up on the stool and helped herself to a cooky. "Do you know what?" she began. I couldn't guess what so she told me. "I'm going to give a birthday party! There's a little girl named Barney and she's eight years old and never had a birthday. So this time we're going to do it right with pink candles and her name on the cake and pink caps for all the children in the Ward and a man to play an accordion and sing funny songs. The Supervisor gave permission yesterday because we haven't any very sick children on the Ward now. I have presents for all the children and it's going to be wonderful-even Dr. Stevens said he might look in if he was around. He seems quite interested in that 

Ward." I saw the sudden flush in her face when she said, "Even Dr. Stevens." I wish I could tell Bruce to turn off the charm around Laurie but tending to other people's business is a hazardous occupation. 

May 25.-The paper today is a confusing mixture of the gloom people are feeling, as the war goes on, and the enthusiasm and optimism that the daring of Doolittle and his men in the bombing of Tokio gives us all. 

It's so easy, back here in comparative safety, to shout for a second front and question critically why on earth 152

we don't see some action. We'll see plenty before this is over. 

May 26.-A telegram from Art this morning woke me up to the fact that I have a birthday-"Many happy returns. Don't forget our birthday song and don't feed the elephant." The birthday song was perfectly clear because he and Peg and Jim always sing to me, “Oh, the old gray mare-she-ain't-what she used to be, ain't -what she used to be, ain't-what she used to be. The old gray mare-she-ain't what she used to be-many long years ago." It has been one of our nice durable family jokes, and we've all laughed over it as the years rolled on and I lost my girlish figure. The telegram came while we were at breakfast and I passed it to Jim. "What does he mean about feeding the elephant?” "Well, I sort of thought we ought to take Cindy and Chunky to see the circus because it might not be here next year and it's your birthday and they'd enjoy the circus. So I wrote Art about it and that's what he means." To see Jim so flustered was really funny. We haven't seen a circus for years-not since our own two were at the peanut and cotton candy stage and then Jim and I enjoyed the sights and the smells as much as they did. I wonder what it will be like with the whole thing streamlined and glamorized by Norman 

Bel Geddes. I remember his Futurama at the Fair and my only sensation was that I felt very thankful I wouldn't be here in the year two thousand or whenever all the wonders were to take place. Living, as he pictured it, was much too planned and efficient to be interesting-imagine apple trees under glass-a whole orchard of them! 153

May 27.-I take it all back. The circus was colossial, stupendious, magnificent-and then some. It was like the Ziegfeld Follies combined with the old-time circus and then magnified a hundred times. The grand entry, with all our American holidays, gave me a thrill I won't forget for forty more birthdays. The girls were so pretty and fresh looking, the costumes bright and new, and, when the pageant of Christmas ended it up, my eyes were filled with tears of happiness. In between peanuts Chunky kept saying, "This is the best circus 

I ever, ever saw." "You never saw one before, Chunky." "I don't care-it's the best one I ever, ever saw.'

Cindy's enthusiasm matched his but she kept picking out, "The prettiest lady in the world! And I want my 

Mommy could make me a dress like that." I kept wishing Sally had come, but she went over to spend the evening with Beth because she thought Benjy was too young to stand the long trip and the excitement. I keep thinking of things to tell her about it and the children join in whenever I forget the slightest detail. "Tell her about the man and the lady that whirled up high in the airplane," says Chunky and I see again that whirling blur of spangled white with the spotlight on it at the topmost height of the building. It took us all a little time to catch our breath when that was over. "Tell her about the elephants that danced," urges 

Cindy. The elephant ballet sways for me again. I never saw such grace as there was in those young mountains of weight. The little Hindu girl, who guided one of the largest of them through the motions of the dance, was fascinating. The motions of her body, as she danced 154

before him, slowly getting him into perfect rhythm.

I agree with Chunky that it's the best circus I ever, ever saw and, if I never see another one, I'll still have something to tell my great grandchildren about.

The city looked very strange when we stepped out of the brightly lighted building. The dimout has made all the streets seem weird and deserted. None of the big advertising signs are on any longer and no illuminated windows. The crowds were as gay as ever and service men in uniform everywhere you looked. Some of the young English Air Cadet boys brought a lump to my throat. They looked so very young and so far from home, but they were laughing and going four abreast, arm in arm, with our own boys-Navy, Army, Marines. Cindy, from her sleepy perch on Jim's shoulder, threw kisses to all branches and all nationalities and they waved and saluted and called back to her.

Chunky saluted right and left and told everybody in sight that he had been to see the circus. Our walk to the train was quite a triumphal march and we finally climbed aboard complete with two weary but blissful children and an assortment of balloons, clown dolls,

Cracker Jack boxes and tired feet-but it was worth it. 

May 29.-Had Aline and her sister, whose name turns out to be one of my favorites-Nancy-over to dinner tonight and we four women seemed to get along perfectly. Aline and Nancy play the same kind of bridge that Sally and I do-stop and go style. First you stop and talk and then you go ahead and play-then you stop and talk again. The dinner was good and they asked for the recipe for the shoulder of lamb stuffed with brown rice and herbs and the rhubarb Betty with 155

frozen ginger cream. There is something about that combination of the warm honey and butter and rhubarb baking slowly with the soft bread crumbs that makes you feel at home the minute you sniff it in the air. And the frosty, tangy ginger cream belongs right with it. 

Aline ate hers down to the last amber edge of the glass dish. "It's marvelous! Bob will love it-he's crazy about desserts." My face must have registered the surprise and kind of shock I felt-Jim always says I couldn't fool a two-year-old when I try to conceal my emotions. 

Aline was silent for a minute. Then she looked up. "I wouldn't say this to everyone-they wouldn't understand. But I've never given a minute to really believing he's gone. I've cried a good many times and I've felt despair-but deep inside me I know Bob is alive somewhere. I know he'll come back-someday. Missing in action isn't the end of our chapter." 

How desperately I hope she is right-such faith and conviction must mean something. I'm sure I'd know, one way or the other, if anything happened to Jim. 

When people are very close, distance doesn't count. 

May 31.-I woke up so early this morning that I thought I might just as well get up-even if it was Sunday. I'm glad I did because the garden was perfect and our one gray rabbit was sitting on the top terrace as though he were waiting for me. The flowering crab was in full bloom, with hundreds of shaded pink and rose blossoms as delicate as porcelain filling the air with their perfume. Along the wall by the drive the tulips stood in slender grace with their colors muted with a film of fine dew and along the border in front of them 156

the English primroses showed golden against the rosette of furry green leaves that poked their way through the masses of heavenly blue forget-me-nots. Things are always later in that bed because it gets so little sun, but that makes the season last longer and it's early May by the rock wall and early June on the sunny top of the terrace. According to the books I should thin out some of the plants, but I can't bear to touch them when they're in bloom and, when they aren't, the beds don't look so full. I guess our garden gets brought up by ear just as we brought up the children. But both garden and children have turned out very satisfactorily at that. 

The books don't know everything. 157

June 

THREE DAYS' LEAVE-I WORK OFF THE BLUES- TOMORROW IS ANOTHER DAY-EXPERIMENT IN CHILD TRAINING-PEG BRINGS ANOTHER BRANCH OF THE SERVICE, AND A GOOD TIME WAS HAD BY ALL-ONLY A REGISTRATION BUT WHAT NEXT? 

June 1.-The call to duty evidently has no answering number on my mental switchboard. I wake up like a nervous canary at the first ray of light on a Sunday morning when I have nothing particular to do and then on Monday, when I have a million things that must be done, I oversleep. When Jim is home I am on schedule like a westbound flyer on the Grand Central. I believe he must have a clock attachment inside him that goes off at convenient intervals because he never oversleeps or misses a train or forgets to send checks at the proper time. Days after they're due and the bank would be putting us out in the road with our household belongings I say brightly, “Oh, Jim, shouldn't we send a check for the mortgage payment or something?" "I mailed it on the twelfth-it was due on the fifteenth." "Oh." I'm always so surprised and he's always so patient. It never shocks me when I read in the paper that some man has, at last, beat his wife. But it does astonish me that more of them don't-women are maddening creatures and I'm one of them. 

Well, my watch said ten o'clock when I came to this morning-it's always fast or slow-and I flew into 158

the shower and out again. Like a fireman's bride I jumped into my clothes and down to the kitchen. That clock said eight but the blamed thing had stopped. I called the operator and her undulating tones came right back at me. "The ti-um is ex-act-ully ni-un-twenty-two." No sign of Sally or the youngsters and the kitchen was as slick and shined up as though the mother cat had just licked it for the day. I went out in the yard and there the three of them sat under the big wild cherry tree eating breakfast picnic fashion and Chunky was telling a wild tale between bites of bacon roll and gulps of milk. He looked up at me with those clear gray eyes of his, that seem as though they could see right through you and button on the other side, and went on with his story. "And then I said, 'You're a big stiff and you can't pull that stuff on me'-and he was anawful big boy, too-and then I hit him and he fell over all dead and he broke his arm, too!" "Did he break it clear off?" asked Cindy. "Clear off-and then he died." "All over he died?" asked Cindy-she has a great interest in detail and this was really good. "All over-and then his other arm broke off because

I hit him so hard because he was a big stiff-and he was anawful big boy-he was nine." 

Cindy smiled on him approvingly, "I love you, Chunky-tell me about it again some more." How I smile when I read about the tender hearts of the little ones. And what fun I get out of watching these two 159

play up to each other. Chunky adores showing off and 

Cindy is a perfect appreciator.

June 3.-Card from Art this morning-"Home to night. Texas on Saturday. This is the life and I don't mean maybe. Pile on the fudge frosting and don't spare the horses. Hope you've cleaned the junk out of my room. I sure hate a mess. Art." I never expected to live long enough to have Art even suspicious of the fact that his room has been a mess for sixteen years. It suited him and what was wrong with it anyway. second-hand junk shop was a spotless laboratory compared to it, and he worked and slept and dreamed among the debris perfectly happy and at peace. Maybe this Air Corps training has made a new man out of him--but I hope not too new. Am I glad I've been saving a little of the sugar each week!
