That fudge icing is going to be so thick you'll have to look twice to find the cake. I was so excited when I read the card that I shouted down the road to Milton, the mail carrier, "Art's coming home!" "Swell!" he called and walked back to the house. I could see that he was bursting at the seams with some news of his own. "They made my boy a Sergeant and his mother's still crying because she's so proud-women are funny, aren't they? But it's pretty good for him being only nineteen. We didn't get any letter from him for seven weeks and three days and then he called long distance from across the ocean. The General must have stood right beside him because he couldn't say anything hardly and on this end wasn't much better, but he did tell us he was made a Sergeant and that he was having a swell time in a forty-dollar-a-day room 160

at a swell hotel and he had three days' leave and the

Government was footing the bill because they owned the hotel. Pretty good for a kid being only nineteen, isn't it?" "It's wonderful," I said. And I watched Milton go trudging up the road again with his one shoulder weighted down by the heavy sack that he has carried rain and shine, hot weather and cold, ever since we have been out here and long before. One shoulder was lower than the other and his back is a little stooped but there was a jauntiness about him just the same. His boy is having the adventure he missed and seeing the world he only dreamed about. How we all live in our children and hope for them the steps beyond those we have climbed. I worry over Art and I worry over Peg and wonder what lies ahead-yet at the same time I thrill over seeing them step out and mature and meet life with the casual acceptance I have never attained. They don't struggle against things as I did at their age-they simply take them as they come and no favors asked or expected. 

June 6.-Art left tonight and there is an emptiness in my heart and emotions that are hard to get used to. 

Three days of him here with his foolishness and joking and his new seriousness and absorption and now a blank-Texas seems so very far away, even though I can see it on a map and read the area and population and chief products and largest cities. What will it be like when I don't even know where he is or how he is for weeks at a time? I keep thinking of Milton and his, "We didn't get any letter from him for seven weeks and three days." The way he had so carefully kept 161

count of those extra three days told me such a lot of the endless hours in which they had waited. That oldtime, mournful poem keeps running through my head- "For men must work and women must weep though the harbor bar be moaning." Those wives of seafaring men knew what it was to weep and to watch for month after dreary month of hardship and no news of either safety or disaster. Their tears must have come many times when the nights were filled with storm, but they kept on with their jobs of caring for their children and their meager homes. They gave us a heritage of courage and will power and fortitude. That ringing line in 

Ballad for Americans—“To keep the faith with those who went before." I must shake myself out of this emptiness and depression and dig into my job of making this home for the people I love. I've got to work and not weep. But it isn't going to be easy to forget the little boy who was Artie and think of the man he has become with all that means in times like these. I know he'll measure up to all the faith Jim and I have in him. 

But will I measure up to the faith they both have in me? Perhaps the answer-the whole answer-is in the phrase from the marriage ceremony of the Friends— "And, with Divine assistance, to be unto him a faithful and loving wife so long as we both shall live." I don't believe in prayer for gaining what I want-but I do believe in it most deeply as a source, limitless and sustaining, for spiritual strength to meet whatever is in my pattern of life. So I don't pray for Art's safe return. 

I only pray that, for all of us who love him, we can face the future unvanquished whatever the outcome. 162

June 8.-I lit into a job this morning that I might have known would take up half the day when I should have finished it in two hours-but I had an elegant time doing it-the dusting of the books and cleaning of the shelves. There I sat on the floor, with piles of books around me, and dipped into one after another of my favorites while the hands of the clock did a marathon and it was lunchtime before I knew it. I woke up to the fact when the noon siren went off and Cindy and 

Chunky stood, like two accusing judges, saying, in chorus, "We're hungry and we want to eat!" I had been far away in peaceful country meadows and woods with David Grayson and his Adventures in Contentment. I had been thrilling to the human drama of that final scene in Conrad's Victory and drifting along the rhythm and beauty of music in words of Keats and 

Shelley and the carved ivory perfection of the descriptions of Lafcadio Hearn. What would he think now of the Japan which he interpreted with such exquisite understanding of the philosophy and psychology and way of life of an alien race?

I pulled myself out of the barricade of books and went out to the kitchen to make toasted peanut butter and tomato sandwiches and big glasses of chocolate milk. I heaped the green glass dishes with the reddest strawberries I could find in the two boxes-the rest will go in shortcake for tonight. I fixed four dishes and 

Cindy looked them over. "Where is Whisbick's? He came home from her grandmother's today and she's very hungry." Whether the hunger belongs to the grandmother or the him-her I don't know-but it certainly complicates meal getting 163

to keep in mind an unseen guest who is like the little man who wasn't there. Chunky was not to be outdone so he brought up the subject of Hoskin's lunch. "Come on, Hosky, old boy, old boy!" he called and there stood our hero with his big brown eyes framed by the drapery of his long droopy ears. I know, at his age, one meal a day is plenty and he'll get fat and dull if he eats too much. But what can you do when he stands so patiently, too much of a gentleman to beg really, but never taking his eyes off of you and every mouthful you seem to be enjoying? He didn't care for strawberries, thank you, but did very well on tid-bits from our sandwiches with a chunk of the cold lamb for dessert.

Lunch went so uneventfully and harmoniously that 

I might have known it couldn't last. Cindy ate like a perfect little lady and said her "Pwese" and "Sank you" at all the proper times. Chunky ate silently and methodically as usual-light chat is not his idea of anything to combine with the far more important business of eating. 

All at once, for no reason that Sally or I could figure out, Cindy reached over and gave Chunky a push that toppled him like a fat Santa Claus. Life was probably just a little too dull and monotonous and she craved excitement. "You're a hansbergel!" she announced and gave him another shove when he had almost righted himself. 

Even Chunky couldn't take this-so he gave her a shove. "You're a bawling finger!" The battle was on with no holds barred. 

Sally grabbed Cindy, I grabbed Chunky-I don't know what the modern books on child psychology 164

would have advised-probably sweet reason or a handsoff policy but we had no time to look it up. Another minute or two and they would have torn each other limb from limb without a doubt. How two youngsters who worship each other so, most of the time, can turn loose in a split second and fight like little demons is hard to figure-but there it is. We sent them both up to their rooms to cool down and think over the error of their ways. Chunky marched up the stairs without a backward glance or a single word to even admit he recognized our presence. But Cindy went stamping on every step and shouting through her sobs. "Nice as I am to you-and you kreat me like this—if

I was a mother-and had a child-and kreated her like this-I'd be ashamed!" Sally and I were weak with laughing. When we finally gathered ourselves together we tiptoed out in the hall. Not a sound-and then, from the two bedrooms on the front of the house, we heard two stage whispers. "Cindy!" "Chunky!" "You're a hansbergel!" "You're a bawling finger!" Giggles from both sides. 

They started to play telephone. The smoke of battle drifted away as though it had never been. Probably all my fault because I was reading instead of doing my housewifely duty by the books. Therefore lunch was late and tempers were near the surface. Even Jim is a trifle on the touchy side when he has to wait too long for a meal-and I'm not fit to live with when I'm starying and no food in sight. 165

June 10.-George brought around some fine looking cauliflower plants to put in our garden and stayed until dark to help me plant them. He thinks we are going to have enough string beans to feed the Russian army and he told me all about the gentle art of staking up the tomatoes to a single stalk so that they bear better, ripen sooner, and don't get bogged down on the ground to rot. If our plants produce half as much as George seems to think they will I'll have to work up a little barter and exchange with the neighbors. I've already planned the swapping of the use of Beth's pressure cooker for her use of our washing machine. The whole neighborhood is getting equipment-minded, and the general rule seems to be to never buy anything until you've called up everybody within range to see if you can borrow it.

Sally has already had the offer of a bottle sterilizer, a play pen, a carriage, and three bed jackets.

We're working out a scheme, too, for dividing up the small fry for the summer-six mothers, all with twoto five-year-olds, are each going to take the whole bunch for one morning each week. It's bound to have some disadvantages, but it will certainly help to know where the little darlings are from one minute to the next and that, for the most part, they are safe and happy. On account of the gas there won't be much going to the beach this year and we'll have to cultivate the joys of home. Several neighbors are building the outdoor fireplace they've been planning for years and never got around to before because most of us used to climb into our cars and go somewhere else nearly every 

Saturday and Sunday. Wonder what this change will mean to Jones Beach and the summer resort sections? 1 166

The tourist cabins are in for a lonesome spell, too, I imagine. It's lucky for us that my brainstorm of a few years back didn't get any farther than talk. I can remember the evenings when Jim and I would sit sketching our notions for a small house and a filling station where we'd make a living and get away from the commuting and high costs. He was going to sell gas, Art was going to do repair jobs, and I was going to bake gingerbread and serve it with ice cold country milk. 

Peg fancied the idea of an herb garden from which she could make herb vinegars and teas and jellies to sell to the carriage trade. I'll bet that Peg, in a becoming flowered smock, could sell anything to anybody-she's born with the gift of friendliness. If Phil stays in the Navy after the war, and she says he wants to, she should be a success as a Navy wife. Peg could make a home in a packing box and entertain the Admiral's wife without a qualm. I never thought I'd be one of those doting mothers who think their own children such wondersbut here I am. 

June 11.-I might have known I'd hear from Peg today. Whenever any member of the family is particularly on my mind they either appear on the front doorstep, unannounced, or a letter arrives. 

CUTEY PIE (and you are cute!) 

You'll probably have a couple of good weeps when you read this letter and then write and say that you are so glad things worked out this way for us and that you'll jump a plane and run out to visit us (just like 

Mrs. Roosevelt!). Anyway-here's the way it is.

Phil is mad enough to spit, and I know exactly how he feels, but I'm pretty happy over it for the time 167

being. I know, sooner or later, he'll wear the Navy down and they'll give in for the sake of peace and quiet. The powers that be, meaning his C.O., have discovered Phil is a born teacher, which is not his idea of helping to win the war. So he has been assigned to duty as an instructor from here to there but mostly on the west coast. "Join the Navy and see the U.S.A.,” says 

Phil disgustedly. So I'm walking out on the summer nutrition course and following the fleet as long as I can. 

Joan has a job all lined up for me near Los Angeles where I can make sort of headquarters and the chance of seeing Phil once in a while will be a whole lot better than they would be if I stayed parked here. The job is assistant dietician in one of the big factories that is working on war orders. At least I know enough not to put boiled rice, mashed potato, and bread pudding in one meal. In ordinary times I wouldn't have a chance at a job like that unless I had a Home Ec degree but, right now, there's an awful shortage of trained people and I'm not as dumb as some. Guess I forgot to tell you that you might as well take Joan off your worry listshe's doing all right and her cockeyed theories on marriage are put up in moth balls. She fell, like a bombed building, for a Marine officer she met at the outgoing male canteen we ran at the railroad station at college. 

It was sure a direct hit if I ever saw one. She gave him a doughnut and he gave her a smile-and it was all over. 

Don't suppose you can imagine Joan babbling but that is what she did. He was in town just two hours and a month later she followed him to Los Angeles and they were married. Those boys certainly work fast from the halls of Montezuma to the shores of Tripoli. He's had his orders now and gone "Somewhere in the Pacific area," and Joan is working, of all places, in a Day Nursery for the children of women in Defense plants. 

She loves it. And I love you and will be home for a 1 1 168

few days before I start on my westward trek. I'll let you know when. "I doubt it," says you, knowing my lifelong habit of doing things on a sudden. Adoringly,

P.S. Tell that handsome brother of mine that I treasure his last letter most highly but would like a companion piece for museum use. Tell Dad my apple pie is almost as good as yours now-though I know he'd never admit it.

I did have a couple of good weeps-and I am glad for 

Peg, but I can imagine how Phil hates shore duty. He's too full of energy and fight to be kept a school teacher for long. 

June 12.-When Claudine came to help clean today 

I decided the time had come for the brass and copper to take a summer vacation in a box in the attic. Wonder how soon the salvage will get to the things you really love and treasure? I'd hate to give these things up, for they have so much value beyond their money cost, but nothing really matters except winning this war and getting back to sane living without fear. I don't ask for security because, once completely attained, life would be very dull and lose all its adventure. But a chance for everyone to live an ordinary, simple everyday existence does seem a human right that we've got to work toward someway. Most people, in all countries, ask so little really-a home, a garden, children, work to do, and enough payment for it to leave a small margin. It's the greed and ambition of the leaders which stir them up to want more and more until war seems the only way to get it. When will the little people of the earth wake and start to think? up 169

June 16.-One of the women, at the Health Association meeting this afternoon, passed around a note she had just received from her ten-year-old away at school. 

A four-act drama that would have given credit to the old Greeks.

DEAREST MUMMY: 

I laid my glasses on the bed. I went for a walk. I came back. I sat on them. Lovingly,

ELLEN 

I recalled the note which Art had sent once from camp. 

DEAR MUM:

I am sorry to tell you that I tore my raincoat and now it is lost. The other rubber is lost too. Respekt your son Arthur. 

Marcia told us about the time their darling daughter was bitten by a snake at camp and all the doctor could get out of her by way of description was, "It was a cute little snake."

We had to stop laughing and talking and exchanging stories when the meeting was finally called to order and the speaker started in, but it was good to relax and enjoy each other. The town is certainly filling up with a lot of new people, and we're getting acquainted fast because there are so many meetings and so much to be done that everybody pitches in-at least, nearly everybody. Some few refuse to do anything and think the rest of us are crazy to take things so seriously. I'll bet they are the very ones who will call for help the loudest if the need comes. The Health Nurse wants more old 170

blankets fixed into bed covers for next winter and anything in the line of wool clothing that can be made over. 

Seems funny to be thinking of cold and snow and insufficient fuel in the middle of these lovely sunny days, but she knows, from long experience, that you have to work ahead before the need is right on top of you. She's going to have no easy job with so many of the doctors going and the nurses being grabbed up as soon as they graduate. Bruce is signed up and waiting for final papers to go through. If they'll only let him stay until after 

Benjy arrives! Which proves, once again, how utterly unselfish I am not. Sally and Jud like him so much and have such confidence in him, and Jim and I would rather go under ether, looking at his face, than any other doctor in the world. He has pulled us all through a lot of tough times. 

June 17.-Our cherry tree is simply loaded this year and I'm up to my ears with making spiced cherry jam to eat with duck this winter, canned cherries to make into pies and steamed puddings and tarts. The plain canned ones I'm trying with lots less sugar and hope they keep well. I can figure out how to sweeten them when winter comes-there must be a lot of Scarlett 

O'Hara in my makeup because I'm always deciding to think about difficult things-tomorrow. There's a lot to be said for that philosophy because tomorrow is often better, and sometimes worse, than you figured. But tomorrow is hours away-a night's sleep away-and anything may happen in between. So often, when I get myself in a dither over the things I imagine may come to pass, I think of the calm and soothing words of my favorite Marcus Aurelius: "Let not future things disturb 171

thee, for thou wilt come to them, if it shall be necessary, having with thee the same reason which now thou usest for present things." I can never in a million years come to his serenity, but it's soothing to the spirit to dip into his sayings, here and there, and come away with a shred of his thought clinging to your troubled mind.

The Minute men, and women, began their house-tohouse canvass yesterday to get people to sign up for ten per cent or better War Stamp and Bond pledges.

Jim signed up long ago at the office, but I took another pledge and now I'll start to figure out just how I am going to manage to meet it. I know there are lots of small corners I can cut-and gladly. Nothing matters now except winning and ending this war and getting back to sanity and peace. The woman who called at our house was sixtyish and not too husky looking, but she had been trampling up one street and down the other since eight this morning. I finally persuaded her to take enough time out to stop and have lunch with Sally and me and the children and the stories she told us brought a feeling of confidence in our fellow citizens. "No one has said 'No' and a lot of the people I have called on haven't much to spare either, but they're doing all they can and doing it willingly. Some of them haven't had jobs for a long time and are just starting to work again, so there are piled-up bills. They don't say much but you can see how they feel-it's their country, their boys-even the small children want to sign a pledge for a ten-cent stamp every week. I sort of dreaded this job after I had volunteered but now I hate 1 172

to stop because I'm seeing people as I've never seen them before." 

June 19.-I had a lovely and enlightening experience in child training this morning that should give food for thought to the ladies and gentlemen who write so glibly of how to guide and understand the little darlings. The currants were ripe-everything is ripe all at once, it seems-so I started to pick them with the bright idea of how character forming it would be to have the children help me. At my age I should have had better sense and done the job myself, letting the character forming fall as it will. I read in a book somewhere that young children love to imitate the grown-up busy world and all they ask is equipment suited to their size. So I dutifully burrowed around until I found two small pails for Chunky and Cindy-told them how much fun we were going to have picking the pretty currants for the lovely jelly for next winter-I really put quite a good deal of drama into my selling talk. I showed them just how to pick currants according to the latest and most efficient method, and how we never, never, picked any leaves because that would spoil the lovely jelly. I sounded like a perfect sap, because currant picking is really quite a job, but they stood there seemingly entranced. When I finished my little speech Cindy looked up at me, with her eyes sort of sidewise, and remarked "Your dress is pretty," and Chunky began running around in circles making weird noises and shouting, "I'm a power diver!" Well, we started in on the picking and, in about five minutes, they were both exhausted. They had to lie down and rest a little while. 

Then Cindy had the bright idea of making a wreath 173

out of currant clusters and playing she was a queen.

Chunky tried to arrange the wreath more to his liking and the nice red juice mingled with Cindy's yellow curls until she looked like a Turner sunset effect. I urged them back on the job, still thinking of that book and the great advantages of training the young in useful industry. But the man who wrote the book didn't know these two conscientous objectors. They individually, and collectively, had a headache. Their stomachs felt funny. They had to go to the bathroom. And they needed a drink. I picked the currants! 

June 20.-Canned currant juice this morning, without benefit of sugar, and I'll make the jelly gradually as I can save up for it. I couldn't see any sense in going down to the rationing board to ask for extra sugar for canning. From all the grapevine information I get there is going to be an extra supply later on anyway. The more I read the papers and the more I listen to absolutely confidential information, my dear, the less I know about anything connected with the war. But Congress seems to be in just as much of a mental fog, so why should I complain? It's simply too big and complicated for anyone to absorb and understand-just go along from day to day and do the best you can. 

June 22.-Art graduated tonight-a star before his name on the program led your eye to a footnote at the bottom-"Left school to join the Armed Forces of the 

United States." There were ten such stars before graduates' names and their parents were all there to wish them well across the empty miles. I know how hard it was for all of them, because I know how hard it was for Jim and me. On the surface smiles, waves of the 174

hand, seemingly wrapt attention, and, underneath, a sense of strangeness as though we were living through a dream of something that never happened. The program went off like clockwork and I couldn't forget my own graduation with its endless onward and upward speeches by visiting dignitaries. Not a single speech this time by any of the faculty or any invited speaker. The times I have raved on and on about progressive education and its faults and failings-I had to take them all back. For these youngsters were self-confident, perfectly at ease before four hundred people, and the forum they conducted on "How the War Affects the High School Graduate" had more real thought in it than you'd find in a group of adults. Their own orchestra and their own solo performers and not a hitch in the whole thing. I choked up when they began to sing -I always do. Someway, you look at youngsters and you wonder what's ahead of them-what bumps that you can't kiss away, what griefs that a cup of cocoa and a handful of cookies can't ease, what fears that the comfort of your arms around them won't banish. Their little years are so short and their big years so long that you wish you had been a hundred times more patient, a thousand times more understanding. I don't believe any parent ever regrets having done too much and so many wish, with all their hearts, that they had done more. I think of the play of Liliom and his second chance at coming back to earth-only to do the same things, make the same mistakes he did before. Perhaps we'd all do that and, therefore, there is no second chance-we shall pass this way but once-Lord help us to be kinder. 175

June 23.-Jim went out to a Defense meeting tonight and Sally and I sat at home reading Past Imperfect by Ilka Chase. She certainly is a witty and observant lady, but we both felt thankful we didn't number her among our intimate, or otherwise, friends. It must be a trifle upsetting to run across yourself in cold black type so neatly dissected and exposed to the public. Miss Chase seems to have compassion and fondness for the little people of the world and no mercy on the big ones. 

The way she can unstuff a stuffed shirt is masterly. We liked her comments on the places and people she has known in her travels, and she has a faculty for making you feel the cold damp of a gloomy English hotel at the end of a weary day and the warmth and charm and timelessness of Venice. She gave me Gondola fever all over again and, someday, I'm going to see for myself.

Jim and I would have such a good time. But that's true of any place we go together-anything we do together. 

You can't look ahead these days as the tempo of the war speeds up and everyone's life is at loose ends. But you have to dream ahead into the future in order to look straight at the present.

June 24.-Peg landed in town this morning-bag and baggage-and left tonight with more bags and baggage.

Anyway, I did get a chance to have a real look at her and assure myself that she seems perfectly well and certainly happy. I am such a conceited idiot that I'm sure my family will die by inches if I'm not right there to fuss over them. Guess I'd better get over that notion -at least for the duration. 

But, "It's just the mother in you," says Art, "and nothing can be done about it." 176

The mother in me rushed right to the surface this morning when I heard Peg's voice on the phone. By this time I should have become used to the fact that there is only one rule about Peg-there ain't no rule. "Hello, Mums!" and, before I could get my breath, "How about lunch for a couple of refugees?" "A couple of " "Just me and a little branch of the service I picked up on the train. An Army Captain with the sweetest wife in the world and three of the smartest kids on earth. He has their pictures to prove it and he's lonesome as a goat and dying for home cooking-how about it?" "Fine! How soon will you be out?" My mind was going in circles trying to remember exactly what there was in the refrigerator and pantry that I could draw on in a hurry. No grabbing the car and driving down for an extra something or other these days. Have to save the gas to get Sally back and forth from the doctor's. 

But I could walk up to the Gallento's and buy some of her fresh red raspberries. Peg always called them "pussy feet"-which is pretty descriptive of their downy softness-but no words could quite describe their fragrant flavor.

Sally got busy making some of her special meringues -they are frosted with sugar crystals on their crisp surface and creamy and soft inside and are the complete answer of what to serve with fresh berries or sliced peaches. I found one can of chicken soup in the pantry and some spring onions in the salad drawer in the refrigerator so decided I could make Peg's favorite

Vichyssoise-which our best restaurants are now listing 177

as De Gaulle. That chilled, velvety smooth soup that is sort of a poetic version of old fashioned potato soup which goes so well with rainy, blowy nights. This one goes best with sunny, breathless weather. I went out to the tucked away damp corner by the basement door where the clump of chives comes up year after year. 

No Vichyssoise would appear in public without its topknot of tiny green straws of chopped chives. 

Made some pinwheel cheese biscuit with a dash of cayenne in the cheese-I like contrasts in food just as I do in people-can't stand blandness in either. Our old blue platter mounded up with a golden heap of scrambled eggs and bordered with thick slices of fried tomatoes made the main dish-and a good one it was. At any rate the Captain took three helpings. He had to leave right after lunch to make some connection in town but I'm awfully glad Peg brought him along. He had been on his way home, for the first time in six months, when he received changed orders and had to turn around when he was halfway there. "It was a darned sight harder than a whole lot of things I've had to do in Army life-and I've been in the service a long time. But it's tougher on Phoebe-she has to do the waiting. I wish you could meet my wife, Phoebe. They don't come any finer."

I wish I could. Women have always had the job of waiting and praying and hoping, but that doesn't make it any easier.

After he had gone I had a chance to talk with Peg. 

Sally went upstairs to put the children to bed for their naps and she didn't come down until nearly time for 

Peg to go-Sally has an understanding heart. 178

Peg had a long distance from Phil last night and his new C.O. wants him on active duty-which suits Phil perfectly. The orders may come through any day now and Peg packed and grabbed the first train out. "But you'll be back here when he's gone, won't you?" 

Peg looked at me silently for a moment as though she were wondering whether I could take it straight or have it led up to. I'm glad she decided not to ease into it. "I'm going to bunk in with Joan for the durationor until one of our men gets back. Maybe I'm silly, but 

I want to be as near Phil as I can in case he goes on Pacific duty and that's the most likely. The East seems so terribly far away." 

I don't think she's silly but deeply and honestly in love which is the way things should be. We went in town together on the five o'clock train and I saw her off at the Grand Central. Then I came home to have supper with Sally. I went up to kiss Chunky and Cindy good-night and their warm arms around my neck did me more good than anything else I could imagine-except Jim's arms around me. They've been my haven of refuge for so long. 

June 25.-For some unknown reason I turned to the financial page of the paper this morning and found the first smile that the news had given me. In a nice bold headline I read-"Farm Bill News Unsettles Grains- 

Cotton Is Jittery." Jim will laugh at this with me when

I show it to him-it's grand to be married to a man who has the same kind of queer sense of humor. I remember one card he sent me when he was out of town at Easter. 179

He'd clipped an ad-"Paas Easter Egg Dyes" and, under it, he had written-"What a pity!" I really believe a successful marriage depends much more on the same sense of humor than on whether you both like Shakespeare or baseball. Nothing quite so deadly as to tell someone your favorite joke and then to be met with a blank look-reminds me of that cartoon series "Nothing 

Can Be Done About It!"

There is a grand plumber story going the rounds out here and I've told it to everybody I meet. Some of them go into roars and others make an inane remark and don't get it at all. They're finishing up a few houses, in one of the new developments, with the material on hand and a new tenant had moved in to a completed one. The house next door was having the final touches done and two plumbers were working in the kitchen. 

All of a sudden the lady heard some choice language and it grew louder as the soft summer breeze blew it her way. She finally decided she could take no more and called the police-at least, this is my pet cop's version of it and he was the officer sent. He walked in on the cursing pair who were, by this time, cozily working on the sink. "What's going on in here?"

Bill looked at Tom and Tom looked at Bill with innocence and puzzlement. "Nothing-nothing at all-why?” "Well, the lady next door called up and said she wasn't fussy but your language was more than she could stand and she thought the police better step in before it got any worse." "She must a been dreamin'," said Tom. "I was just finishing up this joint like and Bill here, he got kind of 180

careless and dropped a little melted lead on my arm. 

So I said, 'Gee whiz, Bill-can't you be a little more careful?' Now, why should a dame object to that?" 

When I told the story to Mrs. Melrose she smiled vaguely as though it baffled her. "I really don't see why she should call the police-it seems to me his language was very restrained-melted lead must burn badly.”

gave up. The woman simply has no sense of humor --which is what I have suspected right along. Anyway, Laurie laughed at my story and said she wished her Uncle Howard could have heard it because he would have chuckled over it for a week. That bottle-tipping, horse-racing uncle was the one bright spot in the gloom of her family. 

June 27.-Sally and Laurie and I went to see Tortilla 

Flat tonight. I can't imagine anyone less like Laurie than Hedy Lamarr as "Sweets" Ramirez, but when she pitched in to take care of the baby in that ramshackle house I could see Laurie in every move and word. She would have gone into action with the same energy and decisiveness. Where children are concerned

Laurie is a mother cat with kittens and would take on a growling bull dog. Hedy Lamarr looked like a million in the outfit that the publicity says cost $3.95 and she really acted instead of just posing and registering emotion. But the old hermit and his dogs stole the show. 

Spencer Tracy is one of my favorites, too, and he made the care-free life, with all its dirt and disorder, very appealing to such a domesticated product as I am. This was one of the best pictures I've ever seen. There are times when I believe the intelligence and artistry of the movies is improving for this took the feel of Steinbeck's 181

book and kept the same difficult combination of kindness and villainy, physical animal pleasure and spiritual qualities.

June 30.-The garden is all green and white and peaceful and cool. The Shasta daisies are glistening when the early dew is on them, the Siberian iris seems to float in the moonlight for the narrow dark foliage vanishes unless you are very close. The honeysuckle is so full this year and the fragrance comes into every open window. I wonder why the perfume is so much stronger when the whiteness of the blossoms has faded to ivory and the end of their brief life is nearly over? There must be a deep thought in that somewhere-but I can't put it into words.

Any more than I can put into words the thoughts and emotions that ran through me when I helped today on the registration of boys 18-20. They seemed so young and so old. Younger than I can remember ever beingolder than I am now. The news is that this registration is simply routine so as to have all available man-power listed and classified. As the law now reads they can not be called for military service. But laws change in the wink of an eye when war is on. Mothers and fathers are voters and must be eased into difficult things. Art has gone of his own free will, and that seems different than calling youngsters who have no voice in the decision. I hope I'm wrong in believing that this listing is only the first step. After election it will be a different story and the heartaches are yet to come. 182

July 

THEY HAVEN'T STANDARDIZED BABIES—NOT YET— THE EARLY BIRD GETS THE GAS-HOSKY IS A CONSCIENTIOUS OBJECTOR-TO MARKET, ΤΟ MARKET-THE QUEEREST, QUIETEST FOURTH- THE JOYS AND SORROWS OF A VICTORY GARDEN. 

July 1-Jim had to catch a six-thirty train this morning to make connections for the one to Washington, so I took him down and planned to go over and get some gas as soon as the station opened. The trouble was that half the women in town had the same bright idea. The cars were lined up from John's Filling Station and Lubritorium-sounds like a word Ogden Nash would make up-clear down to the bridge on one side and a block past the telephone exchange on the other. I dutifully got in line and wished I had an apple and a good book or at least a good book. The station opens at seven and, when John arrived, some of our best people were almost at the hair-pulling stage. "You can't get ahead of the line by parking across the intersection-suppose the fire department had to get through!" "Suppose they did! I could move, couldn't I?" "You can move right now and get back where you belong!" "But I have a baby at home-I can't sit around here all day!" 

The horns honked singly and in chorus while three 183

ladies got out of their cars and converged on the rugged individualist. 

She refused to budge. To show her complete indifference to public opinion she powdered her nose, applied lipstick in scarlet curves, leaned back, and lit a cigarette. 

It looked as though defensive warfare were going to win this battle. But just then John arrived and settled the whole thing very simply. "Those in line will be waited on in turn-those out of line can wait until the delivery truck comes at eleventhirty." John is a man of direct action and simple speech. The lady of the cigarette started her engine, tried to strip a couple of gears, and went snorting up the street. I don't know when and if she got any gasand I cared less. Most people are so fair and decent that the exceptions make a deep impression. In general there is more consideration of the other fellow, more co-operation, more plain human kindness than you find in ordinary times. "Some folks is ornery like March weather," says 

Claudine. "They makes my blood and gorge rise." This strange physical condition is always Claudine's reaction when her usually singing, smiling disposition is rubbed the wrong way. 

July 2.-Sally started to laugh when she was reading the paper this morning. "Here's a news note for Benjy- 'Diapers will probably be standardized in order to insure an adequate supply of this vital commodity. Standardization and simplification are on the way.'

What a help it would be if some planning bureau in our nation's capital would standardize and simplify the babies! Seems to me they are putting the cart before 999 184

the horse. The Plumbers' Association ought to get busy on the job and improve the installation. I've heard of these miracle babies who were completely trained at six months, my dear-but I never saw one. At least, all the best authorities urge mothers now to forget the training until the wabbly little mites can at least sit up without flopping over. Those out-size after-dinner coffee cups that they used to refer to delicately as tots' potties are a thing of the past I hope. Having never succeeded in selling the idea to my own offspring I think the whole thing is a silly waste of time and energy. They all get the notion sooner or later. May I be cast into the darkest pit of the nether world if I ever bleat to a young mother, "Why, isn't she trained yet? My little Peggy wore lace-trimmed panties when she was less than two!" 

How many times plump, middle-aged mothers, with their darlings in high school and beyond, made that speech to me. Failing memory is a certain sign of old age but I didn't realize it then and used to go home and wonder and weep a few tears at my own lack of motherly ability. "Horse feathers!" as Artie would say. 

July 3.-The five of us-Beth Roberts, Marcia Burton, Lottie Gibson, Aline Kirkwood, and I-started off for our weekly marketing this morning and planned a neighborhood Fourth of July supper in Aline's backyard. She has a wonderful fireplace and we can take down our ping-pong table and Beth's dart set and 

Marcia's ring toss so as to have things that everybody can enjoy. Bob King promised us long ago that, whenever we had a community party, he would read horoscopes for us as his contribution. During the depression this hobby of his paid many a gas and electric bill. Jim 185

pretends he thinks it is all a lot of nonsense, but I notice he found out from his mother the exact hour at which he was born. He told her it was information for his income tax report but, someway, one of Bob's personal two-dollar horoscopes turned up mysteriously in the back of Jim's desk drawer when I was cleaning house that spring. Jim is so wonderfully human-one of the nine thousand reasons why I love him. 

Beth makes grand baked beans with a fried whole onion snuggled in the middle of the pot and a creamy, amber juice that combines mustard and molasses and some kind of subtle herb. The chunks of salt pork on the top are a deep chestnut brown all the way through and so tender you can cut them with a fork. She says her great-grandmother bequeathed the recipe to her oldest daughter when she was sixteen and it has passed from generation to generation. Maybe that is just one of Beth's good stories-but they are marvelous beans. 

I'm going to make Jim's pet cabbage salad with the celery seed and whipped sour cream dressing, Marcia will bring her corn bread sticks that are so crusty and good with the real water-ground meal flavor and bits of crisp fried bacon in them. Lottie is furnishing the watermelon and plugging it and pouring in a cup of white wine to soak for twenty-four hours while it chills -that is really something! Aline is furnishing the coffee and any odds and ends will be brought by the rest of the gang. I'll call up Laurie tonight, as soon as she gets back from the hospital, and see if she'll come. I'm sure she will because, in the past few months, she has come outside of herself and seems so easy and happy with our crowd. She's making up for the lost time of being a 186

lonesome little girl. I might ask Bruce Stevens, too, because I know he'd enjoy it if he can manage the timedoctors are getting scarcer and scarcer and he's rushed off his feet. The Army, according to him, will be a rest -at least there won't be any neurotic women or inconsiderate babies that start their trip at two in the morning. He sounds so hard boiled sometimes and he really is so soft hearted that he worries himself half sick over his patients. But if I have Bruce and Laurie it will only give her more notions. She looks at him now as though a combination of the Angel Gabriel, Young Lochinvar, and Clark Gable had landed in our living room. It sort of chokes me up.

The super-market, by most beguiling signs, suggests to its patrons that they do their week-end shopping on

Friday when the store is less crowded. The result is that the store is a mob scene on both days. The habit people have of eating regularly certainly complicates life and uses up good money but the pleasure would go out of life if cooking ceased. Our kitchen is the nicest, homiest room in the house and the one that people are happiest in. There is something so personal about marketing when you're buying for people you've looked after for a long time and loved even longer than that. I thought of Art when I ordered the hamburger, I thought of Peg when I bought the bacon, I thought of Jim when I told the butcher, "very lean lamb stew," and I could almost see Sally's smile when I said, "And a nice, plump fowl that will make good chicken pie."

Chunky and Cindy sort of trailed along in my thoughts on nearly everything, because there is nothing whatever wrong with their appetites and anything which is 187

edible-and plenty of it-suits them to perfection. I'm almost ashamed to be taking board for Chunky because he is so easy to care for and has such endearing little boy ways. But most of the money is going into 

War stamps so I guess it's all right. Hosky-old boy, old boy-is my present problem child. He just does not care for the various shapes and sizes of dehydrated dog food and the canned stuff is a thing of the past. I buy him fresh beef kidney or a chunk of heart every Saturday but, with marketing only once a week, that's the best I can do for him. Whether the new food is in chunks, neat little rounded loaves, kibbled, or in the shape of alluring bones he will have none of it until practical starvation is staring him in the face. The labels say, "Your dog will eagerly welcome this delicious and nutritious balanced diet. It will keep him bright of eye, glossy of coat, alert and healthy. A complete meal in the new scientifically devised form to meet the requirements of government regulations on the use of cans." They must have been thinking of some other dog! Hoskins comes over to the pan eagerly enough but one sniff makes a changed man of him. His tail droops, his ears droop, his whole being droops. He looks at me as much as to say, "And I always thought you were a friend!" 

I look at him firmly and order, "Eat your dinner!"

So he makes another trip to the pan and sniffs again but it's no go. Then I coax him, thinking to myself all the time that I'm wasting good energy to no purpose-I'm right. Ordering, coaxing, firmness, pettingall lead to the same result-he will have none of it until sometime, by the light of the moon, when he is really 188

hungry and can devour it without losing face. He wants meat that looks like meat and not a section of an Egyptian mummy. At the present rate at which the price of meat is going up and the supply going down we'll all soon have to take to his diet-and like it. 

But was I happy when I walked up to the meat counter! After years of waiting my turn, only to have it snatched away from me by some gentle soul with no inhibitions, I beheld a ticket system that is a peach.

Each butcher wears a letter on his manly breast and each letter has a pile of numbered cards. The neat little counter rack displays a card with, "The number now being served is-" So you pick the butcher with the fewest customers waiting and your turn comes up in due time and order. The hours I have spent trying to be a lady and then giving up that line and shouting, "I'm next!" The only thing they haven't figured out yet is what to do about human nature and the small per cent of chiselers who dwell in even the most exclusive developments. I ran into one of them this morning and was so mad I could have done physical battle with pleasure. I had D 6 and D 5 was being served. Just as the butcher turned to me this female barged in with D 2. "I was here long ago and I just left for a minute to buy a few things-it's my turn!" "Madam, you must stay in your place-the lady with number six is next-you'll have to take another ticket." "I'll do no such thing-I'll call the manager-I was here before this woman and she has no right-" "Wait on her," I said wearily and wondered what had become of my flaming spirit as Jim calls it when 189

I go up in the air over something. He never takes my temper seriously and the youngsters tell me I look awfully cute and funny when I'm mad. What can you do with a family like that? But I hate brawls and the lady looked to me like the kind where Queensberry rules would not apply. Just the same, the ticket idea is grand -even if it doesn't work all the time. Beth was sitting at the wheel when I came out followed by two boys with my orders. Aline and Marcia and Lottie came right after me, also followed by boys with cartons and bags. It reminded me of the pictures 

I have seen of the Christmas dinner ceremony at Château Frontenac with the waiters parading into the big dining room bearing baked hams, the traditional boar's head, roasted turkeys and all the fixings. Someday Jim and I are going up to spend a Christmas there. We'll have to live to be a hundred and ten to go to all the places we've talked about and planned on. Jim can go out and ski and swoop around the curves on the toboggan, and me, I'll just sit by the big fireplace in the baronial entrance hall of the Château and order tea and toasted scones at fifteen-minute intervals all afternoon and have one swell time. My last coast was when Peg and Art persuaded me to go down our fiendish side hill. "Go belly-flopper just once, Mom," Art urged. So

I did. Nothing happened, but I died a thousand deaths watching the trees converging on me and seeing my broken bones piled up against the fire hydrant on the curve. I'll act my age and meet my declining years in one piece, thank you.

July 4.-This has been the queerest and, in many ways, the nicest Fourth we've ever spent. There wasn't 190

a sound all day except when we heard the cannon fired off on the Memorial field during the ceremonies and the crowd singing while the school band played The 

Star-Spangled Banner. I've heard that played and sung it myself so many times, but it never meant anything much to me-anything really deep and inside me. But this time I could feel my throat tighten up and little shivers run up and down my backbone. It meant Art and all his friends, it meant Jim in Washington and working nights and Sundays, it meant all my neighbors with their own particular strains and burdens and determination—and it meant Peg with her husband waiting his next orders which might carry him and her heart to the ends of the earth. It meant Bruce Stevens operating on some lonely, wounded boy in a base hospital instead of coming around to sniff at the spices in our cupboard and answering a phone call to go to a sick child. It meant Sally having Benjy, without Bruce to help her, and with no comforting assurance that Jud would be right out in the hall. I looked over at her, sitting so quietly on the big porch swing, and a sudden fury came over me. I hated war with such depth of feeling that I thought nothing must interfere with our winning it no matter how long and hard the struggle. 

We've got to win back what we've lost and make a new world where no one's life can be pulled up by the roots because of the crazy, senseless ambition of a few men. 

I don't know the answer but we've got to find it!

Jim came home about two o'clock and helped some of the other men carry down the ping-pong table and set it up. Cindy and Chunky went around and around the house waving their flags and shouting, "March-ing, 191

march-ing, here we come a-march-ing," over and over. 

There was a certain monotony about the theme song but it seemed to suit them. No firecrackers allowed to be sold or brought into the county this year, so they don't know the joys of burned fingers and loud bangs.

Fewer gray hairs will sprout on parental heads, but the old-time Fourth was fun and exciting. I remember the year when Bud King woke us, in the dawn's early light, by calling up the stairs, "Please come quick! Artie's shot himself!" It was only a torpedo that exploded in his hot little hand-but it's a good thing heart failure doesn't run in our family or neither Jim nor I would be here to do any remembering.

The supper was a huge success and we ate and ate until the watermelon seeds were popping out of our ears. Someone remarked that they didn't see why a seedless watermelon hadn't been grown, and Chunky looked up as solemn as a panda and asked, "What could you spit then? It wouldn't be any fun if you couldn't spit! 

Cindy isn't so good at it—but she's a girl." It's just as

I tell Jim when I give him the latest chapter of the Perils of Pauline, as he calls my adventures, "No truck driver ever expects a woman driver to have a grain of sense and that's why they're so nice about helping me out-if I were a man it would be different.” "And how!" says Jim feelingly.

Funniest thing happened just as we were breaking up for the evening. Maybe it was funny and maybe it wasn't. Anyway Jim and I told Laurie we would walk home with her, even if it is just around the corner or up one hill and down one dale the way the land lies. 192

But Bruce Stevens, who up to that moment had been about as chatty as Coolidge, barged right in. "I'll drive her over-have to make a call in Great Bay. 

I want to show Laurie something. See you later maybe." "Is it the wing?" asked Laurie, which didn't make a grain of sense to me. But Bruce's next remark made even less. "Yep-and the lights. But don't take this seriously— 

I'm just amusing myself. Got the whole thing figured out but those two, between labor pains last night." As the English would say, "The man is barmy, definitely." 

I only hope he isn't amusing himself at Laurie's expense, because anything concerning Bruce is no laughing matter to her. She's as submerged as a submarine. 

July 7.-A copy of the Times, of July 1, was wrapped around a corduroy bathrobe that Beth brought over this morning. Sally is going to make Cindy an overall and jacket suit out of it and a beanie to match. 

It's a grand rich red and the beanie will make her look cute as a bug's ear when it is perched on her fluff of yellow curls. She's like Peg and adds charm to anything she puts on. Well, I unwrapped the robe and glanced at the paper. There is a certain fascination about a paper that is a few days old or a magazine that is six months old-even the Times which usually gives me the sensation of having been doing a little light reading in the Britannica. My eye happened to catch an article by Anne O'Hare McCormick. One sentence stood out like a cigarette in a blackout-"The tide that leads to fortune or disaster is near the flood." That has been going through my head all day. Fortune or disasterthere is no compromise. And I don't believe she meant 193

material wealth, but the fortune of breathing free air, guiding your own children, working at your own job of your own will and choice, and worshiping as your spirit leads you. Anything else is disaster, and mere physical survival is worthless if we lose those. These "peace at any price" people give me the cold chills.

July 9.-Saw a girl today who reminded me a lot of 

Peg-only not so beautiful and not as alive looking. But her great moment had come if nothing grand ever happens to her again. She came into the Old Mill for tea while Sally and I were getting the children settled at a table overlooking the water. This was a celebration of Benjy's coming of age-"Seven down and two to go," says Sally, "and the seventh month is supposed to influence their lives or horoscopes or something. I think a couple of popovers with strawberry jam would set his feet on the right road." So we counted up the gas and decided we could spare the one gallon necessary to make the trip to the Mill and back. Riding in a car these days gives you a feeling of great luxuryand we used to hop into it to go after a package of Camels. Now we live up to the advertising and literally walk a mile for one. Jim says I have a flea mind and guess I have because I started to think about that girl.

The place was packed and she walked in, the focus of all eyes, with a Colonel and two Majors! Maybe they were her father and two brothers, but I doubt it. She reminded me of that Fable in Slang by George Ade, "She was totally unconscious that every opera glass was leveled on her-and determined to remain unconscious." 194

July 10.-Jim had a long distance from Washington this morning and had to make a one o'clock train. Claudine was here to be with Sally and the children, so Jim decided it would be nice if I went in town to see him off-at this rate he might as well get a monthly ticket and commute. He resides in Oakdale and lives in Washington. We certainly saw two strange sights at the 

Grand Central. A Victoria, complete with a team of beautiful horses, driver in livery, a lady in lady in gray silk and a gentleman with chamois gloves and stick pulled into the taxi extrance. If Orson Welles' men from Mars had suddenly appeared they couldn't have caused more excitement. Cops, taxi drivers, and Red Caps all gathered around and stood with their mouths open. And one of the taxi men was a colored woman! I hope I can remember some of the details of these strange times when my grandchildren gather near in the dusk of a winter evening and beg, "Tell us about the time there was a war and you weren't an old lady." I hope I'll remember to tell them of the magazines on the station newsstands in this month of July, nineteen hundred and forty-two. Who would ever have dreamed there would be a time when Glamour, Reader's Digest, Liberty, Good Housekeeping, Harper's, the staid Atlantic, the Companion, the Journal, Vogue, True Story would be standing side by side with every cover-and a couple of dozen more-carrying a picture of the American flag? I don't know whose idea it was but it was a peach!

It gave me the same feeling I had as I looked around at the soldiers and sailors milling over that dramatic central part of the station. All in uniform, all individuals in their own inner character and make-up, and 195

all working toward the same goal of keeping the flag flying. I sound like a grand-stand speech but that's the way I felt honestly and sincerely. Why are we so afraid of expressing deep emotions? The fear that someone will label them "corny" and make us look foolish.

Perhaps the war will change that, too, and we'll become real again and forget the surface veneer and the smart thing to say. I was never any good at them anyway. "Honest Abe" Jim calls me when I go on like this, but he lets me spout to my heart's content and then asks, "When are you going to make me a Dutch cherry pie?" or something equally down to earth. I'll never grow a soap box under my feet as long as I have Jim around-which I hope will be forever!

July 12.-Letter from Jim today with a clipping enclosed, "No more horses to be used in Bermuda. Motor transportation has been adopted for the sake of greater speed and efficiency." How I do hate those two words and how Jim and I did love the leisurely plop-plop of the horses' feet along the coral rock roads. I'm glad we were there before speed and efficiency moved in. 

Had to order a new rubber gasket to go around the refrigerator door-the old one has become so soft and flabby that it sticks and pulls from its moorings. I called the service office and a most abrupt young lady answered-the voice with the smile was not in her lesson book. "I want to order a new rubber gasket for my nineteen hundred thirty-nine model-nine cubic foot capacity"-Jim always tells me to be explicit as most order clerks are not mind readers. "The price will be two-fifty, Madam, and you will 196

have to turn in the old one before we can make delivery. You'll have to put it on the refrigerator yourself,

Madam. We are very short of service men and can send them out only on legitimate repair jobs-this comes under the classification of minor replacements." "But," I argued, "if I mail the old one the box will not work well in this hot weather and I have two children." "Many of our customers have babies, Madam. You will have to turn in the old one before we can make delivery." By that time I wasn't sure whether she was talking about turning in gaskets or babies. She reminded me a lot of a fifth-grade teacher I had once. Her favorite remark was, "I do not like your attitude!" It was years before I knew what she was talking about. 

July 14.-Pulled our first baby beets today and cooked them whole with some of the leaves from the plants I thinned out. I never tasted anything so good!

Gardens are just like bringing up children-you have days of discouragement and days of encouragement and the final crop is usually far better than you expected. I put up two jars of pickled beets because Jim likes them so much with roast pork. No telling whether we'll have roast pork this winter, but we'll have something and pickled beets can cover a multitude of sins as far as Jim is concerned. We are going to have quantities of pears on our two trees, and I'm going to swap with Beth for some of her apples. She taught me how to make the old-time apple sauce by washing the apples and then cutting them up-skin, cores, seeds and alland stewing in a light syrup until mushy. The last ten minutes you add a piece of stick cinnamon and a couple of thin slices of lemon. Then the apple mush is 197

pressed through a sieve. The flavor is so much better than when you peel them first. I like it icy cold with warm gingerbread. 

While I was fixing the beets I heard Sally singing, "Sweet and low, sweet and low, wind of the wes-tern sea." I looked out of the window and saw her sitting in the old wooden two-seater swing with Cindy and Chunky sitting on the seat opposite. Soon they joined in the singing-Cindy's baby treble slightly off key, and Chunky's rough masculine voice nowhere near the tune-but they were having a grand time and it made me happy deep inside me. 

Such a sunset tonight! Coral pink clouds against wide sweeps of yellows and blues and deep rose and the garden lay like yards and yards of flowered chintz. I have ranted about that top terrace flower bed and groaned over an aching back and broken finger nails and said, "Next year I simply won't try to have it." I've sprayed for Japanese beetle and dug out cut worms and dusted for red spider until I couldn't work another minute, but, when I looked at it tonight, I knew it was worth it and next spring will find me on my knees again.

The trumpet vine up the old locust tree is finally blooming and it is a sight to behold. It must have the same deliberate nature as our wisteria which went on, year after year, with nothing but a most luxuriant crop of green leaves. Then, all of a sudden, one spring it decided to try a few blossoms and was so delighted with itself that each season it outdoes the one before. Why do that old gypsy's words come into my head-"A stranger thing will happen to one you love-a blossoming and a singing in the heart." The only person 

I can

198

think of is Laurie and she has seemed like that latelyas though she were reaching out into the sun of human warmth and love and companionship. I wish I could have known her family so I could have wrung their necks separately and collectively-it would have been a pleasure and a civic duty. Why do other people's failures and mistakes stand out so clearly and your own be hardly visible? "Cast out first the beam out of thine own eye." Christianity is very hard to practice. 

July 15.-Took down my first can of fat for salvage today and was paid twelve cents for the three pounds. 

A ten cent War stamp and two cents for the penny jar toward another. I hope I'll get more expert at collecting the fat when I have more practice-certainly made a mess of it this time with two strainers, one table, a patch of floor, and the front of a brand clean apron as the casualties. I couldn't even blame my clumsiness on the youngsters because I shooed them out of the kitchen before I began. 

It was really funny to see and hear the women at the meat counter. Some of them had brought scrap fat in bags, some in glass jars, and one bride had hers neatly stored in an old perfume bottle. She showed it to me with great pride. "It smelled so awful when I melted it that I thought maybe this would help." She looked-when the butcher refused to take it in glass-the way Cindy does when she doesn't find a raisin in her cooky. 

George stopped by tonight on his way home from work and headed straight for the garden at the back. "I gotta take a look over our beans." Ever since he used to come to us for mowing the lawn and edging the beds 199

he has regarded our place and our interests as his own. 

A regular job and good pay hasn't changed him any. 

He came back with his face simply shining with joy. "I tells you they was goin' to do good. Claudine she be in tomorrow mawnin' to help put 'em up." "I can't pay her to help or the beans will cost me too much." 

He looked at me reproachfully, "I didn' mention nothin' about no pay. Claudine she be proud to give you a hand."

Never have I felt smaller and weighed in the balance and found wanting. Just plain, simple goodness is so disarming. Suddenly I had an inspiration. "Claudine and I can work on shares. We'll do the beans together and I'll borrow Mrs. Roberts' pressure cooker. Tell Claudine to bring her jars and then both of us will have beans for winter." "I'll be most pleased to convey the message and explain the import." George always wins the last round. 

July 16.-The full story of the landing of German saboteurs on one of our Long Island beaches in June just came out in the paper this morning. It reads like the wildest kind of adventure story. Where that twenty-one-year-old Coast Guard ever got the nerve and control to face those four on a lonely, foggy beach at midnight is something that is hard to explain. Where do any of us get the courage we draw on when we're scared to death and facing a danger beyond our own strength? Maybe Joan would have the answer from her theory of "Life is just a chemical mistake," but that doesn't satisfy me by a million miles. I should think 200

that boy's family would be too proud to speak to mere mortals! 

July 18.-Sally and the children and I went to a Red

Cross Benefit Tea this afternoon and a good time was had by all. It started out this morning when I was deep in the dishpan with one eye on the double boiler of rice pudding and the other on the oven where a batch of cookies was baking. I don't know why it is, but the phone and the front door bell always ring at a time like that. Both of them went off at the same instant. I grabbed the phone as it was nearest. A drawing-room voice that would have done credit to a Finishing School for Young Ladies started right in. "This is Miss Arkin of the Elite Photographic Studios-GOOD morning." "I'm sorry, but I'm not interested in pictures right now."

But she had her speech and nothing was going to stop her carrying it to the final line-"One must be interested right now. In wartime one never knowsand a photographic record of the little ones is so important! Perhaps their daddy is in service-think how he would treasure such a gift." "But-" I began again. "We are making a special offer, only to the wellknown and important residents of this section." I could just hear Jim say, "The old oil!" The front door bell rang again. The voice went on, "We are making this special offer in order to be able to display the pictures of these charming children in our windows." "I'm sorry-but we don't want our children displayed 201

anywhere. I'm not interested in pictures right nowand there is someone at the door." "Thank you so much-perhaps some other time.”

I hung up the phone with a feeling of inferioritythe perfect control and refusal to acknowledge defeat always get me down. I went to the door and there stood a little girl about knee-high to a toad. Out of the shrubbery came another little girl. So many new people have moved into town that I don't know a tenth of the children any more. The first one was of the chubby, solemn variety; the other one looked like Alice in Wonderland after she had eaten the magic cake that made her grow. "Will you please buy a ticket for the Red Cross Benefit Tea? Our club is giving it this afternoon at three o'clock in our clubrooms. And would you like to make a donation of food?" I bought four tickets at ten cents each and did up a package of fresh cookies. 

So we all went. Chunky and Cindy were so excited over the idea of a party that their naps consisted of shouting from one room to the other. "I'm going to wear my purty dress with a pink bow in my hair and I'm going to look very purty." "I'm going to eat more than anybody and if anybody hits me I'll hit anybody." Male and female created He them. 

The clubhouse turned out to be two old packing cases the girls had set up in the patch of woods at the end of the road. Three camp stools, another box for a table. But the table was covered with a cloth of paper napkins and a bouquet of flowers was arranged in an old jam jar. Most of the mothers in the neighborhood 202

were there and food donations kept piling up. You could see what a struggle our hostesses were having to remember that they were grown-up young ladies giving a Benefit and not little girls with a strong desire to see that some of the refreshments were left for them. 

The pink lemonade was wonderful. Not enough paper cups to go around but we all took turns. Then the club members put on an entertainment of songs and dances and an impersonation of movie actresses. My chubby little friend was Deanna Durbin and obliged with the song, "I like to whistle-for whistling makes me merry."

We all helped count up the cash receipts before we left. Total profit-one dollar and ninety cents. It was one of the nicest teas I have ever attended. And Chunky didn't hit anybody!

July 24.-Letter from Art. It is full of Fighters, Div ers, Scouts, and Interceptors. Full, too, of a pride that is fine. "Our bunch is swell! The Air Force is the most important branch of the service and the winning of the war depends on how we carry on. Went up at sunrise this morning and I felt as though I owned the world. Don't worry-I'm having the time of my life!" 

I tell myself I won't worry-but I can't help thinking.

Anyway, the vegetables got a good weeding and stirring up. The early tomatoes are beginning to color. 

George says they are going to be right full-some. I must search the basement for more jars. Guess we'll have to buy winter carrots-ours are all leaves. Perhaps I was too generous with the fertilizer.

July 26.-The authorities are still at it! The latest instructions are, "Use direct jet of water, not a fine spray, on incendiary bombs. Use sand only where water is 203

not available and when the bomb falls far from any inflammable material." Hope the ones that land here are that considerate. The report goes on to say, "With the new method we will save more lives and more property in the event of a raid. That is the only criterion for judging the matter." The lectures Jim and I have listened to, the orders we have read, on the absolute necessity of using a spray and avoiding a stream. Our bucket of sand on the third floor must be suffering from an inferiority complex. 

July 29.-Birthday of Premier Benito Mussolini! He is fifty-nine but the Italian radio ignored the whole thing. 

It seems the gentleman is sensitive about his age. But 

Hitler sent his usual annual telegram of congratulations. 

Just a form of being nice to the office boy. A bottle of one hundred aspirins would be my idea of the perfect and practical gift. 

July 30.-Went out early this morning to gloat over our thriving Victory garden and saw a sight that made me furious! A mole had taken his nightly constitutional in a zig-zag path through the length and breadth of the whole bed. Plants were heaved out of the earth and some of them lying on their sides as limp as oysters. It took me nearly two hours to re-plant and press down the hummocks. I remembered George had told me once about putting moth balls in mole runs. I've tried it but am certainly "Ye of little faith." None of our furry friends around here ever do anything the way the books say they should. Even the ants in the rock garden are a special imported variety who multiply and grow fat on any poison I can buy-in fact, they like change of diet and welcome each new sort 204

with eagerness. If Hoskins should bestir himself to catch this villain of a mole I wouldn't feel too badly, but his real interest runs to cats-they're more fun even though they perch in treetops and spit at him.

Between moles and cut worms and aphis and queer individual ailments each kind of vegetable seems to get, we'll be lucky to have one sample jar of each put up for the winter. Just the low state of mind I'm in tonight. 205

August

THE CAR STAYS HOME, AND SO DO WE-CHUNKY'S CAVE MAKES WALDO NERVOUS-"I DO WISH HERB COULD SEE YOU!"-"MY HUSBAND'S IN WASH- INGTON,"-GOOD-BY TO NYLONS—“ONCE A MIZER, ALWAYS A MIZER,"-RESERVOIRS ARE HAPPY. 

August 1.-Another Saturday with Jim not coming home on Sunday. The five weekdays aren't so bad because I keep busy and, with Sally feeling less and less peppy, there is plenty for me to do. The youngsters are good but they get restless with closer confinement to quarters. Other summers there has been the beach nearly every day. A square box of sand, a portable shower, is not the ocean and miles of wave-marked sand. It is not the swish of foam or the thrill of building endless caves and castles for the tide to creep up on, inch by inch, and finally carry off to the mystery of limitless but imagined space. I don't suppose they ever consciously realize what it is they miss but the lack is there just the same. Children are so near the natural and the primitive that they respond to beauty of wind and wave and sky and the green peace and adventure of wooded hills. Even the little dab of woods we have across the street and at the end of the road holds for them a source of imagination. Chunky finds strange and wonderful things in those woods. Just how wonderful I discovered this afternoon. The phone rang and a strange voice came over it-a voice that was very near to tears or I miss my guess. 206

"This is Waldo's mother!" "Yes?" I said, wondering who Waldo might be. "We live in one of those double houses in the new development and we've just moved here and my husband is a test pilot out at the Field." Where was all this leading? "Yes?" "And are you the mother of that terrible little boy they call 'Chunky'?" "He lives with us-and what is so terrible about him?"

The loving Christian spirit was not in me at the moment. People can say and think what they please about me as an individual, but let them say a word about my family-and Chunky is part of my family-and I'm up on my ear in two seconds. "He put Waldo in an underground cave this afternoon and locked a heavy door with a huge padlock.

When he finally let him out the child was in hysterics! 

He told him if he ever came back to the woods again he would lock him in there forever." "How old is Waldo?" "Six, going on seven-and he's a very nervous child." "Chunky is five, going on six. I'll see what I can do --but just where is this cave?" "In some deep, dark woods, Waldo says, and the cave is big enough to hold all the little boys and their families. Perhaps it's an Air Raid shelter-but it's no place for children to play and you have to do something about that little refugee, or whatever he is, who's living with you. My husband's a test pilot." She added this last as I used to shout at some playmate-"My father's a policeman and I'll tell him on you!" Most dis- 207

tinctly I did not like Waldo's mother but Chunky can't go around scaring nervous children into fits. I wished Jim were here. He always knows what to do and can get at the root of things in two seconds where it takes me ages. He and Chunky talk the same language. I called Chunky and he came in with such a grin of pure puppy friendliness on his face that I didn't know where to begin. I gave him a cooky and started to wipe up the sink and the table with all the soap boxes and cleaners on it. The youngsters always talk most when I seem to be paying the least attention. "Chunky-do you know a boy named Waldo?"

A look of complete disgust, and he stopped fishing for the raisins in the cooky. “Oh—him!” "Do you know him?" "He's a stinker!" "Chunky!" "And he's a cry baby-and he tells lies-and I don't like him."

This didn't seem to be getting us anywhere except to the logical conclusion that the two were not the closest of buddies. So I tried another tack. "Where is this big cave with the heavy door and the big padlock?" "In our woods across the street-and it's a secret cave, and Waldo's a stinker!" "I don't like that word, Chunky-it's not nice and it's not kind and I don't want you to call children that.” 

How virtuous and smug grown-ups can sound. I really think that's a lovely word when applied to the selected few. Very expressive. 208

"All right," said Chunky dejectedly, "but that's what he is."

He reminded me of that personality-plus grandmother in the book February Hill. When the little girl was scratched by the cat and called it an unmentionable name, the grandmother replied calmly, "Maybe he is a dearie, but little ladies don't call 'em that." 

I asked Chunky if he would show me the cave if I kept it very secret, so we filed out, a procession of two, and walked across the street to the woods. About ten wild cherry trees, a couple of wild locust, and quantities of underbrush is all there is to it. But to the children "This is the forest primeval, the murmuring pines and the hemlocks." "You won't tell anybody?" "Not a soul, Chunky-I promise." "Well, you shut your eyes and -three times. Now you open them." you say 'Whisbick'

I looked and could see nothing. Then I looked again. 

Before my feet was a hole about two feet long and three feet wide, over the top of it was laid one loose plank and on top of the plank lay a rusty, old padlock. "Is that the cave where you locked Waldo?" "That's the cave-and beside, he's a dope. He tells his mother everything-even secrets. I guess this time he won't come back-maybe. And I didn't even put him in the cave-I just told him I would. And Cindy spit in it to show him how deep it was-she can spit awful good for a girl."

August 3.-This was just one of those days that gets out of the wrong side of the bed in the morning. Claudine arrived late with a misery in her feet-too much 209

week-end dancing I imagine-but if I had a husband week-ends I'd want to celebrate too. I'd bought another brand of soap flakes because the box had three more ounces and cost five cents less. These days you look at both sides of a nickel before you spend it. But Claudine doesn't share my desire to save Jim's money. "I don't hold with this here kind-it don't do nothin' to dirt but jes' aggravate it. And the mister's pants is plumb black from that there Washington place-why doan he stay home and not go traipsin' aroun' with white pants?"

I didn't answer. When Claudine gets into one of these muttering moods the best thing to do is keep out of her way. I went upstairs to make the beds. Sally was feeling perfectly miserable. "Hadn't I better call Bruce-he's closed the office but I think I can reach him. Or shall I call the new man?" "Don't call anybody-all they'd say is, 'It's pressure -the baby is starting to move down.' That's all they ever say. Doctors are so airy about having babies."

I stood looking at her, feeling utterly helpless and knowing how every muscle ached and every nerve strained to the limit. Then I saw the tears coming into her eyes and my own slopped right over. Sally has lots of spunk but a human being can take just so much of hot, muggy weather and loneliness and physical misery. I put my arms around her and we hung onto each other. "I know I'm a baby-and I am ashamed-but I want Jud. He'll never get here in time-and Dr. Stevens will 210

be gone-and Jim isn't here. Don't you leave me, Doody -no matter what happens, don't you leave me." "Nothing is going to happen-and I won't leave you.
