I'll get you to the hospital if I have to run down a couple of Air Wardens in a blackout. And Laurie has promised to come and stay with the children, if we need her, at any time of the day or night. You and 

Benjy are going to be all right." I only wish I felt as sure as I hope I sounded. Gosh, will I be glad when this party is over! 

I went downstairs and made us both a cup of good strong tea and we sipped that and had a cigarette and the world looked brighter for a few minutes. Then I heard Cindy shrieking with that varnish-removing yell she has when things go wrong. Chunky was howling in chorus and I dashed out the side door. Such a mess! 

Both of them skinned from stem to stern with plenty of good old Long Island road sand mixed with the blood. They had tried to coast down this demonish side hill of ours in Chunky's coaster wagon and had turned turtle on the curve.

As we entered the house I could hear Claudine shouting, "They ain't no hot water-the heater done bust!" So I swabbed off the dirt as best I could with cool water, smeared on plenty of zinc ointment, made some warm cocoa and a couple of sandwiches apiece and tucked them in bed to sleep it off. I told Sally to close her door and keep away during the whole process and, for a miracle, she did. Those youngsters were no sight for anyone as young as Benjy. 

August 5.-Walked downtown this morning to buy a few things I had forgotten on Saturday-it's hard to 211

get everything when there are four other people waiting to ride home in the same car with you and all of them in a tearing hurry. It has worked out surprisingly well considering we have all been used to having exclusive transportation to take us any place at any hour. While I was standing before an empty shelf, where canned baked beans used to be, a perfectly strange woman stopped in front of me. "Turn your profile to the light," she ordered. I was so surprised that I turned. Then she called one Gertie who was over in the soap section. "Come on over here! I want you to see something!"

The something was evidently me for they both stood and stared. Said the first woman, "Gertie, did you ever see anything like it? If she ain't the spitting image of 

Imogene I never saw nothing." "It's wonderful! I wish Herb could have a look."

She began talking to me with the close and confidential tone of an old college roommate. "Dearie, you look like the living image of my cousin Imogene who lived out in Seattle. She was a sweet girl but she kind of took on weight toward the end." "Toward the end?" I managed. 

She heaved a mighty sigh. "Yes, she passed on two years ago March. She'd put on weight but I never seen a handsomer corpse as she lay in her casket-lined with ivory satin, it was-I do wish Herb could see you.'

She hesitated and I tried to move on but the aisle was narrow and she and Gertie were not-our three pushcarts, parked at angles, didn't help any. “Would you do me a great big favor?" she asked, but waited for no answer-I doubt if I could have made a sound. 212

"Herb is out in a green Plymouth, parked right in front of the drugstore. You go up to him and just say, 'Who do I look like?' I know he'll say 'Imogene,' quick as a wink." 

I finally got out without seeing Herb or he me, but

I can just imagine what might have happened if I'd gone up to an unsuspecting man with the catch question of, "Who do I look like?" On the way home I bought a new lipstick. I just don't like reminding people of someone surrounded with ivory satin. 

August 6.-Still no hot water and I've called three service men, none of which have appeared although they have all promised to send someone as soon as they come in off another job. I can't imagine what is wrong with the burner unless it has learned that Jim is out of town. He has mechanical things trained and they won't do a thing for me. It's probably some simple little thing that can be fixed in two minutes. The boss of one of the Service with a Smile establishments put me through a regular questionnaire over the phone. Did I try this, had I tested that, was the pressure gauge working? I kept saying, "I don't know," until he finally lost his patience with such dumbness. "Lady, I can't send a service man until one comes in -I got two men and I had nine-they're in the army and the navy and the marines, they're in Defense plants. 

They ain't here! I got one truck and I was running five. 

I been working nights and Sundays! You got to be patient-there's a war on. Maybe you could fix that heater yourself if you'd only try."

Maybe I could, but the net result would probably be an explosion. I can tackle almost anything in the way 213

of feeding the hungry, nursing the sick, comforting the downcast-but machinery completely addles my brain. 

Why doesn't Jim come home?

August 7.-He did. The water heater wouldn't work because I had forgotten to order oil! The tank was completely empty. Learning to be the man of the house is going to take some intensive study on my part. I really felt terrible at being such a nit-wit but, for years and years, Jim has always attended to these things and you can't make yourself over in a few months. Somebody once said, "Only the adaptable will survive"-so 

I guess this old dog will have to learn some new tricks.

Stoking a furnace may be one of them, because the oil shortage is getting worse and worse. But Jim investigated the chances of buying grates for the furnace and can't find any. It would mean, too, that we'd have to build a coal bin. If we could get the grates and other parts we'd need the whole job would cost considerable -and then there might be a coal shortage. We simply don't know what we ought to do. But Jim is puttying up all the loose window glass whenever he gets any time, and is having storm windows made for the west side of the house where the winds howl like lost souls. 

Sally and the children and I will have to live on the lower floor and use the fireplace for extra heat-if we can get wood enough. The upstairs will simply have to get along the best way it can. I've ripped out two old sweaters and am going to knit bed socks for the whole family. What was good enough for grandma is good enough for me. 

August 11.-Bed socks will be a great comfort on a cold night with cold beds and less heat but the daytime 214

stocking problem is something else again. I tried to buy some Nylons today and the Duchess behind the counter looked at me in pained surprise. "Madam, we have had no Nylons for several weeks. 

The silk stockings are out of stock except in very small sizes." "I wear ten and a half.” "You might like to try our new rayon hosiery-many of our customers find them very satisfactory-we have them for a dollar forty-five a pair and up-three shades from which to choose." Then she had another thought. "Have you tried the new leg make-up? Simply fascinating to paint your stockings right on-and wash them right off. The girls all use it."

Little as I like to admit it-I am no girl and painted on legs are not for me. Not with Art and Peg and

Jim around. But I do think it's a grand idea for the young and adventuresome-but what happens when it rains? I must ask Peg. I bought two pairs of rayon and they take twenty-four hours to dry to a faint dampness-a week-end to get really dry. 

August 14.-Picked a lovely bunch of Queen Anne's lace by the side of the road this morning. It reminds me of magnified snowflakes and makes a beautiful arrangement in the deep red pottery jar that Jim gave me one birthday. I had admired it in a window when we were strolling along in town one night. Jim has a remembering heart. So many of our things around the house have associations with certain people and certain especially nice times. When we finally give up this big place it will be hard to choose what to take and 215

what to give away. I only hope I can give some of the happiness along with them.

Wish I could have brought home some of the blue wild flax that was blooming near the lacy white but they don't take kindly to picking. Their delicate clear blue folds up and loses all its beauty. It's just as well because the clumps belong where they are and make the roadside seem cool and landscaped. The railroad embankment has all its ugliness covered with wild white morning glory and the sturdy homely little flower,

Bouncing Bet-a buxom country girl with no style but lots of stamina and a certain freshness. It reminds me of Louisa who used to work for us in one of our numerous migrations before we settled in Oakdale. She wasn't much to look at but so pleasant and dependable when tough times came along. What a good friend she was.

August 16.-Three of the boys who were in the class with Art came by this quiet, hot Sunday afternoon.

All of them were in blue work shirts and slacks and carried big lunch boxes. I haven't seen any of them for ages and wondered where they were bound for. I hadn't long to wonder because they stopped in to ask about Art and how he was geting along and when he'd be through the cadet training. "Hope he likes flying the ships that come off our assembly line,” said one of them. "They sure are sweet babies. And boy, is that some plant-why, it takes half your lunch time to get to the cafeteria and back-have to punch the time clock going and coming. That's why we're carrying our grub-gives you more time to eat.' "But," I broke in, "do you work Sundays, too?" 

216

"Some of the shifts do and some don't. We're on the four-thirty to midnight now-ten per cent extra pay and some more bonus if Sunday is your seventh day of work. You don't have to take it on Sunday, but I figure if you're going to get ahead you have to be sort of co-operative and not crab about things." 

And these were the boys who, only a year ago, were complaining about high school home work and figuring that parents and teachers were their natural enemies bent on working them to death! Just confirms my opinion that kids like something that shows concrete results. They can't be bothered with figuring what 

Milton meant when he said, "Of Cerberus and blackest midnight born, in Stygian cave forlorn." I remember

Art's book review on Silas Marner-"This is a book by 

George Eliot who was a lady and ashamed of it so she called herself George so that no one would know. Silas 

Marner was a nut who hid gold in his fireplace. Effie was kind of cute but too good to be real. When he reformed because he loved Effie more than his gold it was hard to believe. I didn't. Once a mizer, always a mizer is more true to life." He got a C on that. English teachers are not apt to rate original thought very highly.

Our three Defense workers left all of a sudden so as to catch the guy they ride to work with. Sally persuaded them to add a couple of her devil's food cup cakes to each box but we had a hard time getting them in. "Mom thinks I'm going to starve to death if she doesn't load it up-both home and at work. She reads books on the Yardstick of Nutrition and He Works on 217

the Night Shift. Then she worries and puts more sandwiches." "And carrots!" "Supposed to be good for eyesight or what have you." "Well, so long," they called back. “Tell Art to keep 'em flying and we'll keep 'em sliding." in a couple August 19.—It rains and rains and rains. The City 

Fathers are rejoicing over the filling of the reservoirs, but the rest of us are groaning over the soaked gardens and the washing that won't dry and the children that drive you slowly crazy saying, "What'll we do now?" Sally was trying to get some rest this afternoon because she sleeps so badly at night. Benjy does his pole vaulting and deep sea dives in the still hours and then lies quiet as a weary athlete in the daytime. Babies are so inconsiderate.

I tried to amuse the children by reading a new book to them but they're restless from so much indoors. I finally rigged up two huts for them by draping a couple of army blankets over two card tables and giving them an apple and a cooky apiece. Feeding children between meals may be frowned upon by the best lecturers but the habit has saved the reason of many a good mother. Bananas are wonderful because they stop off the vocal cords completely but bananas are now five cents apiece-when you can get them, which is seldom. 

Jim brought home four from Washington on his last trip and they were a rare treat. 

August 20.-Laurie came in today and brought me some iris roots, a gift to her from one of her grateful patients. 218

"He's an Italian gardener, the father of Francisco, the little boy in my Ward. We call him Frankie and he's six hands' full-always getting out of bed when you aren't looking and then poking his head out at you when you turn a corner with a full tray." "Sweet child!" 

Laurie bristled right up. "He is sweet! Bruce is crazy about him-I wish we had four-" She stopped as though she had jammed the brake clear to the floor boards. "He has beautiful eyes and is so loving." "Who? Bruce?" I couldn't resist it.

Laurie colored a nice pink, from the beginning of the plunging neckline she wears off duty these days, to the beginning of the Claudette Colbert hairline she is affecting. She's a changed woman but she can still blush-and I ought to have my neck wrung. I'm sure if Jim were here he'd be glad to oblige. Anyway this is the first time I ever heard her say, "Bruce." 

August 22.-Sally had a letter from Jud today and he can promise definitely to be home by September second. If Benjy will only wait. Sally chirked up to such an extent that we got one of the high school girls to stay with the children and went down to the movies. 

Didn't have sense enough to phone ahead so ran smack into a second showing of Bette Davis in The Little Foxes. She did it so marvelously that it was simply horrible! Instead of cheering us up it gave us as nice a case of the blues as anyone could imagine. The scene where she sits, and sits and sits-never moving a muscle of her face-and lets her husband die would send cold chills down the back of a torch singer. An inhuman, calculating man is bad enough, but a woman seems 219

a thousand times worse. We stopped off at the drugstore to try to forget it in one of Bob's Oakdale Specials-coffee ice cream, chocolate syrup, salted almonds -and whipped cream. If that doesn't take your mind off your troubles nothing will. All it did for me was to make me wish I hadn't and to think comfortingly of how nice my bed was going to feel. All it did for 

Sally was to give her two horrible pains right in the middle of Benjy. I never drove home so fast in all my life or felt so scared. After this, when we want a little relaxation, we'll read one of the Elsie Dinsmore books and drink weak tea. 

August 25.-Not another sign of pains or aches with 

Sally since Saturday night. Guess Benjy is going to wait for his dad after all. And Jim will be home this week-end. Glory Hallelujah!

August 26.-Saw in the paper that there is a new organization-the Unknitters Club. They ravel out the pairs of sox that are made up of a size eight and a size ten. They undo the sweaters that have an Army neck on a Navy sweater and ones that are suitable only for a man who has one arm six inches longer than the other. 

I just wonder if my one and only pair of Sea Boots landed in their laps? Goodness knows they looked queer enough to be worn by a penguin but I followed directions and trusted to fate. Ever since then I've knitted scarfs-you can't go far wrong on those. Jim insists mine are made for giraffes but I measure them on the floor and, according to the book, they are the specified length. Straight garter stitch, for an hour before bedtime, is better than counting sheep-far more soothing and monotonous. 220

August 27.-The news from Russia is terrible! How long can they hold out in the face of thousands of fresh troops and seemingly endless supplies of tanks and guns and planes being hurled against them? And if they don't hold out! And still you hear people complaining about the few adjustments we have had to make so far in our normal, easy lives. "They make me sick in my stomachs," as Cindy says. 

August 28.-Jim home tonight. The house is shining, the oven is working overtime, the garden is a solid mass of every shade of phlox we have ever planted. The bright cherry ones accent the snowy white ones and the foliage hasn't a spot on it this year. After dusk their fragrance, like newly cut grass, fills the air. I'll bet Jim will smell those and the fried chicken and fresh corn fritters with broiled tomatoes-our own tomatoes-and cheese as soon as he boards the train in Washington.

When he gets a faint suspicion of the lime sherbet he'll leave the train and take to the air. He sent me a clipping last week, "Try our Fresh Lime Freeze. A trip to the tropics through a straw." Tomorrow night I'm going to make one of those steamed blackberry puddings he's so crazy about. My grandmother, who gave me the recipe, always said it was one sure way to keep a man home and contented. She should know because, even after fifty years of marriage my grandfather worshiped the ground she walked on and would never let one of his sons hold her coat for her or push in her chair-"That's my honor and privilege."

This pudding sounds sort of hot for August but someway it isn't-especially when you serve it as dessert after a salad and cold cuts meal. It is snowy white and 221

very light and fluffy and the berries make little pools of warm, red juice that surprise you every time you strike one. The sauce is stewed blackberries that is chilled through and through and served separately with the steaming hot pudding. And Jim and Art think nothing of heaping on a tablespoon of hard sauce as a finishing touch. I beat the white of an egg in it for this particular dish-looks prettier and makes the sauce go farther. And the lemon rind grated over the top makes all the difference in the world. Think I'll start off dinner with hot chicken broth with a dash of curry, then lead into a big bowl of cottage cheese and tomato salad sprinkled with chopped pecans and chives-then the pudding and coffee. How we do love coffee! And how 

I do love to feed my menfolks! 

August 31.-Jim was too tired to eat, too tired to talk and spent the whole week-end in bed. Even Cindy and 

Chunky seemed to get on his nerves so Sally and I tried to keep them outdoors all we could and had picnic meals under the apple tree. They thought it was grand but Sally and I were just going through the motions of being gay and underneath was the worries of our own and the worry about each other. Jim left again this morning and a wire from Jud says he can't make it until the fourth-something about plane reservations from the west coast. Think I'll ask Laurie to come over for a few days. Three of us will be better than two of us. The twelve o'clock noon whistle just blew from the top of the firehouse-it always sounds like a bronchial cow-and it seems at least a week since Jim caught the seven-thirty-eight this morning. "There's nae luck aboot the hoose." 222

September

BENJY PICKS A BLACKOUT-TWO KNIGHTS COME RIDING IN A POLICE CAR-JOURNEY'S END IN LOVERS' MEETING-DIBS AND DABS OF CANNING -"A NEW BABY IS SURE COMBUSTING”—THE JUNIOR AND THE SENIOR-SALVAGE IS THE O. D. 

.. · 

September 1.-An hour ago-midnight-it was August thirty-first and I feel as though I had lived through months and aged at least five years. I phoned Laurie about two o'clock this afternoon and asked her if she could stand the society of two lone females and a couple of young for a few days. I didn't know then why I called her except that Sally and I were getting pretty forlorn and on edge with the seemingly endless waiting for Benjy to make up his mind. But I know now that 

I called her because I had a hunch we were going to need her-and we did. Jim laughs at my hunches and quotes at me Gracie Allen's line, "George says I am positively psychiatric on account of I am such a good dreamer."

Laurie came over about four and we had a tea party on the porch with the youngsters nicely spread over the grass where they jabbered and scattered sandwich crusts to their heart's content. No use telling Cindy that crusts will make her hair curly because it already is and curly hair holds no charms for Chunky. So the birds get the crusts. One of my Sunday School teachers used to say, "Be kind to God's little feathered friends" and I always had visions of friends in human form cov- 223

ered with full plumage-only I never saw one to be kind to! It bothered me a lot.

I can't get to sleep and I've already told Laurie all there is to tell of the last two hours and a half. Sally had been looking sort of queer and peaked all day but I figured that a lot of it was due to the wire from Jud and the disappointment. But at dinner, just as I was cutting the blackberry pie, she gave a funny little cry and doubled up over the edge of the table. 

As soon as she got her breath she said, "Doody, please blow out the candles-they wabble so-and I feel dizzy." 

Laurie gave her one look and stood up, "I'm going to call the doctor!" But Sally held out her hand, “No -wait a while-it was only one pain-and I may not have another for hours. I went to the hospital with 

Cindy on a false alarm and came home again in two days to wait another three weeks. This time I want to be sure."

Laurie and I did up the dishes and went up to see if the children were covered. We came down in a hurry because Hosky was standing at the foot of the stairs making a moaning noise that went right through you. 

That dog knows when something is wrong and his big eyes are full of sympathy. Sally was lying on the davenport with her hands tightly clenched and her mouth a straight, tight line. Her face was so flushed it frightened me. "That's the real thing," pronounced Laurie. "And this time I'm going to call the doctor—no matter what anyone says. I'd give a million dollars if Bruce were around -why couldn't they wait to induct him-I can't stand 224

this Elliott-he knows his job but he's a cold fish. Bruce is so human!" "So am I!" Someway it didn't sound like Sally's voice -more like an animal in pain. The next second it was back to normal. "I'm sorry, Laurie, I don't like Elliott either he is a cold fish-but he's got to see me through this and I can't let myself think how much I wish it were Bruce." 

Laurie couldn't reach the doctor and left word for him to call as soon as they traced him down. It was nearly ten-thirty and the pains were coming five minutes apart and getting harder and harder. Sally was hanging on with every bit of control there was in her. got out the car. Laurie put in her bag and we were off. 

I looked back as we turned the corner to see Laurie standing under the entrance light with Hosky standing beside her. Things at home would be safe. “Please hurry!” I tried to speed up but, with the dim light regulation and the dark twisting roads, I was afraid to take a chance on going too fast. There wasn't a sign of a moon and the stars were too remote and indifferent to be any help. All at once I heard a fire siren blow.

Then another and another. The world was full of them in short, horrible blasts. Church bells began to ring. The street lights went out. Every house within sight went dark and forbidding. Whistles were shrieking. A rising and falling jangle of sound! "Blackout!" said Sally. "Please hurry!" I knew all the rules. I knew I was supposed to extinguish the car lights, draw to the side of the road and stop-but how could I? I kept on driving. I don't know how fast we were going and I didn't care. But suddenly I jammed 225

on the brakes and reached for the emergency. A man was standing in the center of the road with his arm raised. And on the arm was the white band of an Air 

Warden. I stopped not two feet away. "Blackout!" he shouted. "Didn't you hear the Alert?" "I heard everything-I know it's a blackout-but I can't stop-I have to go on-now please get out of my way!" He didn't budge and it's pretty impossible to run a man down deliberately unless you've had a lot more practice at that sort of thing than I have. I told him about Sally and the pains five minutes apart. I was so frantic that I even threw in the silly remark, "Her husband is on the west coast and my husband is in Washington so you've got to let us go!" I haven't any idea why I threw in that piece of information unless I thought it might work on his sympathies. Jim always says I have no compunctions about using every trick in the bag when I'm trying to get my own way. Jim has known me a long time. But this man weakened-only for a split second though. "Well-all right." Then he changed his mind. "You'll have to pull over to the side of the road and wait—those are my orders." I felt Sally grab my arm with both her hands. Then she let out a scream that simply paralyzed me. Someday I'm going to find out whether she did it on purpose for Sally is not a screamer. It worked! The 

Warden was on the running board and yelling in my ear-"Step on it, lady-I'll take you to the next Postand turn on those bright lights-that'll get the cops if there are any in this section. I'll have to get you an escort or you'll be stopped by every Warden from here to Malstead Hospital." 226

We drove, as near as I can remember, about three miles. Sally never made another sound, but I could feel her tense up beside me at such regular intervals that I knew nothing had stopped. Nothing would stop-until the finish. At least five more miles to go and that man yelling at me, "Go faster-step on it!" until I was so nervous it's a wonder I didn't smash us all against a telephone pole. I saw another figure, with upraised arm, standing by the side of the road as we came around a bend. I swerved the car and went past him-no use taking time out to discuss Civic Duty. I heard his whistle blowing wildly and then the sound of a police siren. 

Never has anything sounded so beautiful to me. Never will anything ever look so perfectly heavenly as the sight of that shining white night patrol car coming over the rise of the hill! No knight of old in gleaming armor was ever more welcomed by ladies in distress than were those two, fat Irish cops. 

The Warden, with what little breath he had left, tried to explain but he didn't need to finish. "You two ladies get yourselves in the back seat and 

I'll drive you to the horsepital the short way. My brother officer can be the escort in the police car. And the Warden can sit forninst him in order that he can be taken home again to his lawful section. And don't you be worryin'-" I could have thrown my arms around him and kissed in sheer gratitude. But I put my arms around Sally instead and we clung to each other until we pulled up at the hospital entrance. As soon as the door opened she was "the patient"-I was something very much in the way and not wanted. The nurses took charge, the doctor had arrived. I hung around as long 227

as I could. Then the continuous blast of All Clear sounded and they politely told me, "You can go now. 

The patient is resting comfortably and we'll call you whenever necessary. Dr. Elliott says it may not be before tomorrow morning." 

September 2.—It is tomorrow morning and no word from Sally. I tried to sleep and finally gave it up. My watch said three o'clock and I couldn't, for the life of me, think what day of the week it was. I phoned the hospital but all I got was, "Dr. Elliott's patient is still in labor-she has not gone up to the Delivery Room." "When will the doctor be back?" "Dr. Elliott is still in the hospital. Dr. Elliott never leaves an obstetric case until the baby is delivered and the mother is comfortable." What a comfort and what a man! Will I ever learn to hold my tongue and withhold my judgment until I know what I'm talking about?

Laurie smelled the coffee I was making and came down. 

I told her, “The doctor hasn't left Sally-he won't leave her until the party's over." "Did you ever," said Laurie, "feel like seven kinds of an idiot and wish you'd been born with more sense?" "Lots of times. Why?" "I went shooting off half cocked to Sally about what a cold fish this Elliott is-and then look how he turns out! I upset her and I was certainly unfair to him. I'm simply not fit—” and she started to cry. I joined in and we both felt better. There are lots of advantages to being a woman. I suppose Laurie meant she wasn't fit to be a Nurse's Aid-but I didn't ask her. But I did think of all the times when I have felt I wasn't fit to be the mother of our children or the wife of Jim. Then 228

you pick yourself up and go on with the comforting sense that keeping on trying probably counts for something in the Record. At least you hope so. I told Laurie all this and she started to smile. Then she leaned over and took my face between her hands and kissed me. I have never known her to do anything so impulsive and humanly warm before, except with children. It's a thrilling thing to watch her breaking away from the repression of years.

September 3-Laurie had to report on her job today so Beth took care of Cindy and Chunky while I went to the hospital. Cindy nearly breaks my heart. She is so quiet and her eyes so pleading when she asks, a dozen times a day, “When is my mommy coming home?” and "Where is my baby brother?" I can't answer either question really for all I get from the hospital by phone is, "The patient in four eighteen is still in labor-she has not gone up to the Delivery Room-her condition is good-Dr. Elliott is still here." When I get in to see 

Sally there isn't anything I can do except to, "Step into the hall, please," when a nurse comes in for something or other-you'd think I was almost old enough to know the facts of life and be allowed around-after all I have heard of enemas and such. Sally is too miserable and half doped to know whether I am there or not but I have to go for the little comfort it gives me to see her. 

All she ever says is, "How much longer? Why don't they do something?" or, over and over, "Where is 

Jud?" I only wish I knew! Somewhere in transit-he's left the coast. The few brief times I've talked with 

Dr. Elliott he has been everything kind and patient. He 229

insists Sally is all right and it is just a slow labor. Dear 

God, let it be over soon! 

September 4.-Bruce is back and has been called in consultation. They've decided to wait a few hours more. 

Wire from Jud and he arrives at five this morning. I came out of Sally's room last night just too torn with fear and emotion to hold on another minute. I leaned against the wall down the corridor and tried to get control of myself. The curtained, swinging door of the room nearest me came open and a man in blue overalls walked out. He stopped when he saw me-"You too?" he said. "Can I help you?" The kindness and the sympathy were just too much and the next minute I had my head on his shoulder and his arm was around me. 

The human comfort of it made me realize, more than ever, how much I have missed and needed Jim. When 

I finally quieted down the man gave my shoulder a final, reassuring pressure. "I know," he said. "Believe me, I know," and he was gone. It was a floor nurse who told me his wife had just died in that room in back of us. I'll probably never see him again, I don't know his name, but somehow, somewhere, may I have the chance to be as kind and understanding to someone else in deep trouble as he was to me. I only wish I had known how much bigger a sorrow he was facing. Even now I might be of some help but the hospital refuses to give me his name. I wonder if he has children? 

September 4.–Midnight-Sally's baby was born at ten o'clock tonight! Jud was with her and everything is fine! Benjy is another little girl-perfect, healthy. Sally is all right. Bruce stopped by to tell Laurie and me. He looks tired and says Dr. Elliott is all in but thankful 230

they waited for the baby to be born naturally. I was so relieved after the strain that I had to get up and do something, so I went out to the kitchen to make coffee and cinnamon toast for the three of us. I could hear 

Laurie and Bruce talking for a while and then absolute silence. I called out something, once, and had no answer. 

When I came in with the tray I knew why. Bruce was sitting in Jim's big chair and Laurie on the floor beside him with her head on his knee. His hand was resting on it and stroking the soft waves. I stood in the doorway too astonished to move. Then Bruce looked up-"Well -this is it-I've waited a long time to find her-and she was here-right here-but now it's too late!" "Too late?" I couldn't bear the hopelessness in his voice-his down moods are so down. "Unless I'm one of the lucky ones to come backsome do." 

Laurie sat there, saying nothing, but I never saw anyone look more beautiful and with such shining faith. 

Her love may bring him back—I hope it will. 

September 6.-Just back from the hospital and Sally is gaining strength and looking like herself. Flowers from all the neighbors and their old friends wherever they have lived. She and Jud get friendship because they give it in such generous measure. Lottie had sent a pink begonia from her small greenhouse. I think it had a thousand waxy pink blossoms and she had dressed it up in pale blue crepe paper with a huge gauze bow. I saw the baby through the glass partition of the nursery. I suppose that only a doting grandmother should say that she smiled-but she certainly did. Even Sally and Jud laughed at me when I told them. The nurse said it was 231

gas but I know better. She did smile. Anyway Cindy and Chunky believed me and Cindy tells everyone in sight, "I have a new baby sister. Would you like to see her?" Even Hosky seems delighted and is jumping around like a puppy these days. Maybe it was the extra good beef kidney I cooked for him in celebration, but

I think it is the general atmosphere of joy that is singing through the house. Hosky is a most discerning dog. 

September 8.-Mrs. Melrose is giving a farewell dinner for Bruce tonight. Jim is home and has promised to go. Laurie has a new dinner dress that is soft gray with embroidery in yellow and she looks simply stunning. All the beauty clinics in the world can't give a woman that self-confident, shining look that being in love gives her. Bruce leaves on Sunday. Why don't they get married before he goes? Some crazy notion of his— 

I'm certain it isn't hers-that he may not come back and he doesn't want to tie her down. Maybe his reasoning is sensible but it doesn't make sense. Captain Bruce Stevens can be as stubborn as they make them and this seems to be his present mood. "They're both of age," says Jim when I argue about it. I guess that's as good an answer as any. Jim does not sympathize with the Girl Scout in me when I get a sudden streak of doing my daily good deed and trying to run other people's lives.

September 9.-The dinner was a great success-two 

Colonels, a Major, a Captain, and a Lieutenant Commander of the Navy. Mrs. Melrose kept introducing 

Bruce as, "My dear Captain Stevens-a brilliant physician and he saved my life when I had double pneumonia and was literally at death's door!" I heard her, in an 232

aside to one of the guests, "He's exactly the sort of man 

I simply adore--he's so masterful!" I wonder if she tells her meek little husband that? Once during the dinner he tipped over one of the exquisite tall goblets and the lady drew herself up reprovingly, "My dear-one would think you weren't accustomed to such things!" Why do men let women get like that? I suppose it's like putting on weight-it creeps up on you gradually. Many more dinners like that and I'll be ready for a stylish stout model. I must ask for her recipe for the mocha soufflé. 

September 11.-Made grape butter and crabapple jelly today-grapes from our house and crabapples from Lottie's. The extra sugar I have been saving up all summer even though I probably could have asked for extra from the Rationing Board. The trouble was that I couldn't remember how much I had put up last year and that is one of the questions asked. My non-mathematical brain wouldn't add up the dibs and dabs of canning I did. 

The shelves were full but I added to them gradually. 

One of the women at Red Cross told me the other day that she and her husband knew, to a penny, exactly how much they had spent on everything for the ten years of their marriage-even every movie and coke and soda! 

I'm glad Jim and I don't know-it would take half the fun out of it. Life wasn't meant to be put down in two ruled columns like that. I hate regularity and routine and yet I got the biggest kick out of counting nine pints grape butter and fourteen glasses of jelly and labeling and arranging them in neat rows in the cupboard. I have a most unformed character with a million contraof 233

dictions in it. But Jim doesn't mind. He says I drive him crazy but I'll never bore him to death. 

September 14.-The children started to school today. 

Cindy in Play School and Chunky in kindergarten. He feels vastly superior and says grandly, "She's just a little children but I'm a Sen-ior!" They walked, hand in hand, around the corner, but they came back at noon with 

Cindy walking with a little girl and Chunky, five feet ahead, walking with one of the boys. "We don't like old boys!" says Cindy. may "Girls are just sissies!" says Chunky. But all afternoon the two of them have been playing together as usual. 

Like politicians they have their public to consider. 

September 15.-Laurie and I have been reading in the long evenings and have just finished They Were Expendable. I hope it will be read and re-read by every one of us who has the slightest tendency to complain about the piffling little inconveniences of wartime civilian living. It made me proud to belong to the same human race as those men and it made me very humble to realize how little I deserve it. We're all to blame for the "Too little and too late." Too dumb and complacent to realize the danger that was creeping up on us-too selfish to care until it struck close to home through the lives of our own people. 

September 16.-Coming home from market this afternoon I heard the fire siren and then more blasts from everything blowable. My first thought was a fire, my second a real air raid and my last was right-a surprise test. Most of the cars kept on moving and a few women 

Air Wardens came dashing out but the general effect was business as usual. The one place in town that was 234

ordered and orderly was at the High School. The students were just coming out as the Alert sounded and they all marched back in again to take up their various posts according to instructions. I wouldn't be surprised, when the real thing comes, if they wouldn't be quieting panicky parents instead of the other way around. I'd rather have Peg and Art telling me what to do than a whole lot of older people I know. 

September 18.-Saw in the paper this morning that

Grand Central Palace has been taken over by the Army for an Induction Center to replace the one on Governors Island. It certainly is better located and more convenient, but I wonder if this means they are getting ready to move down the draft age? We won't know until after election, but it looks that way. What a changing that old building has had-Army disembarkation hospital in the First World War, then the excited holiday crowds at the Flower Show and the Motor Boat Show and all the rest of the big exhibitions, and now long lines of men going in for examination and induction into this war. It will be a long time before the old gaiety returns. 

September 19.-Sally coming home tonight with Miss 

Elizabeth Barbara Greene aged two weeks and two days. It will seem funny to hear a tiny baby, crying like a forlorn kitten, after all these years of a growing up family. Funny but nice. I feel just like an excited grandmother and it's wonderful! A dozen times today

I have gone into Sally's room to give another poke to the curtains, another feel to the crib, another look around to see that everything is ready and just right. 235

It has been for days but, as Claudine says, "A little new baby is sure combusting!" 

September 20.-Claudine certainly hit the nail on the head even though she may not have meant just what she said. Babs took one look at the old home place last night and started off like a four-alarm fire. She didn't like any of our faces, or her bed, or her mummy. She didn't like anything-period. At eight o'clock this morning she fell into a peaceful sleep and is still at it except when Sally wakes her up to be fed. I have a feeling she is gathering strength for the night shift when she'll really go to work. I wonder just how innocent babies are? Seems to me they know a whole lot about running the universe-their way. Cindy remarks, “My baby sister skreeks!" Chunky simply observes the young lady and says nothing but his expression is one of bafflement. 

No use to tell him that he was once little like that and yelled like a Comanche Indian. His reply is, "Boloney!"

Kindergarten is doing a great deal to increase his vocabulary. "All the guys talk tough like that." 

September 22.-Had almost an hour to spend in the garden today and was out there two hours and more. 

I never saw so much rubbish from what were once flowering plants. It's a pity the stuff can't be turned in for salvage. I did find a rusty trowel-one that Jim distinctly remembered returning to Paul Gibson. Just as I was positive I had sent back one of Beth's patterns and found it looking straight at me when I cleaned out a drawer months later. Anyway, the trowel went into the salvage box because Jim had bought Paul a new one. 

There were several varieties of new weeds doing nicely and I pulled those out or dug them up. I suppose some 236

miserable fragment of a root still burrows in the ground from each one of them to start up fresh and vigorous in the spring. I can't quite get the philosophy of the people who believe the world is all lovely and sweet and that evil things have no reality. They always quote at you, "Dark is simply the absence of light and has no entity of its own." How do they explain weeds and mosquitoes and Hitler and Hirohito? 

September 23.-Read a strange and thrilling story in the paper this morning of the rescue of eighteen men who had been adrift, from their torpedoed freighter, for thirty-two days. The strange element in it was the fact that the Captain of the rescuing ship had set his course and then, for no reason that he could explain, five minutes later returned to the chart room and changed the course five degrees to the north. Without that last minute shift of course the ship would have passed the life boat unsighted. Did those almost hopeless men someway get a message through the ether waves? Perhaps that sounds fantastic but no more so than sitting in your living room, here in America, and hearing Winston 

Churchill speaking to the British Parliament. Why do people laugh at telepathy, and other intangibles of the mind and spirit, and accept electricity and radio as commonplace facts? Bruce says he knows some of his patients have pulled through because someone who loved them has refused to let go and give up hope when medical science has come to the end of its knowledge. 

September 26.-Great celebration-Babs likes us! She has settled down to sleeping nights! Still on her two

A.M. feeding, of course, but she takes it like a contented little pink Berkshire pig and then falls back to sleep 237

again. What a blessing. She still skreeks enough in the daytime to satisfy Cindy who is so proud of the loud noise she can make when she is so little. "There is a girl at Play School who is lots bigger and she kicks and skreeks on the floor but she doesn't make such a much noise as Babs. Her mother reasons with her a long time but she still kicks and won't go home nice-the way

do. She sits on the Naughty Chair but I always act nice!" "Tell that to the Marines," comments Sally. 

September 27.-A long Special Delivery letter from

Art today-one and a half pages which sets an all time high for his correspondence- 

DEAR MOM AND DAD: 

Well, how goes it? Things are looking up here and, if luck is with me, hope to get my wings about the first week in October. This has been the longest and the shortest seven months I've ever put in and-in all ways the swellest. Thought I'd never get used to the 5:50

A.M. business, but it's an old story now. Imagine your darling son, clothed and in his right mind, standing at attention for breakfast at 6:15-it seems it can be did! 

I laugh when I think of how Dad used to yell up the stairs at least five times to get me to move and then I never combed my hair until he fired me out of the dining room to look half civilized. Remember that verse you used to read to Peg and me? It ended up, "How improved our Willie is." The Air Corps will make a man of me yet.

Jim and I read on through a strange world of unfamiliar words and then came to the last paragraph. 

Don't let this upset you, but it sort of looks like maybe we won't get leave when we're commissioned. 

They need us right now wherever we'll do the most 238

good, and the grapevine has it that commissions and orders will be given at one and the same time. Then we proceed to our post and a few days' leave is granted from there. No telling where it may be and home might not be handy. Hope I can make it, but don't count on it. 

We couldn't look at each other. We couldn't say a word. Jim got up and began tapping his pipe against the side of the fireplace. I lit a cigarette. He sat down in his chair and I perched on the arm of it-slowly the smoke drifted upward and swirled together. His arm went around me and I held his hand. How many times we have comforted each other without words, for silence is so much deeper and more comforting when two people understand.

September 28.-I simply flew through the work this morning-brought Sally her breakfast, fed the children and Jim, washed dozens of diapers-Babs has the usual faulty plumbing of the young-made scrapple from the remains of the Sunday chicken, dusted the living room and collected the Sunday paper from nine points of the compass. Then I went out to the garden and began pruning the fall shrubs and raking up all the debris I could find. The pile by the side of the road grew higher and higher and I felt better and better. When I set it afire a breeze blew up and it kept me hopping to prevent it spreading to the bank of dry grass. But, as the flames crackled and the wind blew the tension, tied up and tight inside me, seemed to be released. I felt alive and part of something so big that I didn't matter. Religion and faith comfort me when I can be quiet enough to let them seep through my spirit. But working with my hands and coming close to fire and air and grubby 239

earth give me a different kind of uplift when I am in too much turmoil for inactive serenity. 

September 29.-Another big metal salvage drive is on and Lottie called me up to remind me the collection trucks would start tomorrow. "I've gone through everything we own and turned in the last hairpin that I'm not using-I did it two months ago and there isn't another thing!" "Look again-the trucks will start at seven in the morning." Lottie is not one to be lightly turned aside which is one reason she has done such a grand job. The other reason is that she is a grand person at anything she tackles-friendship being one of them. I knew, however, that it was no use to look-but I did anyway. Net result-one old radiator tucked up in the rafters of the garage, one iron swing frame that has been in the dark corner back of the furnace since eight years ago when we bought the new one, fourteen odd keys, over a dozen wire coat hangers, a rusty wire corn popper that we hadn't used since I blew in ninety-eight cents for an electric one, an aluminum plunger cup and part of the arm that Claudine had broken off one morning when she decided to surprise me and wash a sizeable 

Oriental rug and had overloaded the machine. "I ain't doin' a thing and it rare up an' bus' off right in ma face," she explained. Mechanical things have a decided antipathy to Claudine. Three bobbins from the old sewing machine that I inherited and then traded in turned up strangely enough in the medicine cabinet. I could have sworn that I had cleaned that cabinet thoroughly and that they weren't there then-housekeeping certainly makes you believe in pixies! Well, I gathered 240

together quite a pile of junk and dragged and carried it out to the curb as instructed. The neighbors were doing the same and our street looked like one of the news reels showing hastily barricaded roads. Half an hour later it looked worse! The children were coming home from school and greeted the heaps with shouts of joy. "Look what I found! Boy, will this be neat! Give me a hand with the radiator-must weigh a ton!" "Go get your wagon, Fat, and we can take a lot of stuff."

It seems there is a salvage drive on at school too and the point system between classes has stirred up real competition. By the time the willing workers had departed with their treasure we needed one of those road signs that say, "Road slightly obstructed. Proceed at your own risk." Which would have been a decided understatement. Anyway the stuff will all go into the same piles no matter who collects it, or how, and the youngsters got a great kick out of it. 

September 30.-Jim and Jud held the fort tonight and 

Sally and I stepped out to the movies to see Cary Grant and Jean Arthur in The Talk of the Town. The last time I saw Jean she was in some romance of the wild where she talked to deer and things-must have been years ago. Sally says it was Jean Parker in the earlier picture. Anyway this time she was more human and the dialogue was grand. All about a noble young workman with ideals and no sense and a prominent lawyer-in the movies they are all prominent-with theories and no sense. But the little woman fixes the whole thing and they all lived happily ever after. Good entertainment and no strain on the brain. Sally still a little wabbly but 241

enjoyed a glimpse of the big world even though the bright lights of the movie entrance are no more and the main street is as dim as fading old eyes. After the feature they put on The Battle of Midway in technicolor. What a picture! It was thrilling and heart breaking and gave you the shivers and the blues and then lifted you right up again with pride and amazement at the courage and endurance of those boys. I wish the director had left out the little side scenes of the suffering mother, with the Service flag in the window, pleading brokenly, "Get them to the hospital. Please get them to the hospital quick!" That part seemed phony and Hollywood and the rest was so real and American.

We stopped at the War stamp booth on the way out and bought a stamp a piece for the children's books. 

They do fill up, bit by bit, and you're surprised yourself when you finish the last page. The woman in the booth looked awfully tired and I'll bet she was-these volunteers may make good copy for the cartoonists but most of them earn their uniforms with weary backs and aching feet.

We drove down on account of Sally and felt very grand and sort of apologetic. Nearly everyone was walking and the streets were almost empty of traffic. 

Funny when you think that, a few months ago, you had a hard time to find parking space within four blocks. The quiet and more leisurely pace is pleasant. 

Wonder, when this is all over, if we'll go dashing around again as usual. Maybe we've learned something and maybe we haven't.

Got home to find our two men with paper spread all over the dining-room table and scribbling away like 242

mad. The evening news was spread out, too, and first one and then the other would pick it up and pore over it. "You're crazy!" said Jud. "Floor areas times degree days divided by K equals fuel requirements. You've subtracted instead of adding on that last line." "You're nuts," said Jim.

So they both explained it very slowly and carefully to Sally and me. We didn't understand a word of it except that we are going to be colder this winter than we were last. The bed socks may not fit into any formula but I'll bet they'll be welcome. The paper says there is a request for mathematics teachers to help on local ration boards to aid in fuel calculations. If Jim is out of town when the application has to be filled out 

I only hope they'll assign a private tutor to me. Otherwise we'll have no oil or I'll land in Leavenworth. I was informed by my lord and master that we are in the B thermal zone and that our average winter has 

5,270 degree days. Jim is awfully smart about some things—but I can bake an apple pie that makes him purr with contentment. That accomplishment may win me no medals but it's important just the same. 243

October 

WE PLAY GOING TO JERUSALEM-AT JONES BEACH -THE LIKES OF ME ON MY HANDS AND KNEES— WE'LL STICK IT OUT TOGETHER-MILTON DE- LIVERS A POSTCARD-GEORGE IS IN HIS GLORY- ONLY ONE CUP OF COFFEE A DAY-HALLOWEEN! 

October 2.-Schools were closed today because of 

Teachers' Institute and Sally and I were so restless, she waiting to hear from Jud and I with Jim away and Art on my mind. This house without a man in it is just so much walls and roof with no point to anything you do. 

I went through the motions of straightening things up and came into the living room with a pile of mending. 

I hate to mend and there is something of the hair shirt martyr in my makeup that always makes me mend when I'm feeling the lowest. "Sun is nice and bright," remarked Sally.

When really close friends begin to talk about the weather they are pretty far gone but, at least, it's a nice safe topic when the tears are near the surface and you want to keep them back. "The air is crispy-real autumn," I said, sort of automatically. "The garden looks beautiful-so many lovely colors."

All we reminded me of was that poor, brainless girl in Pygmalion who repeated drilled phrases at the proper moment at tea parties. Then another scene popped into my mind-the one where Leslie Howard finds the girl, 244

blubbering in the rain, on a curbstone in London. I recalled it to Sally for we both loved that movie. "Remember," said Sally, "the speech he made to her?

It was something like this-'Here you are, a human being, made in God's image and you sit there like a squashed cabbage leaf.'" "Let's go to Jones Beach!" I said suddenly. "We haven't been there all summer on account of the gas. 

The youngsters would love it and we can park Babs in a clothes basket and take along her bottle packed in ice. 

There's a Sterno somewhere around and I'll dig it out. 

I'll call Aline and her sister and we'll have a picnic." 

Sally is a peach about falling in with your mood and all of a minute plans. Some young mothers would have thought of thirty-nine reasons why it wasn't the sensible thing to do and it might rain and the baby is so young and I don't feel quite up to it yet and so on far into the night. What's the fun in doing anything that is perfectly reasonable and sensible? Maybe we would use up all our gas and couldn't run the car for a week. Maybe it would rain. Maybe the children might catch cold and maybe we'd be dead on our feet. But, living with Jim all these years has taken practically all the maybes out of my system. We've had a wonderful time doing foolish things and thoroughly enjoying them. I called Aline and her sister and they were delighted. In fact they were so enthusiastic that it pepped Sally and me up a couple more notches. I called Cindy and Chunky and there was no sales talk needed there either. The sun was shining, the air was crisp, and across the hills in Great Bay the trees were turning to crimson and gold and soft, muted browns. 245

I went out to the kitchen and hauled some boiled potatoes and the end of a tongue out of the refrigerator. 

Into the big brown picnic crock they went with a smooth, creamy cheese sauce blending the diced potatoes and the chopped tongue. Just enough slivered onion sizzled in the butter before I made the sauce and, to add the final touch, a top layer of buttered bread cubes. Into the oven it went while I cut some carrot strips and celery and stored them in a fruit jar. A big thermos of cocoa for the children and another of coffee for the grown-ups. A few bunches of grapes, some apples, and the maple black-walnut cookies that Aline insisted upon bringing and I didn't argue with her-and luncheon was served-or at least ready to be. At the last minute 

I put the hot cover on the bubbling crock and wrapped the whole thing in layers of newspaper. That crock has gone to many a picnic with this family and is simply soaked with memories of good times. It's funny-or is it?-how attached you get to your household belongings. The longer the war goes on the more I seem to treasure the familiar things of home, the familiar and dear ways of the people I'm used to. These anchors to windward are all that keep my spiritual boat from floundering in the storm of tension. That sounds almost high brow and literary-it's so hard to express simple things simply. 

The picnic was one grand success-in some ways.

Babs slept in the shelter of a sand dune and Cindy and Chunky raced up and down the beach as though they had been penned up in an apartment for years. Children respond to plain, unvarnished nature and feel at home in it. None of the silly, artificial restraints of civilization. 246

The water was a sparkling blue green with just enough white caps to trim the edges and add variety.

The sky looked like a Maxfield Parrish painting-such a clear, breath-taking blue with a few clouds scattered artistically and the sea gulls swooped and curved with their effortless grace.

We spread out a couple of old army blankets and sat down to lunch. Chunky went into his usual strong, silent-man act that always comes at the sight of food. 

But, all at once, I noticed he wasn't eating. I looked again-his eyes were filled with tears. you sick?" "Chunky! Are 

His lip started to tremble and, with that, Cindy threw her arms around his neck and let out a series of sobs. 

What on earth? "I wished Hosky was here and his eyes looked sad when we went away!" said Chunky at last. I had explained, so carefully before we left, how dogs weren't allowed on the beach and I had plumb forgotten that the rule wasn't enforced out of season. A wabbly little pup was trotting over the sand toward us and that was what had started Chunky off. There wasn't a soul in sight and the dog looked about as big as a sand crab against that miles of open beach and open ocean. He came straight for us, at least as straight as his young steering apparatus would let him, and cuddled up against Chunky. He began to lick Cindy's hand and then reached that minute pink tongue up to her face as she leaned down. He certainly was as cute as a bug's ear! "Her would be nice for Hosky-wouldn't he?" asked 

Cindy conversationally. 247

Chunky looked at her solemnly. "He is a he, Cindy, and you don't call a he a her." "I do-and he is cute, isn't her?”

Chunky just sat there cuddling the puppy and staring into space. I might have known a thought was brewing. 

He always lets his ideas sort of simmer until they come to a full boil and then out with them in measured and deliberate words. They came at last. "I was just a thinking that if Hosky had this baby puppy to take care of-and sort of play with-his eyes wouldn't look so sad." "All Hoskys have sad eyes, Chunky," I tried to explain. "It doesn't mean he feels unhappy-that's just the way his looks are made." "We could make him some new looks if we gave him this baby puppy to take care of and sort of play with.” 

Nothing like a one track mind with a bull dog grip! 

Chunky isn't stubborn-only persistent. But enough was enough. "Let's play a game of Going to Jerusalem and the big bumps in the sand are the chairs and I'll sing, and when I stop, everybody has to find a chair. There are six people and we'll have only five chairs, so the one left over has to sing next." I started to sing, "Oh, don't -sit un-der the ap-ple tree-with any-body else—but me, any-body else but me-" and then I stopped. There was a wild scramble for the sand hummock chairs and 

Sally was it. Then Aline and Nancy and Cindy. The favorite songs, from Onward Christmus Sol-gers, as rendered by Cindy, to Whistle While You Work, picked by Aline. Then it was Chunky's turn at last, because he had stopped to pick up the puppy who was 

248

trying to keep up with the procession and tumbling over at every third step. Chunky was it. "Ready everybody!" he announced and began marching around without a sound. "Sing, Chunky," I called, "that's part of the game."

But he kept right on in steady silence. All at once Aline stopped dead still. "Listen!" None of us moved-her voice was so strange, tense and yet so ringing with happiness. Not a sound to be heard but the swish of the waves on the sand and the long notes of the wind through the wiry beach grass. I started to say something but her hand touched my arm. "Please-listen!" I began to get a little frightened and looked over at Sally and Nancy. Aline has been under such strain that I thought anything might happen. I'm not sure yet what did-I can only hope she's right. For suddenly she went limp and sat down on the sand. The tears came in her eyes but they were from happiness and faith-not grief. "I heard Bob whistling-She'll be coming 'round the mountain when she comes, when she comes-it's the tune he always whistled when he came up the path to the house at night and whenever he left again. Now I know! Someday he'll be back and I can wait until he is." 

None of us said anything. There was nothing to say.

We packed up the things with sort of a silent signal to start for home. It wasn't until we were in the car, and going onto the causeway that I noticed Chunky still had the puppy. There hadn't been another soul on the beach so I couldn't figure where to return it. Not a State 1 249

Trooper in sight. We took the puppy home and it's fast asleep in Chunky's bed-with Hoskins standing guard. 

A most unhygienic arrangement and very foolish of me --but I never have bragged about my excellent judgment and disciplinary powers. What was it Aline heard? 

Or did she hear anything? I wish I could talk to Bruce about her. Maybe Laurie would be a help-she senses people, just as he does, and sees below the surface of deep emotion. 

October 3.-No word from Art. Jim gets in town at midnight and will probably be too tired to talk but just looking at him and having him near is everything. Saturday marketing to do the children all over the place, the puppy into everything and I'm thankful for all of them. We simply can't keep that puppy-and I know we're going to. Even Hosky mothers the little thing with an aloof and dignified affection as if to say, "Well, somebody has to look after it."

October 4.-Jim's train was late-something about standing on a siding while troop trains went through. 

He was too tired to say much, but it was good to see him sitting in his big chair and easing off with the soothing fragrance of his pipe while I fixed up a tray at two in the morning. The house was so still that Hosky's snoring rose and fell with a steady and sort of peaceful rhythm. The chunks of wild cherry crackled and sizzled in the fireplace. One of the children would shift around in bed and talk in a sleepy, contented murmur. If only 

Peg and Art had been in their beds, too, what heavenly comfort it would have been. My mind and heart goes out to both of them across the miles and, if love can conquer distance and separation, they must be very near. 250

I wish we'd hear from Art-anything would be easier than this uncertain facing of uncertainty.

We finally got to bed after eating big blue bowls of hot cornmeal mush and milk and getting rid of most of the fresh apple sauce with a top frosting of cinnamon and sugar. There is something about homely, simple food that soothes taut nerves and rests tired muscles. 

At least that is the kind of food Jim likes when he's starved to death and thinks he doesn't want a thing to eat. But there was something troubling him last night too deeply to be talked about. I don't know what it is but, sooner or later, we'll figure the problem out together, as we have so many others. 

October 6.-Cindy is sitting on the side porch with the puppy, who has now been named Jonesy in honor of Jones Beach, and singing at the top of her small voice, "Three lit-tle sissers, three lit-tle sissers-stay home -and read theirs magazines-you can tell it to the sojers -you can tell it to the shailors-you can tell-it-to the werines." The last line comes out with a lusty shout and

Chunky joins in with his husky little boy bass. Jonesy sleeps peacefully through it all and snuggles closer in Cindy's lap. I'll get attached to that complete nuisance yet, even though he much prefers the living-room rug to the carefully placed newspaper near his box, and confines his tooth sharpening exercises to my red bedroom slippers instead of his scientific rubber bone. Jonesy is a rugged individualist which makes him interesting though annoying. 

October 8.-I finally decided tonight that it is up to me to fill in the fiendish oil ration questionnaire. When 

Jim is home so little I haven't the heart to urge him to 251

do the measuring and figuring so Sally and I started in. 

I've long had a suspicion that this house is much too big -now I'm sure of it. The likes of me, on my hands and knees, crawling along dragging a tape measure after me would have been good material for the candid camera shots of a tabloid. "What's six feet four inches and four feet nine inches?" I would say to Sally. "It says you don't have to count the inches-just even feet." "But four inches and nine inches make almost a foot," 

I argued. "It makes an inch over a foot-and you didn't measure the brick part on the fireplace-and what about the bay at that end where the desk sits?"

I sat down and read, once again, how to figure on irregularly shaped rooms. Only a genius at higher mathematics could make head or tail out of it. But if we ever buy another house it is going to be shaped like a square box with four equal divisions where the rooms go. 

We finally finished up at the stroke of midnight, kindly assisted by Jonesy who took the tape measure for many a romp under the pieces of furniture. The only thing that I'll stand in court on is the one sentence in the questionnaire which says, "The facts and statements herein contained are true to the best of my knowledge." 

October 12.-If we ever have a real air raid I am sure that it will come when I am right in the middle of doing something. Tonight I was boiling eggs for tomorrow's lunch and baking prune-filled cookies which are a tricky bit--but good. I heard sirens blowing but paid no atten- 252

tion because we have fire alarms nearly every day when somebody's oil burner decides to go on the rampage.

I finally came to and decided it was a surprise blackout. 

Called upstairs to Sally and dutifully put out all the lights on the first floor. Then I sat in the quiet and waited. It was unusually still and I could hear the Wardens' whistles clear across the bay. A voice shouted, close at hand, "Put out that light!" Queer, I thought, how long it takes some people to catch on. The shout came again. Then a banging on the door. Hosky gave a low growl and Jonesy wandered in and out between my feet as I groped my way to the front of the house.

The Air Warden stood, as stern as a Justice of the 

Supreme Court. "Put out your lights-there's a glow from your kitchen windows." 

Those blamed eggs were boiling merrily away and the gas flame shining like a mild beacon. Funny how long it takes some people to catch on! 

October 18.-It seemed to me this sunny, crisp Sunday morning that I couldn't stand it another day if we didn't hear from Art and if Jim didn't tell me what he has been brooding about for the last two weeks. You can't be married for as long as we have been without sensing every mood and longing, with all the strength there is in you, to be of some help. There is nothing worse than imagining a million things that might be wrong—and never guessing right. 

Well, I can stop guessing and worrying now because

I know and it's far easier to face something definite. 

After the children were in bed tonight and Sally had gone upstairs to write to Jud-somewhere in the combat 253

area (wherever that may be) all she has is an army numbered address that looks like code, Jim sat down in his big chair and Hosky settled down against his knee with his eyes looking pleadingly into Jim's face. I started to knit with knit one, purl two, knit three going around and around with a certain soothing monotony. Suddenly 

Jim stood up. "Like to take a walk with Hosky?" he asked casually.

At the word walk that dignified animal nearly wriggled out of his curly coat and dashed for the front door. He roams over hills and fields all day and every day but a walk with the boss is something pretty special. 

The stars were very bright and the still air was scented with the winey perfume of the apples on our tree and the herby fragrance of the chrysanthemums in the front garden. Jim's arm went around me as we started up the dark road. The dimmed out street lights make your own small world seem very personal and close and the sky sort of tucked around you in a much more intimate way than when it was lighted by the glow from the city and the blaze of the corner lamps. 

When we came to the woods at the end, Jim stood still and my heart stood still with him. "You've got to help me decide," he said. "The company had a request from the government that they lend me for the duration-sort of a traveling trouble shooter as far as I can make out. I'll have to go and I'm glad to be of help-but what do you want to do, trail along or stay here?" "What do you want me to do? What would make you happiest?" It seemed to me, at that minute, as though the whole world were upside down and my 254

head spinning so that I couldn't think. I could only feel and the feeling hurt. Jim turned and we started back very slowly until we stood in front of our own house with the friendly lights shining out through the livingroom windows and the trailing sprays of ivy making patterns against the glass. "I'd like to think of you here-that would make me happiest. Sally and the children need you and this is our home. Art has carried it away with him and Peg counts on it as sort of a back log in her wanderings. 

Remember how they felt when we were planning on an apartment?" 

Deep inside me I know he's right. There is something here too important to us all to break up-too necessary to our spiritual needs. Jim will be gone on longer, more tiring trips, living in defense areas in any kind of hotel room he can get. When he does come home there must be a home to come to-the one we have built together through the years of our love and comradeship. When 

Peg and Art are back the cooky jar must be full and on the same corner of the kitchen cupboard and the old, familiar things bid them welcome. Sally and Cindy and little Babs, with her yawn like a kitten, Hosky and

Chunky and even Jonesy have tucked themselves into my heart and we'll stick it out together. 

October 19.-A card from Art this morning-I could hear Milton shouting up the hill before I could see the top of his hat-"Your boy's landed safe and sound!" "Where?" I shouted back and ran down the road toward him.

At last I had the card in my hand and could barely read it for the tears of relief that filled my eyes. It was 255

mailed from this side but the message was, "Arrived safely. Love, Art." That was all, but it was enough-for now. 

October 20.-Planted the first two bowls of white narcissus this morning and Sally and I went to see Mrs. 

Miniver tonight. It takes a long time for pictures to wander out this way, but it will take a still longer time for this one to go from my mind. It is so human and fine with courage and family loyalties. That line, "There'll always be roses," is one to remember when the headlines in the papers make you feel nothing will ever be the same. Looking at the children asleep tonight gave me much the same sense of assurance and stability. There'll always be children and may God help us to guard their happiness and well-being. 

October 23.-George came by this morning the proudest six men this side of the equator! He has been put on a night shift with ten per cent more pay and, on the lapel of his coat, for all the world to see-the Army-Navy E. It has just been awarded to his plant and if George had been given the Congressional Medal his chest could not have stuck out any farther or his eyes been shining with any greater joy of accomplishment. 

I was so happy for him and went flying into the house to find some little gift to mark the occasion. It happened to be one of Art's new ties that Jim had bought him last Christmas. Art won't mind-in fact I know he'll be glad to have even a small share in George's triumph for he'll remember all the many kindnesses that George did for him when he was a little fellow in need of just such a warm and loyal friend when he got himself into nu- 256

merous jams. George held the tie up to his collar and his face was beaming. "I sure will be most gratified to be adornin' the tie of my young flyin' officer-he is one fine gen'leman.” I must remember to write that to Art. It would please him and all the little things of home are what he likes in his letters. 

October 25.-My turn to stay in bed this Sunday morning with Sally looking after the young and bringing me up a tray. Every other Sunday each one of us is a lady-"It's a poor house that can't afford one lady!” comments Claudine. Read the paper in a nice leisurely fashion and certainly found contrast between the news and the advertising. A statement that there is a temporary lull in the grim battle for the Solomon Islands and the statement by Rear Admiral McCain that the soldiers and marines on Guadalcanal are "Resting in the mud, fighting off the bugs, being shelled at night and bombed by day-yet they are not beaten nor are they downhearted." Thrilling, simple words that should make us all look in our inner selves to see whether we measure up to their courage and fortitude.

But the advertisements, addressed to women, made us out a pretty paltry lot-only I don't believe the vast majority of everyday women are. "The soft and winsome frock with the spirit lifting buttons that will keep your morale on high," read one. "You owe it to yourself, in these tense days of strain and war duties, to come home to a bedroom of charm and serenity that will refresh your spirit. Crisp, fragile organdy with the breath of spring in its enchanting flower design. A room that enhances your femininity." Guess mine will 257

have to go unenhanced at about a hundred plus for the outfit. These ads make me furious and make me laugh 

I can never decide which emotion is on top for more than five minutes. Guess laughter is best, for heaven forbid that I should ever grow one of those grim faces that seem to go with women who espouse causes. Jim would just say, "Relax, Doody!" and my earnest endeavor would be over. Just the same I think advertising like that is horrible taste and stupid in the bargain-and now I feel better. 

October 26.-Met Mrs. Newton downtown today and was she in a dither! Had I seen the morning paper? I hadn't. What was I going to do about it? I wouldn't know-not having the vaguest idea what she was talking about. Then the blow fell- Coffee is to be rationed to one cup a day for all persons over fifteen, or rather who were fifteen or over when registering for sugar rationing on May fourth and fifth last-which certainly includes me and Sally-and how we do love coffee! Early and late, winter, summer, spring and fall we count on it to cure whatever ails us. If we're tired it rests us, if we're blue it cheers us up. Think I'll save my one cup a day for my hour of greatest need like the magic fruit in the fairy tale of The Three Lemons. I think that one was in the Yellow Fairy Book that I doted on as a child.

I had the whole set of Red, Blue, Green and the rest of them. Then Art and Peg sort of inherited them and I enjoyed the stories just as much as ever. "They're silly but nice-like you," Art would say with a twinkle in his eyes that found more magic and mystery in actual, everyday things like planes and cars. 

But Peg would get a far-away dreamy look and sigh 258

with complete satisfaction. "I'd like to be a princesses and marry a prince and live happily ever after-I'd like to do that." Well, she married her prince but the happily ever after is something we can only hope and pray for. If one cup of coffee a day will help bring it sooner it's a very small thing to give up. I'm honestly glad that life isn't going on as usual. The quicker we are all made as uncomfortable and out of the complacent rut as possible the sooner we'll dig into this war and get it over with. Too bad we can't all have the experience of one day in the jungles or the desert with our men on the far-flung fronts. I imagine it would put an end to all civilian complaining about nothing-and everything. 

October 28.-Jim sent me the latest London news about the Gremlins and it is delicious reading. I wonder what young aviator first thought them up and started the long string of stories about their habits and habitat?

It seems they like to frequent four-motored planes best --because more things can go wrong with them. They make their clothes from pocket fuzz and live off the stuffing out of the empty olives that are served in Martini cocktails. They live in little houses made out of paper clips, which explains why paper clips, never known to wear out, must constantly be replenished. 

There are all sorts of suggested methods of getting rid of them but, the write-up goes on, "The trouble is that by the time you are aware that Gremlins are at work the damage is usually done."

Sally and I have a notion that Whisbick is a Gremlin -or a near relative. He has now taken to scooping the whipped cream from refrigerator desserts in a swirling, but irregular design, as though made by very young but 259

distinctly human fingers. He has written on the wall right beside Cindy's bed and salted peanuts mysteriously vanish whenever he is around. "I telled her and I telled her," explains Cindy with a sorrowful expression, "but him still dos those bad things-doesn't her?" "Her does," agrees Sally, "and if him doesn't stopshe is going to spank he." At which nonsense Cindy smiles joyfully and then says, "Don't let's better not talk about that—just turn your neck around and look out the window." 

October 31.-Halloween-and a stranger and more thrilling one I have never spent. The town so quiet, the lights so dim and the only sound the noise of the passing of special police patrol cars to see that no bonfires are lighted and no deviltry done. Inside our house, with shades drawn, there was a bustle of work and the happiness of shared laughter and a common activity.

Aline came up this morning, just as I was getting out the baking pans to make cookies and Christmas fixings to send to Art. The boxes have to be mailed by November first and I had planned just a little bit of everything he likes most that could possibly stand the long trip. But

I stopped everything when I saw Aline's face and the yellow telegram in her hand. She was laughing and crying-all in the same breath it seemed. Her Bob is alive!
