In a military hospital, somewhere in the Pacific area, he is recovering from hunger and exposure, but his condition is good and a thirty-day furlough in the states will be granted as soon as he can travel. My arms went around her and hers went around me, and we stood there too happy and overcome and excited to say one 260

word, but our day at the beach came back so vividly and now I understand.

Tonight the five of us womenfolks-Aline, her sister Nancy, Sally and Laurie and I-joined forces and filled five boxes to be sent off in the morning. Bruce is still in this country, being given special training at first one hospital and then another, but any day now he'll be gone, too. Laurie made some filled chocolate bars with the Christmas jam in the center, Aline made cocoanut molasses cookies, I baked a huge pan of Brownies with hickory nuts and thick fudge frosting, Sally made stuffed dates, and Nancy supplied us all with candied orange and grapefruit peel that was as glistening as the first snow fall. If one tenth of the love in those boxes arrives at the end of the trip it will still be overflowing. 

A wonderful Halloween! 261

November

WHISBICK GOES TO MARKET-MY VOTING IS IM- PROVING ARE THEY THINKING NOW?-HERE COMES MRS. ROOSEVELT-HAPPY WEDDING DAY- ST. GEORGE AND THE DRAGON-DO NOT OPEN UN- TIL TRENTON-THANKSGIVING-A STAR GLIMMERS. 

November 2.-Marketing used to be one of those routine jobs that you did three or four times a week and then promptly forgot about until the next trip. You saw most of your friends and caught up with the latest news. 

You bought what you had planned to buy and a few extras that caught your eye as you trundled the doubledeck cart around. But marketing in wartime, accompanied by Chunky and Cindy-plus Whisbick-is something else again. It's an adventure-and then some. I wish the authors of those numerous articles on Teach the Little Ones Self-Reliance had been along. They would have gathered material for several books. 

While I stood, ticket in hand, waiting for my favorite butcher-a grocer is a fairly impersonal matter but a butcher you get attached to and think no one else understands your finer nature and its need for a certain delicate slant of the knife in cutting your order. Any- 

I'd wait an hour for Gus. He has such a sympaway, thetic way of saying, "I'm awfully sorry-but not an ounce of beef in the place-and we have no ham and no bacon and no fresh pork. How about a good chunk of neck of veal for stew?" 

I started Cindy and Chunky off with an empty cart 262

and the happy suggestion that they might get six cans of milk for Babs and they could each pick out one box of cookies-one small box. The woman next to me remarked, "How very sweet! It's lovely to see little children trained to be helpful. So important for them to learn to use their own judgment." 

I'm glad she wasn't there when the young voyagers returned! By that time they had a cart a piece and both were loaded to the top decks. They had collected the largest containers of every product the shelves displayed that were within their reach. A five-pound can of baking powder adorned the Captain's Bridge on Cindy's cart and a two-pound can of black pepper was Chunky's greatest pride. The store must have a boarding house section that I never discovered-and they did. Bottles of ginger ale snuggled cozily against those holding ammonia and laundry bleach. All the signs of Share With 

Your Neighbor-Only One to a Customer had made no impression. They were out for bigger and better. They had even unearthed a carton of coffee carefully tucked away in some dark corner awaiting the All Clear signal or something.

It took one half hour to unload and replace and another half hour to soothe their wounded feelings. "Whisbick likes all those nice things to eat-and you hurt his peelings-and he's gone to her grandmother's.

Her is a berry nice lady-him is." I had a definite impression that even an ice cream cone and driving the long way home past the duck pond still left me grades removed from the virtues of Whisbick's grandmother whose kitchen floor is nicer than ours because it's cleaner. But the atmosphere did clear a little when I 263

showed the dejected youngsters a pair of bright and sassy bluejays in the old locust tree. Wonder if they're here for the winter or just a temporary stopover on account of transportation difficulties? 

November 3.-Election day started off raw and rainy -which was good for the Democrats. But it ended up clear and sunny-which gave the Republicans a break. 

Not that anyone, except the politicians, seemed to have much interest one way or another. The editorials in the papers kept hammering away at the importance of this war time election and the local civic groups phoned and called to try to get out the vote. I knew I should go and I kept thinking up excuses why I really didn't need to try another round with those intelligence tests they call voting machines-at least they always give me a terrible inferiority complex because I do something wrong every time and come out completely baffled and wondering whether I voted as I intended. The only way

can judge is that my favorite candidate is always snowed completely under. 

Sally went down early in the afternoon to vote and came back looking none the worse so I decided I'd better take a chance and sleep with an easier conscience. 

November 4.-Maybe skill does come with practiceeven with a voting machine-for my Candidate was elected with a huge majority! 

November 5.-Our oil registration form, which I had mailed so carefully and according to instructions by my lord and master to the local Ration Board, came back by mail today with a letter saying it should be taken to the school where we registered for sugar. The paper said "Hours four to nine daily-November 2, 3, 4 and 264

5." So, at four-fifteen I presented myself. The doors were locked and the place deserted. Seems the hours were nine to four. Follow your daily papers closely for all needed information. 

November 6.-Claudine and I rolled up our sleeves this morning and gave the living and dining room a complete re-decorating job-by the soap and water method. I washed curtains, scarves, chair back tidies, as 

Jim calls them, the glass and ornaments and Claudine devoted herself to floors, walls and woodwork. The salvage boxes came in for several contributions that I found cluttering up drawers. The two rooms look and smell shining fresh, and Sally and I sat in front of the fire tonight just sniffing and admiring. There is such a lot of satisfaction in a job like this-something so concrete and real. It's an awful temptation, when there are no menfolks around, to let things slide and think you'll fix them up again as soon as you hear they are coming home for a day or so. But it's a good feeling to know you're ready and the house is clean and in order and waiting for that phone call or letter. We've made up our minds, too, that we aren't going to get into the easy habit of slopping around in just any old thing and looking like the wrath of heaven. We don't need any antique gold bracelets "with the amusing and morale lifting ornament of a cute simulated frog-price five hundred and seventy-five dollars,” as one ad put it, to keep us going, but looking halfway decent does make you think more of yourself and do your job better. 

November 7.-I was watching Sally give Babs her daily quota of cereal and squashed peas this morning and thinking how nice it is that babies go on being 265

babies when so many other things are changing from day to day. Babs lets you poke in this new and strange food until her mouth is completely full and you're feeling very complacent about the process. Then, with one easy motion, she spits it out to the four points of the compass. Peg had the same method but Art used the law of gravity and simply opened his mouth so it would all run out. Cindy and Chunky are fascinated and think it would be fun if they tried one of the systems. Sally and I don't. 

November 9.-I ache in every muscle I own and my old bones are ready to snap at the slightest word. This morning, bright and early, I went out to do a little final clean up on the vegetable garden after reading an article in the Sunday paper that said, "The autumn is the ideal time to spade a plot for next season. All old growth should be removed and weeds turned under. Ground should be left rough and not harrowed or raked." It spoke lightly of destroying all infected plants and watching for larvae that do not go deep for their winter sleep. Not being familiar with the bedtime habits of the little dears I decided to take no chances and spaded up halfway to China. I'd like to take a sizzling hot bath and crawl deep into bed until the daffodils bloom again. 

Never realized the human body had so many muscles that could ache. Physiology was not my best subject but 

I think Cindy and Chunky are going to shine in it.

Mealtime is getting to be a question and answer program on anatomy, physiology, and hygiene. With the shortage of ordinary meat we've been going in for all sorts of oddments. So tonight we had calves brains, delicately 266

disguised with a crusty brown coat of bread crumbs and served with spicy tomato sauce. 

Cindy eyed her plate suspiciously, "What is those meats?" "Calves brains, dear," said Sally, "and they're very good-Mummy loves them!"

Chunky was eating steadily but stopped, with his fork in mid-air. "Are these the brains they think with?” “I suppose so,” I answered, feeling a little like a cannibal with a toothsome member of an enemy tribe. "Oh!" said Chunky. "Are they thinking now?" 

Cindy gave him a look of scornful amusement. "These meats are died and dead! Gus deaded them. 

That's why they don't talk to me.”

I tremble to think of the research if we serve kidneys and liver and tongue. I remember a sweet old lady on the train who said, "How fascinating it is to watch their little minds develop." Wish I could invite her to dinner -when we serve brains! 

November 11.-Armistice Day and Sally and I sat reading the paper, with its encouraging news from the battle in Egypt, and listening to the speeches and ceremonies being held in so many places. There is renewed hope but little exultation or over-confidence. I believe all of us have, at last, realized that there is a long, hard road ahead through darkness and difficulty and each one, in his own way, must help. The whole pattern of our lives is changing and we have gradually learned to accept it from day to day and hour to hour until it is sometimes strange to try to think back to nine short months ago. Even so small a thing as watching the trains go over the trestle at night-the shades tightly drawn on 267

every window and only a dim bar of light showing between the coaches. The signs in the cars read-"U. S. 

Army requires shades drawn sunset to sunrise." And I have yet to see a single shade raised or to hear one passenger complaining about it. It is only some of our nervous congressmen who fear for the breakdown of the morale of the people if more restrictions are imposed. The people themselves will take anything necessary. When you've given up your menfolks for the duration, and perhaps forever, nothing else seems of very great importance except doing your job to the best of your ability and clinging to an undying faith that kindness and decency and fairness to one another will and can and must be the law of life to survive. Otherwise, life itself is of no value.

November 13.-Letter from Peg and one from Art so this is anything but Friday the thirteenth as far as my luck is concerned. Art, as usual, is brief and to the point but I have read his letter so many times that I know it by heart. 

DEAR DAD AND MOM:

I sure began to feel at home over here when who should turn up at our quarters but Mrs. Roosevelt! She's as American as hot dogs and Cracker Jack and the fellows think she's right on the beam. The R.A.F. men are O.K. and one of them sure made me think of Mom coasting down our hill when he told about a crash landing and called it 'a bellyflop'-made me sort of homesick. I'm not really, but coming back will be O.K. by me when we finish up the job. Only now I know, what 

I always kind of thought, that I belong in a plane and nowhere else. You sure feel on your own when you're playing tag with the atmosphere. Give my best to the 268

young and tell Cindy I'll wait for her if she'll hurry up a little-she's sure the six cutest kids in the U.S.A.including the British Isles-what I've seen of them. Hope 

Peg is making out with that sailor of hers-anyway, the 

Navy got us here and I'm grateful for that. Tell you all about it someday when I can. Don't worry. Love,

ART 

Peg's letter was longer and full of news about Joan and the apartment and their two jobs and no husbands. 

Phil had three days' leave and that was wonderful then, bing, he vanished into thin air without a word.

He said it would be that way and I thought I could take it in my stride and go on making ham sandwiches and coffee for the swing shift boys at the plant. But, when 

I realized he was actually gone I didn't know whether I was making cheese on rye or ham on white. Maybe it has become second nature to me by this time because no kicks came in from my customers. The only thing that bothers me about the mountains of food that pile in every day is the smell of mayonnaise in the morning -that gets me. I'm supposed to only plan and supervise but, when things get hot and heavy, I pitch in and really go to work. But before ten A.M. I concentrate on something which does not require smears of mayonnaise.

Even then I can smell the stuff and my head gets woozy -me with the digestion of an ostrich. 

I wish Bruce was around so I could ask him if this is one of those occupational hazards you read about and whether Peg will get over it in time. I hate to think of her so far away where I can't fuss over her. There is a lot of mother hen in my nature. 

November 14.-It is just too bad that I can't patent my wishing ability and put it to general use. So many 269

times when I am thinking about someone in particular, and wishing I could see them, they turn up. Jim says have a genius for remembering the things which go right and completely forgetting the ones which go wrong. Guess he's right but I always figure I have a fifty-fifty chance and I might as well hope for the best as the worst. But this time my wishing for Bruce to be around, so that I could talk to him about Peg, worked like a charm-but I didn't get a chance to talk about Peg for I was far too excited to get my thoughts down to earth. Excited and happy! I am so utterly miserable, when my friends and family are troubled, that I suppose the law of compensation makes me equally blissful when their worlds are going as they should. 

Right after what Sally and I humorously call "their naps" the youngsters decided it would be fine to get all dressed up in their "warmerish" clothes and go out to play. It turned really cold last night and there was thick frost this morning so that the grass was crispy under foot when I dashed out to get the paper-the boy who brings it plays sort of a game with himself and with me and never throws it in the same place twice. I happened to see him one day last week and mildly suggested that the brick stoop by the front door would be a good idea. 

I even played on his pride by adding, "I'm sure you could hit it every time-you're so good at throwing." I thought it was a pretty clever approach myself-but this lad was too smart to be taken in by my amateur psychology. His face, freckles and all, took on a sober expression. "In ordinary times-yes. But these are war times and 270

customers can't expect the usual service-tires and gas you know." "I see." But this does not explain why it saves tires and gas to throw the paper halfway down the side slope or land it neatly behind the fire thorn bush which is the only shrub we own adorned with spiky thorns. Maybe it's the Gremlins-or Whisbick. 

Whisbick still comes and goes and has come back from his grandmother's in the apple tree, so Cindy announced that he was going to play with them and they needed two more, this many, graham crackers because 

Whisbick has a cold and he needs pineapple juice for her, too.

Sally and I settled down to some mending and the soothing, happy music of the Strauss waltz records on the radio. I can always mend better when those are playing and I can't mend all the time when I have to stop and change records-which also suits me. In one of the intervals I glanced out of the window and it was snowing! The first of the season and I always get a thrill out of it. Cindy and Chunky were shouting to us through the window-"It's snowing! It's snowing!" as though they didn't want us to miss the fun. Then, around the bend at the corner, we saw two people laughing and pushing against the wind. We looked again. "It's Laurie!" said Sally. "And Bruce! What in the world?"

We ran to the front door and stood there with the fine whirling snow blowing in our faces. They were waving and we were waving. Through my mind was also whirling the thought of "What will we have for 271

supper?" Scrambled eggs and toast and hot cocoa was all I'd planned for Sally and me. In spite of our good resolutions we do sort of go in for the simple life on meals. But I needn't have worried for, as they came in the front door, Bruce handed me a package. "A pound of coffee, a pound of bacon, a can of tuna fish-and our best wishes for our wedding day!" 

I still don't know how I got my wits together nor my feet to moving because I was so all of a dither. But 

I do know that I almost squeezed the breath out of 

Laurie-and the same for Bruce.

A fire in the fireplace, the Strauss waltzes all over again, the percolator brimming with coffee and scenting the air with the most heavenly smell this side of heaven. A can of mushroom soup helped make a perfect sauce for the tuna and I piled it on toast, topped it with a thick layer of grated cheese and grilled bacon over the whole thing. Brown bubbles on the cheese, brown crispness on the bacon-we haven't been able to buy any for weeks-and our amber glass bowl full of carrot strips and ice cold celery. What a wedding feast-and what happiness to see Bruce and Laurie together forever and always! Only five days' leave now for Bruce, but neither time nor distance can ever separate those two again—I'm sure of that. 

November 15.-Too much happening, and too fast, to keep track of time or events. War has its tragedies and its comedies. Perhaps tragedy is too strong a word to use for the passing and signing of the new draft bill for the eighteen- and nineteen-year-olds, but it is going to bring the war very close to so many families who have hoped and prayed for a little more time for their 272

boys to grow up-or for the war to end before they were needed. All any of us can do now is to accept what comes and do the best we can "To keep the faith with those who went before."-That Ballad for Americans has a lot of inspiration and uplift in it.

The comedy in the paper this morning was furnished by none other than our own picturesque Mayor La 

Guardia-the Little Flower-playing the part of St. 

George with the Macy Thanksgiving dragon donated for rubber salvage. No parade this year and all the beasts, birds and fish and huge, grotesque figures joined the scrap pile. At the ceremonies the Mayor slew the dragon and kept asking, like an eager little boy, "Shall 

I do it now?" Someone should have recited the Jabberwocky: 

One, two! One, two! And through and through 

The vorpal blade went snicker-snack! 

He left it dead, and with its head 

He went galumphing back. 

November 17.-It just came to me this morning that 

Thanksgiving and Christmas are both very near day after tomorrow and I'd better get busy. Don't know what either day will be this year, because I know Jim can't get home for Thanksgiving and may just about make it for Christmas-if that. The first time we haven't all been together for the big holidays, and Sally and I will have to plan something pretty special-might invite some of the boys from the Air Field. When they have been over before the little things of candles on the table, the bright linen doilies and napkins and the gaiety of peasant pottery made a great hit. Men are funny! They 273

pretend they don't care for all the trimmings and the food is the main thing, but they mighty soon get tired of the best of food when it is dished up in quantity and without benefit of parsley. Laurie always says she likes food that is cooked with affection. If Bruce doesn't put on five pounds a month, when she has the chance to feed him, I'll miss my guess. Wish I could have been tucked somewhere in a back pew in that little church while they were being married, but I'm satisfied and happy all through me to know that Laurie can wait for him as his wife. That, alone, would mean a great deal to her all the rest of her life, but she has some deep-laid plan buzzing around in her head now so as to keep busy and useful while he's away. Perhaps she'll tell me about it after he's gone to "Somewhere in the combat area." 

How vast and impersonal that sounds-and is. 

November 19.-Bruce left this morning and stopped in, for a minute, to say good-by and ask us to keep an eye on Laurie. He has such a kind, intelligent face with a sense of humor that makes his mouth go up in a little quirk at one corner and brings crinkles around his eyes -character lines, a beauty parlor expert would call them and go to work to remove them. But, to all of us who are so fond of him, they spell the endless nights and days that he has been such comfort and strength and assurance to so many families like ours. I have wished so often, and Jim has wished with me, that we had the money to back his hospital for children. Perhaps wishes do come true, if they aren't purely selfish ones. We'll keep right on wishing. 

November 20.-Laurie didn't come over yesterday after she saw Bruce off in town and I thought of her 274

a good deal in that exquisite, but empty, small house of hers. It will seem more empty than ever now. Love and happiness always come at a high price in loneliness and grief-but they're worth every bit of it. It was in Flight to Arras that I read, "To live is to be slowly born. It would be a bit too easy if we could go about borrowing ready-made souls."

I kept thinking about Laurie this morning while I started some of the fixings for Thanksgiving-the spiced, canned peaches and the cranberry relish with orange and honey. Then I mixed up a batch of the Christmas cookies that have to ripen in the dough for a couple of days before they are rolled out and stand overnight, ready for baking. They are so crisp and spicy, and 

Christmas Eve wouldn't be complete without them and the hot mulled cider we always have just before we go to bed on Christmas night. I started for the phone to call Laurie to remind her to come on Thanksgiving bright and early so she can fix the creamed onions-hers are super-special. She says it's because she always shuts her eyes when she measures the butter for the sauce. 

Rumors are about, via the grapevine, that butter is going to be scarce-but not at this house-on this Thanksgiving. Too thankful to have the old day and date back again and read the war news which really begins to sound hopeful and encouraging that, in our time-Jim's and mine-victory and peace will come again.

The doorbell rang-and there stood Laurie. She and 

Sally and I sat down for a cup of tea while Cindy and Chunky had theirs on a small table. Babs, on the davenport, crowed and blew bubbles and waved her hands 275

in sociability—and Hosky curled up on his favorite corner of the rug and looked at us each in turn as though to say, "Well, this is pretty nice-and I'm glad to be here." The perfect setting for Laurie's big news. She undid a long roll she'd brought under one arm, and spread sheets of blueprints all over the floor. We didn't know what in the world it was all about, but it was exciting enough to see her curled up on the backs of her heels and looking so free and eager and full of enthusiasm. "I gave Bruce a duplicate set, just before he boarded the train, and made him promise not to look at them until Trenton. It's the plans for his hospital! We've worked out every inch of them together-only he thought we were just talking. Everything he's ever wanted is here-and everything I've ever dreamed about what I'd like to do for children. Now we'll do it together. I've been accepted at the hospital to enter nurse's training December first so I'll be ready to help him that way, too. The Supervisor encouraged me to do it because she said I had the makings of a really good nurse and they need every one they can get."

Sally and I sat there too flabbergasted to talk but we finally came to and such a jabberfest you never heard.

We both think it's wonderful. And we both wish we could see Bruce when he opens the package at Trenton!

Laurie is renting her house for the duration and will use this one as headquarters whenever she has time offwhich I hope is often. If anything more exciting happens my breath will simply go and forget to come back. 

November 21.-It did happen-and I went right on breathing but my heart is still jumping around like 276

Cindy and Chunky when they play they are a circus. 

Night letter from Peg It wasn't the mayonnaise. It was Phil Junior and I'm coming home. If you can't take me in I'll board out but much prefer you and home cooking. Joan is o.k. running a day nursery but she hasn't the mother touch that I sort of crave. Arrive in time for the turkey and better put on a couple of extra plates. The trains are full of lonesome men in uniform and I might collect one or two. Love,

PEG 

Never a dull moment around this house. Babs had better hurry up and outgrow the family cradle and also pass on a few clothes. War or no war Phil Junior is going to arrive with a twenty-one-gun salute! I must start a blue sweater tonight and write to Jim that he is about to become a grandfather. He'll be so proud there'll be no living with him and I'll be unbearable to anyone who will stop long enough to listen to my boasting. Glad Peg wants the mother touch because that is the only thing I'm really good at and how I do love putting it to use. Sally just put on a record of How

About a Cheer for the Na-vy! by way of celebration. 

November 22.-Gas ration to be cut to three gallons a week-coffee sales to be stopped for one week, starting next Sunday, and then rationed to one pound every five weeks-further cut in oil for heating is likely on Eastern seaboard-heavy cream to be taken off the market soon and butter rationed-meat rationing to start early in new year-few vegetables to be shipped and fewer still to be canned-no raisins or chocolate or olive oil or baconless of everything including trains and buses and cars 277

and telephones. But more of neighborliness and family loyalties and appreciation for the beauty and freedom and worth of the country we have so long taken for granted. We're all in it together and we'll all come out of it with colors flying-no matter how hard and long the way. This I do know with my heart and mind and faith. 

November 25.-Brought the turkey home today and

Cindy and Chunky are fascinated with the size of the bird. They want to know the name of each and every part, inside and out, and are curious as to the whereabouts of its head. "Whisbick took it for her to play with him," suggested Cindy-which is good enough.

Mashed yellow turnip with a little potato to make it smooth and delicate in flavor, green beans with the wild extravagance of a few chopped mushrooms browned in butter with a dash of grated onion, sweet potatoes simmered slowly with honey and grated orange rind—and butter, mashed potatoes-as fluffy and light as a snowdrift, creamed onions à la Laurie-and one of my huge and superlative pumpkin pies for dessert-that's a dinner! Next week-well, there are always baked beans. 

November 27.-Peg and her military escort-only three this trip-arrived just in time to pour the cider and light the candles. Seems the men on the train were all headed for home and mother but these three got marooned in the Station Canteen. Nice boys-and how they could eat! It made me purr with contentment to watch them. We tucked them away overnight and had a glorious breakfast with the dramatic news of the sinking of the French fleet coming over the radio-they said 278

many a French captain stood on his bridge until his ship sank. 

November 29.-Churchill broadcast to the world tonight-on the eve of his sixty-eighth birthday. A speech with more encouragement than he has been able to give since the war began but, as always in his talks, a warning against overconfidence and dreams of an easy way. Sally and Peg and I sat listening and thrilling to the inspiration and power of his voice. I believe it is because he has a natural sense of appeal to great numbers of people so that each one feels an individual, and important-and because he is sincere in what he says. Real honesty is always impressive. 

November 30.-Christmas is coming-and maybe Jim!

If he can get home that is all I want in my stocking. The store ads carry the slogan-"Rule for a Wartime Yuledon't delay-buy it today-carry it away." I must get busy. The lights of the world are dimmed but, far off, there is the glimmer of a star and we'll guide by that. 279
